<meta>This page continues Robert Fludd’s critique of Aristotelian natural philosophy, specifically focusing on the origin of winds. Fludd argues that the Aristotelian (Peripatetic) explanation of winds—as mere physical exhalations—contradicts the Holy Scriptures, which describe winds as divine instruments or spirits.</meta>

### ->On Wisdom with Folly<-

They do not blush to assert [these things], and so they imagine many actors in nature besides God alone, the sole actor in the world, without whom there is nothing. Thus, with their Aristotle, they believe that the sun and stars and their influences, as well as the various qualities of the elements, are the simple and only actors in the world. Conversely, the Sacred Scriptures recognize that the one and only God does all things in and through all these instruments. Yet these "Christian philosophers," following the precepts of their pagan <note>original: "Ethnici"</note> master, report and believe that winds are produced from exhalations rising from the earth by the power of the sun and stars; and yet the Sacred Page makes God alone the author and producer of the winds. Whence <margin>Job 27.</margin> it is written: *By the blast of your nostrils the waters were gathered together.* <note>original: "Flatu narium tuarum coaceruatae sunt aquae." This is actually Exodus 15:8, though the margin notes Job.</note> And elsewhere: *By the breath of God frost is given.* <margin>Psal. 103.</margin> <note>original: "Flante Deo concrescit gelu." Likely referring to Job 37:10; Psalm 103 (104) refers to God's breath creating life.</note> And elsewhere: *God makes a weight for the wind.* And elsewhere: *Who makes His angels winds.* <note>Psalm 104:4.</note> Aristotle and his Christian followers say the material of the winds arises from the earth, but the Sacred Scriptures say: *Jehovah brings forth the winds from His storehouses.* <note>Psalm 135:7.</note> And He declares His treasury to be in a high place—the heavens—and not the earth: *Jehovah shall open to you* (says Moses) *his good treasure, the heavens, to give the rain, etc.* These men want the material of the winds to ascend from the earth by the power of the sun, which then, by the force of the cold in the middle region of the air,

tends downward, and thus is moved laterally if it meets resistance from the ascending exhalations. <margin>John 3:8.</margin> But we are taught by Saint John that *the spirit <note>The Latin "spiritus" can mean both "wind" and "spirit." Fludd plays on this dual meaning throughout the argument.</note> blows where it wills, but you do not know where it comes from or where it goes.* If, therefore, it blows where it wills, then it is not forced nor moved by the violent impulse of rising exhalations, as the <term>Peripatetics</term> <note>Followers of Aristotle’s philosophy, named for his habit of walking while teaching.</note> claim. If the place from which it comes is unknown, then it can by no means be held for certain that it has its origin from the earth according to their opinion. If it blows where it wills, then its motion is not merely lateral or forced. Either, therefore, the pagan Aristotle saw less into the mysteries of God and nature than the Evangelist, who was a true and sincere Christian, or Saint John is plainly a liar in his doctrine—which to assert for the love of Aristotle would be the highest impiety in Christians.

But to that doubt—namely, whether the path of the wind’s material is first from the earth toward heaven and then lateral—a man far more learned and wiser in the mysteries of nature than Aristotle directly responds in these words: *He knows not the way of the wind; man knows not the work of God himself, by what way He does all these things, etc.* <note>Paraphrase of Ecclesiastes 11:5.</note> But Aristotle was a man—indeed, a pagan—to whom, consequently, it was given much less to judge concerning such abstruse matters.

Hence also Jehovah, mocking such men, says: *Do you know where the way is by which the light <note>original: "exhalatio" — Fludd uses the word for exhalation here, though Job 38:24 usually refers to light or wind.</note> is parted, which scatters the east wind upon the earth?* Here he argues the impossibility of this thing for a man to whom Divine Wisdom (to whom alone it belongs to teach all these things) has not been granted by the Creator.

And yet Aristotle seems to respond to Him impudently, saying: "I know." For the path of this kind of wind-making exhalation is from the earth upward, where it is scattered and driven laterally over the earth, partly through <term>antiperistasis</term> <note>A philosophical concept where one quality is intensified by being surrounded by its opposite (e.g., heat intensifying when surrounded by cold).</note> by the cold of the middle region of the air driving it downward, and partly by other exhalations striving upward and meeting those on the descent, etc.

Furthermore, he even makes the stars the active cause in the summoning of such an exhalation from the earth, and the accidental quality of cold the cause driving it downward. From this, he concludes that the spirit by no means "blows where it wills," but that winds are merely produced accidentally, destitute of all internal and central or natural acting form—notwithstanding the Sacred Scriptures, which made the angels winds and, by a reversible logic, the winds angels, or spirits animated by divine light.

But look here (my Peripatetic), what was that logic of struggling exhalations and that order of gushing from the earth when the Prophet, by divine admonition, said to the spirit: <margin>Ezek. 37. 9.</margin> *Come from the four winds, O Spirit, and breathe upon these slain, that they may live?* <note>Ezekiel 37:9.</note> Was this Spirit from a Peripatetic exhalation? It seems to me that not only Aristotle, but also his Christian followers, must fall silent and be utterly deprived of the tongues with which they might respond. The Bridegroom says: *Awake, O North Wind, and come...* <note>Song of Solomon 4:16.</note>

<summary>Robert Fludd argues that the Aristotelian theory of winds—treating them as mere physical vapors or "exhalations"—is a pagan error that contradicts the Bible. He asserts that winds are divine spirits or instruments of God, moving by His will rather than by accidental mechanical forces like heat and cold.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Robert Fludd’s argument from page 32, moving from the origin of winds to the origin of lightning and thunder. Fludd continues to contrast the "pagan" Aristotelian view—which sees these as natural, material phenomena—with his own "Mosaic" philosophy, which views them as direct, miraculous actions of God.</meta>

### ->On the Contest, Book I.<-

“...and come, O South Wind, and blow through my garden.” <note>original: "veni Auster & perfla hortum meum." A quote from Song of Solomon 4:16.</note> How absurd this invocation of the divine spouse would be if the matter and path of these winds were such as Aristotle ascribes to them.

But we shall proceed to his other absurdities and vanities, or empty assertions, and consequently his false doctrine which contradicts the Sacred Scriptures. Aristotle teaches that the general material cause of lightnings is a hot and dry exhalation raised from the earth into the middle region of the air by the power of the stars; there, he asserts, partly by the forceful collision of clouds among themselves and partly through **antiperistasis** <term>antiperistasis: a philosophical concept where a quality is intensified by being surrounded by its opposite, such as heat becoming more intense when compressed by cold</term>—namely, because of the intense cold—that enclosed material is ignited, and because of its earthy and compact disposition, it tends downward. Furthermore, he calls the noise produced by this kind of violence "thunder." But the Holy Scriptures recognize that the material of lightning has arisen and been derived from the treasury of God.

<margin>Ecclesiasticus 43</margin>
They say elsewhere that Jehovah by His wisdom makes a way for the lightning of the thunders. And <margin>Job 28</margin> elsewhere it is said that Jehovah makes lightnings with the rain. And elsewhere: Lightning proceeding and flying from the mouth of Jehovah. And elsewhere: Lightnings proceed from the throne. Hence also He is <margin>Psalm 17</margin> said to flash from above with His own light, and the foundations of the sea are covered. And elsewhere: From His mouth, as if from passing torches, a flame would come forth from His mouth. And the Psalmist says: Your lightnings lit up the world. And Job: He extends the clouds like Your tent, <margin>Psalm 18</margin> and He flashes with His light from above. Smoke went up in His anger and fire from His face glowed; He made darkness His hiding place round about Him, etc. Because of the brightness in His presence <margin>Job 36</margin> the clouds passed, the Lord thundered from heaven, and the Most High uttered His voice, He multiplied lightnings, etc. <margin>Psalm 17</margin> That thunder is also the voice of Jehovah, do we not find confirmed everywhere throughout the thread of <margin>Psalm 29</margin> Holy Scripture? For in Psalms 18, 29, 77, 104, and Job 37, Jeremiah 10 and 51, it is said that when Jehovah gives His voice, the waters are multiplied in heaven. And elsewhere, copious mention is made of the terrible and peaceful voice of God.

Therefore, let an examination be made by good Christians as to whether that opinion of Aristotle agrees or not with the sacred text. His opinion makes the material of lightning and thunders to be exhalations from the earth, mediated by vapors raised from water and earth, and thickened into the form of clouds in the middle region of the air. The sacred text asserts that clouds in the sky are extended by Jehovah like a tent and produced from the treasuries—namely, the heavens—and consequently their existence is derived neither from the waters nor from the earth, nor is their action derived from the heat of the stars. Similarly, it asserts that lightnings are light proceeding from the mouth and throne of Jehovah, where they are even called the lights of Jehovah, insofar as they are miraculously fashioned and multiplied by Him.

But Aristotle (acting like an atheist in this) ascribes such acts, which are owed to Jehovah alone, to the stars and the cold of the middle region of the air. And consequently, these "Aristotelian Christians" have thought that this whole miraculous work happens by chance and is procreated solely by the imbalance <note>original: "intemperie"</note> of created nature. Wherefore they not only harm themselves, but, submerged in ethnic ignorance of this kind, they are contrary and adverse to GOD and His son Jesus Christ. Among these, we can especially number Marin Mersenne <note>Marin Mersenne (1588–1648) was a French mathematician and philosopher who defended mechanical philosophy and criticized Fludd's occult views.</note>, who, notwithstanding these trifles of Aristotle so adverse to the truth, does not hesitate to trumpet and preach to the world that Aristotle is a true pupil of philosophy, a leader to blessedness and a good end, the prince of philosophers, the illustrator of the mind, etc. But if it were truly so, why should we not reject the sacred page so that we might adhere to Aristotle alone, since it is certain and well-known among mortals that there is but one single truth? Yet we find the sense of the Holy Scriptures most opposite to Aristotle. What then remains now, except that we receive and embrace either one or the other? Mersenne therefore says that Aristotle teaches men the way to blessedness. But we say this is impossible, because his doctrine is false; whence it follows that blessedness is by no means to be drawn from it. It is false (I say) because it is contrary to the truth, and the source of truth is the word of GOD, the written sacred word of GOD; therefore if the doc-

<meta>This page continues Robert Fludd’s critique of Aristotelian natural philosophy, contrasting it with the "Mosaic" or biblical worldview. On the previous page, Fludd discussed the origins of lightning and winds; here, he enlists the testimony of other Christian scholars who, despite their Aristotelian training, found his natural explanations lacking compared to the Holy Scriptures.</meta>

# On Wisdom with Folly

...Aristotelian doctrine is opposed to Sacred Scripture, it is from the Devil and from below, and not from above. Indeed, even among the Christian philosophers themselves who were raised in Aristotelian philosophy, we have several who seem to publicly acknowledge this very thing. 

<margin>Book 9, chapters 6, 20.</margin> 
For the *Philosophical Pearl* <note>original: "Margarita Philosophica," a famous 16th-century encyclopedia by Gregor Reisch.</note> testifies that some philosophers, considering the wonderful operation of lightning, judged it to be not a work of nature, but an immediate effect of the most high God. And among the Christian professors of natural philosophy, Freigius <note>Thomas Freigius (1543–1583), a German scholar.</note>—who was quite well-versed in Aristotelian philosophy—after he had spoken in his own philosophy about the efficient causes of meteors and winds, was immediately led by a good conscience to withdraw from Aristotle’s opinion and broke out into this discourse: 

<margin>Freigius, Book 25, On Natural Philosophy.</margin> 
*Although the whole dispute concerning winds, as with other meteors, is one of wonders for which no firm and sufficient natural causes can be brought forward, it is nevertheless useful to see how far human reason can proceed. Sacred Scripture indeed says that God brings forth the wind from His treasuries, whence we hear their blowing, but we do not know from where they come or where they go.* Thus he spoke. 

And Ioannes Velcurius <note>Johann Velcur, a 16th-century commentator on Aristotle.</note>, a man very much devoted to the Peripatetic <term>Peripatetic: A follower of Aristotle, named for the covered walkways (peripatoi) where Aristotle taught.</term> philosophy, says: *The Holy Scriptures do not plainly agree with the 
<margin>Book 3, chapter 6, in the Commentary on Physics.</margin> 
Peripatetic Physicists concerning the origin of springs and rivers; Ecclesiastes testifies that these flow from the sea through various channels and passages and flow back to their sources, saying: "All rivers enter the sea, yet the sea is not full; unto the place from whence the rivers come, thither they return again, that they may flow again." <note>A reference to Ecclesiastes 1:7.</note> However, Aristotle and the Peripatetic Physicists say that the material of these is likewise vapor resolved into water and liquefied by cold and heat together within the earth.* Thus he spoke.

Similarly, his empty error concerning the eternity of the world—which the Scriptures testify was created by God—and the essence of light—which he himself devises to be a mere **accident** <term>accident: In Aristotelian logic, an "accident" is a property that does not belong to the essence of a thing (like the color of a shirt), as opposed to its "substance."</term>, contrary to the sentence of the divine page, which establishes light as the first creature—as well as many other false opinions, prove that he is most opposed to sacred truth. 

But here (O Marinus) <note>Likely referring to Marin Mersenne, Fludd’s intellectual rival.</note> is your "philosophical God," whom you seem to venerate above all. Oh, the wonderful stupidity and remarkable folly of a Christian man who, while observing that this philosophy attacks the Holy Scriptures, nevertheless does not blush to keep calling it "true philosophy." Indeed, he even proclaims those who act against this spurious philosophy to be "introducers of atheism"—those who desire to preach and publish to the world that Mosaic philosophy, which is truth itself. 

When the matter is considered well and accurately, our "good counselor" here seems to support atheism himself rather than proving it against others with so many futile words. For (as has already been said in the preceding pages), if the sense of Sacred Scripture is infallibly true—indeed, truth itself—what is to be thought of such men who preach that philosophy as true (sounding it as if with a trumpet) and its author as a leader to blessedness, when in reality it is not consistent with the truth, but is as close as possible to Ethnic <note>i.e., Pagan.</note> religion and idolatry? They consider with neither eyes nor an upright judgment the mind of the Prophet Baruch, brought forth in this sense:

<margin>Baruch 3</margin>
> It is not fitting to desert the fountain of all wisdom and to seek art, science, and understanding in foreign regions, as the Agarenes <note>Descendants of Hagar; a term often used for desert dwellers or Arabs.</note> did, and those who were in Teman, who indeed never recognized the way of true wisdom, etc.

By this excellent teaching of the Prophet, we are clearly enough informed how impious and profane is this imitation of the doctrine of the Pagans.

For in truth, we judge it to be a certain species of idolatry to adore—with such devotion and obstinacy—the barely probable opinions of a worshiper of other gods, and his unstable inventions which are least consistent with the truth (while neglecting the spring of true Wisdom and indubitable knowledge). It is as if no true erudition, nor contemplation of natural philosophy, nor the judgment of diseases, nor the science of applying medicines could be acquired except in Pagan spirits—namely, the Greek and Arabian schools.

# On the Contest, Book 1

But so that you might be able to discern these things with even a little more accurate inspection (most desired readers)—namely the vain judgment of Mersenne regarding the origin of the sciences, by which he seems to take away from the privilege of the holy fountain, so as to claim all true science and knowledge only for the Pagans <note>original: "Ethnicis"</note>—I would have you observe what kind of text he introduces for the praise and glory of the Pagans, and for the disgrace of Christians and of the divine Spirit itself, whose role alone it is, by the testimony of Holy Scripture, to teach all things.

->Mersenne’s Text. Column 1475.<-

<margin>Col. 1475.</margin>
> *Let us admire the divine goodness and providence, which suggested to us so many, and such useful, and such most excellent arts even through the sons of the impious Cain; thus we have received almost all arts from the Gentiles: From Socrates, Plato, and Aristotle, Philosophy; from Hippocrates, Galen, and if you will even from Paracelsus <note>Philippus Aureolus Theophrastus Bombastus von Hohenheim (1493–1541), a Swiss physician and alchemist whom Fludd admired, though Mersenne mentions him here with some irony.</note>, Medicine; from Euclid, Geometry; from Ptolemy, Astrology; from Hiero and Archimedes, Mechanics. Who is more skilled in poetry than Homer and Virgil? Who could ever be compared with the eloquence of Demosthenes and Cicero, when nevertheless all those were Pagans and impious?*

The Holy Scriptures teach us that there is no true wisdom and science except that which is given by God. This the Prophet seems to acknowledge in express words: that God is the giver and inventor of true wisdom. And Solomon says in that very same vein, *Wisdom is from the Lord, as if from whose mouth prudence and science go forth.* Wherefore he himself seeks Wisdom and science and understanding from God, and at length received it as he had hoped. And in another place he confesses that Jehovah illuminates the eyes of the simple and the industrious (who meet each other). Hence David, illuminated by this light, breaks forth into this speech: *I have become more intelligent than all my teachers, because your testimonies, O GOD, are my meditation.* And elsewhere: *Teach me goodness and discipline and science because I have believed your commandments.* Similarly he says, *Make me prudent, that I may know your testimonies.* 

However, in this place he does not intend that spurious science which is worldly, of which the Apostle speaks in one place, *The wisdom of this world makes men fools.* And in another: *The wisdom of this world is foolishness with God.* But he speaks of that divine wisdom, of which it is said, *Prudence is the science of the saints.* And this is the reason why the royal Psalmist says, *Blessed is the man whom you shall have instructed, O Lord, and taught from your law.* Concerning that worldly science or human invention of that kind, the Apostle understands it where he warns and exhorts us to beware of vain philosophy; just as he intends that true and real wisdom where he speaks in these words: *No one of the princes of this world knew the mystic wisdom, which nevertheless God revealed to us through his Spirit.* 

And this wisdom was not published to the world in its naked truth, like that common pagan wisdom, but was hidden among the holy and worthy, and not promulgated to the world except in parabolic words, and that by divine command; seeing that God predestined it before the ages for the glory of the elect and Apostles, as St. Paul testifies, who speaks in these express words: *We speak the wisdom of God in a mystery, which is hidden, which God predestined before the ages for our glory.* Therefore, so that the men of this world—who are Israelites in heart and lead a Christian life—might tread that only way and seriously persist in it, they are instructed by the Prophet Baruch that the fountain of such mystic wisdom is by no means to be forsaken so that we might seek sciences in foreign regions (that is, outside the tribe of true Israelites), seeing that from such people there is no true Wisdom. 

But if Christians or "true Israelites" do contrary to the Prophet's command—namely, if they neglect the Holy Bible and recur to the writings of the pagans for the purpose of acquiring science—undoubtedly heavy afflictions will come upon them, which the Prophet threatens in that same chapter. Since this is the case, as the Prophet Jeremiah spoke concerning proud Babylon, so may I speak of Marin Mersenne, and that deservedly, as he is swelling with arrogance: *This wisdom and science of yours has deceived you, and you have said in your heart, "I am, and besides me there is no other,"* etc.

# Concerning Wisdom with Folly

He himself <note>referring to Marin Mersenne</note> (I say) presumes to accuse, judge, and condemn many upright men of atheism, heresy, wicked magic, and ignorance, as if no one were comparable to himself in science and prudence. Yet in truth, we observe that no one is closer to atheism and heresy than he is, insofar as in his own discipline he is most opposed to **GOD**, and consequently acts as a deceptive seducer of the world. For the Sacred Scriptures command that we should by no means desert the fountain of divine Wisdom; yet he urges us toward Diabolical wisdom, boldly asserting that those arts are most useful and excellent for us Christians which are drawn and derived from the ethnic <note>pagan</note> and diabolical dunghill. Indeed, he seems to confess and affirm this in plain and open words, saying in his own text: *Which suggested to us so many, and such useful, and such most excellent arts through the sons of the impious Cain.* As if **GOD** were accustomed to attribute knowledge to his elect by means of the devil, and not by means of that wisdom which is the "splendor of eternal light proceeding from the mouth of the Almighty." Since it has been declared clearly enough from what was said above that **GOD** alone is the giver of true knowledge and wisdom, it is therefore not at all to be believed that **GOD** gave knowledge to the impious and the reprobate before the elect. Did not the Apostle demonstrate to us the most exact difference between that Wisdom of the elect and that of the reprobate in these words:

<margin>
James 3.
1 John 2.
1 Corinthians 3.
</margin>

> *The wisdom (he says) which is from above, is chaste, then peaceable, modest, easy to be persuaded, full of mercy and good fruits, etc. But that which is not descending from above from the Father of lights, contradicts the truth and is animal and diabolical.*

The Evangelist therefore calls the first "the truth itself," while the Apostle calls the latter the *folly of the world*. It is from this that so many and such great errors have crept into the world, so that by means of this kind of ethnic or Pagan doctrine—the wisdom of worldly dominion—the truth itself is submerged in darkness everywhere, and especially among us Christians, and is wrapped on all sides in a veil of ignorance. From this (I say) arises the uncertainty of worldly philosophy and the inconstancy of Christian philosophers. Such confidence in ethnic philosophy grows daily that those things published by the impious sons of Cain or by infidels in their philosophy are judged most worthy of faith, even though they are in truth most opposed to the intention of the Sacred Pages, as we shall show more broadly and clearly below—namely in Books 2 and 3, where we will expose the vanities of Mersenne. For he did not hesitate to assert that Aristotle was a foster-child and disciple of true philosophy, and that his philosophy was true and complete. 

From this (I say) it happens that very many professors of Theosophy <note>Fludd uses this term to mean "divine wisdom" rather than the later 19th-century movement</note> seem to judge as if looking at colors while blind. They grasp the sense of the Scriptures poorly, insofar as they confuse ethnic philosophy with divinity and accept the text of the Holy Bible more according to the letter or the shell <note>original: "corticem"</note> than the spirit and the central kernel <note>original: "nucleum centralem"</note>. Indeed, they notice their own philosophy contradicting the truth in very many places even in the literal sense, as was declared before. And this is the reason why such "learned" men in their own imagination—theologians—are accustomed to bring forth so few fruits packed with virtue and power into the public light of the world, unlike the Patriarchs, Prophets, and Apostles, or even their listeners. Such were also some of the Fathers of the Church, whose deeds and miracles fill whole volumes. Indeed, in every age of the world, and in every age of the seven Churches indicated in the Apocalypse <note>the Book of Revelation</note>, that grace—that (I say) mystic wisdom (which, by the witness of the Sage, makes men holy and friends of **GOD**) was, is, and will be granted to men, as the Sacred Writings testify on every side. For it was said in the first age of the Church to the angel of Ephesus:

<margin>Revelation 3.</margin>

> *Because thou hast tried them who say they are apostles and are not, and hast found them liars, etc. To him that overcometh, I will give to eat of the tree of life, etc.*

Now, we find elsewhere in the scriptures that the wisdom of **God** is called the tree of life.

In the second age, it was said to the angel of the Church of Smyrna: *I know thy tribulation, but*

<meta>This page continues Robert Fludd’s vigorous defense of divine "mystical Wisdom" against the French philosopher Marin Mersenne. On the previous page, Fludd began arguing that true knowledge is a gift from God, not a product of pagan or "impious" lineages. # On the Contest, Book I

"But you are rich, and you are blasphemed by those who say they are Jews and are not, but are the synagogue of Satan, etc. To the one who overcomes, I will give the hidden manna, and I will give him a white stone, and on the stone a new name written, which no one knows except the one who receives it." <note>These are citations from the Book of Revelation (2:9 and 2:17), addressed to the early Christian churches.</note> In another age, to the Angel of the Church of Thyatira: "To the one who overcomes, I will give the Morning Star, etc." From these passages, it is right to see that in every age of the Church, this **mystical Wisdom** was revealed to certain people—that one "good," I say, in which all things are contained; that one truth without which there is none; that very discipline or Holy understanding from which all true and certain science has its origin. Hence it is said that "Wisdom built her house upon seven columns," <note>original: "Sapientiam super septem columnas domum suam ædificasse," a reference to Proverbs 9:1.</note> where by the "seven columns" the Wise Man <note>Solomon</note> understood the true sciences, and not the spurious or worldly ones—he who confessed that he received all his science and knowledge of all things from Wisdom sent forth from God. The Apostle <note>St. Paul</note> intends this when he says that all other arts are her handmaidens. And although the worldly or common arts and sciences may smell of that fountain of knowledge, they are nevertheless so veiled by the chimerical shadows of the heathens, and contaminated by their filthy inventions and additions, that it is clearly shown that the Devil himself has cast his sickle into the divine harvest. Men, I say, have tasted of the tree of good and evil with their first parent, so that they have many times obscured goodness with the darkness of depravity. Thus, men in ancient times who contemplated pure truth are now seduced and blinded (to speak with the mouth of Mersenne) by the impious sons of Cain; for by sowing falsehoods with truths, they cause us wretched Christians, seduced by their deceits, to grope as if in the dark.

Wherefore, that opinion of Mersenne seems to smack of atheism, which asserts that we Christians received the sciences useful to our lives from the impious sons of Cain, rather than from the sons of God, who were the first possessors of the divine Spirit and consequently the inventors of the true sciences. We have this explained more clearly in the text of the Holy Scriptures, and especially in Exodus, where we are taught that all true science proceeds from the **Spirit of Wisdom**, which God gives to men, and not the Devil.

Hence Jehovah says: *“You shall speak to all who are wise of heart, whom I have filled with the Spirit of Wisdom, etc.”* And elsewhere: <margin>Exodus 28</margin> *“I have called by name Bezalel, whom I have filled with the Spirit of God, regarding skill, understanding, and knowledge, and for every art: for devising things invented by industry, for working in gold, silver, and brass, and in the jeweler’s craft of setting stones, and in the woodworker’s craft of performing any art. Therefore I have added Aholiab, and I have put skill into the mind of every industrious person, so that they may do whatever I have commanded you, etc.”*

Here, therefore, you see that the true origin of the sciences—not only the speculative but also the practical—is from the Spirit of Wisdom, which God did not wish to grant to the impious; and consequently, nothing is to be expected from them except human inventions and worldly science or wisdom.

<margin>Wisdom 10:2</margin> This Spirit of Wisdom gave Adam the power to contain all things. This Spirit made Abel more pleasing in the sight of God than his brother Cain, and departing from Cain, rendered him contemptible to God. <margin>Wisdom 10:1, 3</margin> This Spirit taught Noah the true method of building the ark, Moses the tabernacle of the testimony, and Solomon the temple. Indeed, David also confessed that he received the model of the temple depicted by the finger of God. <margin>Numbers 11:17</margin> So it is said that Jehovah took a portion of the Spirit of Moses and placed it upon the seventy Elders, who immediately became wise and full of the Prophetic Spirit. <margin>Wisdom 7</margin> Thus was Solomon instructed—namely, by the mediation of this Spirit—so that he had knowledge of things both hidden and manifest in the world. <margin>Wisdom 9</margin> (And yet, when this helping Spirit is neglected, as the said Wise Man testifies, the paths of those who dwell on the earth are corrected, and men are taught the things that please God.)

Marin [Mersenne] takes his refuge in the diabolical offspring of Cain, and again has recourse to the Heathens and Pagans, I say, so that he may obtain true sciences from them, and not at all from that **mystical Wisdom** which, although it is hidden in the "letter" as if in a chest, is nevertheless to be found everywhere in the spiritual kernel of the Holy Page. Indeed, this "good man" follows the philosophy of the heathen Aristotle with such devotion (even though in many things, as has been said, it is most opposed to the sense of the Holy Page) that he uses such language in its praise...

<sig>e 3</sig>
<meta>...language [continued on next page].</meta>

<summary>Robert Fludd argues that true scientific and practical knowledge originates solely from the Holy Spirit and "mystical Wisdom," rather than from the pagan or "Cainite" sources suggested by Marin Mersenne. He uses various biblical examples, such as the craftsmen of the Tabernacle and Solomon, to prove that God is the only source of authentic invention.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Robert Fludd’s aggressive critique of Marin Mersenne. In the previous section, Fludd argued that all true knowledge originates from "mystical Wisdom" (divine revelation) rather than human invention. Here, he specifically attacks Mersenne’s praise of Aristotle and the pagan foundations of medicine, arguing that relying on "heathen" knowledge over the Word of God is a form of spiritual betrayal or even atheism.</meta>

# On Wisdom versus Folly

<margin>Column 1385.</margin>
...speech. <note>Fludd is quoting from Mersenne’s own text here to critique it.</note> *Aristotle—whose name means "the best end"—has handed down to us those things which lead toward happiness and the highest goal; for to the Philosophers, of whom he was the prince, he provides a contempt for human things, a desire for the celestial, and an enlightenment of the mind that leads to bliss, etc.* What more could be said in praise of Christ, the Savior of the world, than what this "upright" man <note>Fludd is being sarcastic toward Mersenne.</note> has brought forth in honor of a pagan and infidel? But if things were truly as he says, what further need is there for the Holy Scriptures? Why do we not simply follow the doctrine of Aristotle alone, setting aside all other volumes, since "it is in vain to do through many things what can be done through fewer"? <note>This is a reference to "Occam’s Razor," the principle that the simplest explanation or method is best.</note> For what more should be sought in this life than to desire heavenly things, to reach the kingdom of the heavens, to acquire the highest enlightenment of the mind, and to arrive at the goal and shore of true happiness? If Aristotle can bring such a "highest good" to us Christians, what need is there to place any further faith in the Sacred Letters, which testify that this highest good comes solely from the Spirit of Wisdom proceeding from the mouth of Jehovah?

But if Aristotle (or his defender Mersenne) is to be believed in this, the question is whether that man ever reached the happiness of paradise through all his art. If not, why would anyone who is consistent in their thinking give credit to his fictional speech or doctrine? For (as they say) he was seized by despair and brought a miserable end to his own life. <note>Fludd refers to a common medieval legend that Aristotle committed suicide by drowning because he could not understand the tides.</note> Is this the path to happiness? Let Mersenne, therefore, choose the impious Aristotle—a "son of the impious Cain"—as his guide to happiness and the enlightenment of his mind. Meanwhile, I, as befits a true Christian, shall insist upon the paths of the Holy Scriptures. I shall seek the way of salvation, happiness, and the enlightenment of my mind from the Word and the Truth found within them, and not in Aristotelian fictions, and I shall wait for it with unwavering faith.

What also should be thought of Galen and Hippocrates? <note>Famous ancient Greek physicians whose theories dominated medicine for over a millennium.</note> Are we to believe (according to Mersenne’s assertion) that the power of the medical art is to be drawn from them alone? Mersenne affirms it, but I would like to add this: it is the power of a deceptive and uncertain medical art, founded as it is upon nothing but inconstant experience. I speak from experience myself: Hippocratic or Galenic medicine relies more on opinion than on indubitable truth. But indeed, we have drawn from the Holy Source the knowledge that the true art of healing is certain and infallible. It is through this true art that the dead were restored to life and the sick were suddenly returned to their former health by a single touch—indeed, <margin>Mark 15:17.</margin> even by the Word alone spoken from the mouth. This art was possessed by Elijah, Elisha, Christ, and the Apostles, and also by all those who are real disciples of the Apostles, as may be gathered from the words of Saint Mark. As for the true foundations of this kind of healing, the Wise Man teaches us in these words: <margin>Wisdom 16.</margin> > *Neither herb nor ointment healed them, but your Word, O Jehovah, which heals all things.* <note>original: "Neq; herba, neq; malagma sanauit eos, sed tuum O Iehoua Verbum, quod sanat omnia." From the Book of Wisdom 16:12.</note> etc. Let us therefore have our refuge in the divine oracle, if access to it can be had, rather than recurring to heathen trifles with such confidence. Indeed, God seems to be angry with us Christians because we adhere so firmly to the medical doctrines of the heathens that we neglect that true formula and fountain for curing both the soul and the body. We have this confirmed by the example of King Ahaziah, who, when afflicted by a certain disease, <margin>2 Kings 1:16.</margin> commanded his men to consult Beelzebub, the false god of Ekron, neglecting Jehovah, the God of gods, to see if he would recover his health. Because of this, the wrath of Jehovah was kindled against him, and he died without any help from the false god.

From these things it is clear to see how absurd Mersenne’s assertion is, and how inclined toward atheism, in claiming that we received all useful and excellent arts from the impious "sons of Cain" and not from God’s elect. It was to the elect that Jehovah faithfully communicated all true sciences through His Spirit, and not to the Pagans, who understood neither true Wisdom nor the Word of God correctly. Instead, the Pagans derived their philosophy from the Devil, from the teachings of the worst gods, and from the false oracles of necromancy, as well as from their own inventions and fictions...

<meta>This page continues Robert Fludd’s polemic against Marin Mersenne. Fludd is contrasting "Divine Wisdom"—which he claims as the basis for his own philosophy—with the "human wisdom" of Aristotle and the pagans, which he accuses Mersenne of following. The previous page ended mid-sentence discussing how people have brought forth their own knowledge.</meta>

...brought forth their own knowledge and the rest of the sciences.

<margin>Daniel 2:20</margin>
Which the Prophet also seems to acknowledge in a similar context of words: *Wisdom (he says) and strength belong to God; he changes the times and the ages; he gives Wisdom to the wise, and knowledge to those who understand discipline; he reveals the deep and hidden things and knows what is established in the darkness, and Light is with him; therefore the mystery which the King asks, the Wise men, magi, soothsayers, and diviners cannot judge, but there is a God in heaven revealing mysteries, etc.* <note>Fludd is quoting the Book of Daniel to argue that true insight into the "mysteries" of nature comes from God, not from human logic or pagan philosophers like Aristotle.</note> Therefore, Marin Mersenne ought to inquire, if he wishes to be truly wise, what the difference is between that Wisdom of the heathens and this Wisdom of God. For as that former wisdom proceeds from the impious and the Pagans (as he himself admits), so it cannot happen otherwise than that it draws its origin from the parent of impiety—the Devil himself, who is the fountain of lies (even though he is skilled at subtly mixing the true with the false). Conversely, this Divine Wisdom draws its beginning from the clear fountain of all truth. But the Apostle also seems to verify this in the words we previously cited, which are these: *The Wisdom that contradicts the truth is not that which descends from above from the Father of Lights, but is earthly, sensual, and diabolical, etc.* <note>original: "animalis," often translated in this period as "sensual" or "soulish," referring to the lower human nature as opposed to the spirit.</note> Indeed, Mersenne seems to be among those men of such a "clump" <note>original: "farinae," literally "of that flour/meal," a common Latin idiom for "people of that sort."</note> who are turned toward this future "Wisdom," as appears from his own text and the praises he assigns to his teacher, Aristotle. Truly, he seems to observe the precepts of that kind of Wisdom (which St. Paul calls *foolishness*) in the practice of his own life, which is most opposite to true Wisdom, of which the Apostle says:

<margin>James 3</margin>
> *The Wisdom which is from above is first pure, then peaceable, modest, easy to be persuaded, full of mercy and good fruits, not judging, without rivalry.*

But the fruits of the wisdom of the impious sons of the cursed Ham <note>A biblical reference to the son of Noah, often used in this era to denote those who are spiritually "unclean" or alienated from God's grace.</note> growing out of Mersenne are contrary to those produced by the divine; for since his wisdom is earthly, sensual, and diabolical, it has rendered him impudent, contentious, immodest, perverse, bloodthirsty, and cruel—sparing no one in his curses, and pursuing all those endowed with virtue and learning with envy and rivalry, as you (learned Readers and Judges) can see from his "fruits" expressed in his own volume. *By the envy of the Devil (says Solomon) death entered into the world; those who are on his side imitate him.*

Therefore, I pray you, judge whether this "good man," in his own opinion, is consistent with himself or not, and whether he is not profoundly submerged in the sleep of ignorance—he who willingly accepts evil for good, falsehood for truth, and darkness for Light. Does the Prophet not intend to speak of such men when he says: *Woe to those who call evil good and good evil, who set darkness for light and light for darkness, etc.*

From these things, therefore (unless I am mistaken), it is clear to a mind free from the filth of darkness that the subject of true Philosophy—extracted from the very sacred fountain and confirmed by the testimony of the entire Holy Bible—must be held in higher value than that which is drawn from the fantastic opinion of a pagan man. Insofar as it is Divine Wisdom, and consequently insofar as it is the sole truth, it must be held in the highest estimation, while that human or worldly wisdom is relegated forever beyond the Hyperborean limits <note>A classical reference to the far northern "edge of the world," symbolizing extreme exile or the outer darkness.</note> into exile where darkness holds dominion, because there is no truth except in Divine Wisdom. Now, however, we think it worthwhile to put an end to this book with a certain text of his that "barks" at me and Campanella; <note>Tommaso Campanella (1568–1639), a contemporary Italian philosopher and astrologer who, like Fludd, was often attacked by Mersenne for his unconventional views.</note> for it pertains greatly to the scope of our subject of Physics.

### Text of Marin [Mersenne] from Column 714.

<margin>Col. 714.</margin>
*We have said several things against the Atheists which clearly agree that God exists; for whatever opinion, or even whatever trifles of the Paracelsists, Campanellists, Fludds, and others' supporters, one must always recur to something that produced that Chaos of the macrocosm <term>megalocosmos: the "great world" or the entire universe</term>, and attributed to some agent—whether the Sun, or heat, or light—that power which produced the rest of the visible things from the primary mass (even if, by hypothesis, it contained everything by a containing power) and drew them forth into the light. Thus, no truth, nor even the fable itself, favors the Atheists. For that Chaos could not make itself, nor can you call that thing eternal which existed for all eternity before the drawing-forth of forms, since there is no greater reason why at this or that [time]...*

# On Wisdom and Folly

*...whether they came forth into the light at this or that time, rather than from eternity—unless perhaps you were to say that the initial Chaos itself intended to emit those forms and the poorly distinguished parts of the world from itself, but reserved that for time, etc.*

### Robert Fludd’s Examination.

You see here with how much malice this author of ours <note>The "author" refers to Marin Mersenne, Fludd’s chief intellectual rival.</note> continues to rage against Robert without any just reason being given; namely, by calling those things in his works "trifles" which he himself seems frankly to confess—provided Robert’s name is suppressed—do not differ at all from the Holy Scriptures. Indeed, led by some sort of envy, he wished, if he could, to include Fludd along with several other learned men in the category of atheists or the promotion of atheism; even though, nevertheless, he is able to refute nothing in our writings. 

However, you can discern from the preceding arguments how far our opinions stand from Atheism. In fact, Mersenne’s own writings (as they wander from the true paths of divine literature) prove him to be a thousand times more an atheist, or a false supporter of atheism, than mine prove me. For my writings attribute all things produced in creation primarily to God, the Best and Greatest, following Moses, the Prophets, and the Apostles. I do not attribute them to the vain and false principles of the Heathens, as Mersenne himself does, being nurtured on the spurious and vain Philosophy of Aristotle and, as it were, spending his beginning and his youth in the filth of the heathens with the "pigs" of his own doctrine. <note>Fludd is using a common polemical trope, comparing Mersenne’s reliance on pagan philosophy to the Prodigal Son feeding the swine.</note> 

I, therefore, receive my Philosophy from the fountain of all truth, where I acknowledge a unique and solo God acting in the creation and preservation of the world. I acknowledge that He is the acting center in every single thing (not in a way that limits Him), but He is the one "whose circumference is nowhere," <note>This is a famous Hermetic definition of God: "God is an infinite sphere whose center is everywhere and whose circumference is nowhere."</note> that is, He is in all things and outside all things. By His sole and all-sufficient life-giving virtue, He is present everywhere in the world, animating it and preserving all things within it by His benevolent presence. This I have also received from the infallible precept of the royal Psalmist, who says:

<margin>Psalm 103:18 <note>In modern English Bibles, this is Psalm 104:27-30.</note></margin>
> That all creatures look to God; when He opens His hand, all things shall be filled with goodness; but when He turns away His face, they are troubled; when He takes away His Spirit, they shall fail and return to their dust; when He sends forth His Spirit, they shall be created, and the face of the earth shall be renewed, etc.

I acknowledge, I say, that the creature depends upon the divine Word and virtue. I am fixed in Unity; he is drowned in the multitude of Heathen uncertainty. For he calls the Peripatetic or Aristotelian Philosophy <note><term>Peripatetic Philosophy</term>: The school of philosophy founded by Aristotle, so named because he allegedly taught while walking.</note> "true" in the aforementioned text, and consequently he seems to judge our philosophy—which we have fortified on all sides by the testimony of Holy Scripture—as false. In this way, he himself strives to release Barabbas (which is interpreted as "son of confusion") along with the Jews, but wishes to keep Christ—who is the only Truth, the Life of the world, and eternal Wisdom—always bound in darkness.

But now, concerning the **Soul of the World** <note><term>Anima Mundi</term>: The "World Soul," an invisible spirit that Neoplatonists believed animated the entire universe as if it were a single living being.</note> (which Mersenne himself regards as a thing so empty and futile), we think it very useful and necessary in this place to put forward some discourses most opposite to his mind and opinion. By these, we shall clearly show to the world that, despite the objections of him and others of his kind, there is a Soul of the World, and it exists in the world in no other way than the soul exists in a human being.

On the Conflict, Book II.

# BOOK TWO.

## IN WHICH THAT FAMOUS QUESTION,
## Whether there is a world soul, is discussed; and finally,
## against the insane opinion of Marin Mersenne, it is
## vigorously defended that there is a world soul.

->Marin's Text, Columns 1451, 1452.<-

<image-desc>Ornate woodcut drop cap 'V' with scrolling floral and vine motifs.</image-desc>
> *But since there is no world soul, it should be most carefully avoided, so that those shadow-dwellers <note>original: "tenebriones"; refers to those who operate in darkness or deal in hidden, suspect arts</note> hear the worse of it—those who busily try to recall it after Plato, Iamblichus, Macrobius, Apuleius, Thales, and Agrippa. For if those monstrous ideas which the gentiles asserted were concluded—namely, that this celestial soul is the source of all souls which serve God the Creator, as can be seen in Apuleius regarding the dogma of Plato—some universal mind would exist through which individuals would understand, just as the impious Arabs believed with Averroes <note>Averroes (Ibn Rushd) was a medieval Andalusian philosopher known for the theory of "Monopsychism," the idea that all humans share one single intellect.</note>. Finally, to let many other things pass, not only the Magi, but also the Atheists would protect and propagate their impieties by means of this soul and this spiritual vehicle.*

->Robert’s Examination.<-

Here it seems the author has read much, but does not possess judgment, nor can he distinguish the truth, nor refute anything in a philosophical manner. Instead, all the power of this monk's <note>Marin Mersenne was a member of the Order of Minims, a Roman Catholic religious order.</note> refutation seems to be: "it is so or so because it is so," or "because it seems so to me," with no other reason given. Or simply "because he said it." For he has nothing from his own brain. 

Why we act in this little book concerning the world soul <term>World Soul: In Latin, "Anima Mundi"; the idea that the universe itself is a living being with a rational soul.</term> against his mind, we will briefly recount here. For it is not our habit to draw the edge of our pen against anyone's opinions, however false, unless we are stirred to it and violently provoked by the incitement of others. 

The first reason, therefore, is because we have made that world soul (which Mersenne denies exists in the nature of things) the chief agent in our mundane harmony. If it is taken away, it will be necessary for the consonances of our mundane harmony to be utterly removed, and consequently, the whole harmony or mundane music would lack both tones and symphony. 

The second reason is that, in express words, this Mersenne denies there is any world soul in the nature of things, against both the opinion of Johannes Kepler <note>Johannes Kepler (1571–1630), the famous astronomer who also sought a harmonic "soul" of the earth to explain planetary movements.</note>, a most learned man, and our own. His words are these:

->Marin's Text, Column 1561.<-

> *Therefore, whether you admit the Keplerian soul of the heart of the earth, which corresponds to the various angles of celestial aspects as soon as it perceives them, or the Fluddian soul of the air, which is a part of the world soul (about which he treats in his analysis of Kepler’s 13th Text), or whether you establish no world soul—as I truly judge there to be none, such as those Neoplatonists <term>Neoplatonists: a school of mystical philosophy that followed Plato's ideas, emphasizing the world soul as a link between God and matter.</term> imagine, etc.*

->Besides these things, also at Column 1744, he says: "I challenge Fludd with his world soul."<-

Moved by these reasons, therefore, we have decided to descend into the public field of the world in the cause of its most noble and divine soul. In which business, I hope that the literate men of the world—if they deign to look into the matter with a slightly more accurate gaze—will see that I shall remove them from the false, atheistic, and truly insane opinions of Marin Mersenne and direct them into the paths of truth. Whether I should speak of the Cabalists' angel Metatron <note>original: "Mitattron"; a high-ranking angel in Jewish mystical tradition, often identified as the mediator between the divine and the physical world.</note> (lest he call me a magus for naming angels), or the invisible fire of Zoroaster and Heraclitus of Ephesus, from which they said all things in the world were born, or the opinion of Democritus, Orpheus, and the Pythagoreans...

<meta>The catchword "pinio-" indicates the next page begins with the completion of the word "opinio" (opinion).</meta>

Of Wisdom with Folly
<meta>Catchword: let us show,</meta>

...whether they came forth into the light at this or that time, rather than from eternity—unless perhaps you were to say that the original Chaos did not wish to emit those forms and poorly-distinguished parts of the world from itself earlier, but reserved them for a specific time, and so on.

### Robert Fludd’s examination.

You see here with what great malice this author of ours <note>Referring to Marin Mersenne</note> continues to rage against Robert without any just reason being given—namely, by calling those things in his works "nonsense" which he himself, though Robert’s name is suppressed, seems frankly to confess do not at all deviate from the **Holy Scriptures**. Indeed, led by some sort of envy, he wished, if he were able, to include Fludd along with many other learned men in the number of atheists or within the scope of atheism; yet nevertheless, he is able to refute nothing in our writings. 

However, you can discern from what has come before how far our opinions stand from **Atheism**. Indeed, Mersenne’s own writings (as they wander away from the true paths of divine literature) prove a thousand times over that he himself is either an atheist or at least a promoter of atheism, more so than my own works prove me to be. For my writings attribute all things produced in the creation primarily to the Best and Greatest God, along with **Moses, the Prophets, and the Apostles**, and not to the vain and false principles of the Pagans <note>original: "Ethnicorum"</note>, as does Marin himself, who was nourished on the spurious and vain philosophy of **Aristotle** and, as if living among the filth of the pagans like swine, spent the beginning and youth of his teaching there. 

I, therefore, have my **Philosophy** from the fountain of all truth, where I acknowledge one and only one God acting in the creation and preservation of the world. I acknowledge that He is the acting center in every single thing—not in a restricted sense, but as one *whose circumference is nowhere* <note>This is a famous Hermetic definition of God: "God is an infinite sphere whose center is everywhere and whose circumference is nowhere."</note>—that is, He is in all things and beyond all things. By His sole and all-sufficient being-making <note>original: "essentifica"</note> virtue, He is present everywhere in the world, animating it and preserving all things within it by His benevolent presence. This I have also received from the infallible royal precept of the **Psalmist**, 
<margin>Psalm 103:18 <note>In modern Bibles, this is Psalm 104:27-30.</note></margin> 
who says: 
> That all creatures look to God; when He opens His hand, all things shall be filled with goodness; but when He turns away His face, they are troubled; when He takes away His Spirit, they fail and shall return to their dust; when He sends forth His Spirit, they shall be created, and the face of the earth shall be renewed, etc.

I acknowledge (I say) that the creature depends upon the **Word** and divine virtue. I am fixed in **Unity**; he is submerged in the multitude of Pagan uncertainty. For he calls the **Peripatetic** or Aristotelian philosophy "true" in the aforementioned text, and consequently seems to judge our philosophy—which we have fortified on every side by the testimony of Holy Scripture—as false. In this way, he himself busily strives to release **Barabbas** (which is interpreted as "son of confusion") along with the Jews, but to keep **Christ**—who is the only truth and the life of the world, and eternal **Wisdom**—forever bound in darkness.

But now, concerning the **World Soul** (which Mersenne himself considers to be a thing so empty and trivial), we think it quite useful and necessary in this place to cast out several discourses utterly opposed to his mind and opinion. By these, we may clearly 
->show<- 
to the world that, despite him and others of the same grain, the **World Soul** exists, and is in the world in no other way than the soul is in a man.

<meta>This page continues Robert Fludd’s vigorous defense of the "Anima Mundi" (World Soul) against the attacks of the French priest and mathematician Marin Mersenne. Fludd is transitioning from citing ancient philosophical authorities to grounding his arguments in Christian Scripture to prove that the universe is a living, divinely-animated organism.</meta>

# On Wisdom with Mary <note>The header refers to Fludd's theological identification of Divine Wisdom (Sophia) with the archetypal feminine, often associated with the Virgin Mary in his mystical framework.</note>

...the opinion with which they began to persuade the world: that a God or a certain soul presides over every single thing, and that all things are full of gods—whom they nonetheless all referred back to a single Jove <note>Jupiter/Zeus, used here as a name for the supreme monotheistic God in a philosophical context</note>. This was the view of the *Platonists*, *Astrologers*, *Egyptians*, and *Chaldeans* regarding the World Soul, by means of which they maintained that the world itself is a living animal, and that the heavens and stars are living creatures. Even *Mercury Trismegistus* <note>The mythical founder of Hermeticism</note>, *Aristotle*, *Theophrastus*, *Avicenna*, *Algazeles* <note>Al-Ghazali, the Persian theologian</note>, and all the *Stoics* and *Peripatetics* <note>Followers of Aristotle</note> seem to fall into this opinion. So too does the divine power of the soul mentioned by *Marcus Manilius* <note>A Roman poet-astrologer</note>, which rules the work of the vast world; or the spirit or mind of *Virgil* <note>Referencing the Aeneid, Book VI</note> infused through the limbs of the world, moving its entire mass; or the soul of triple nature in the middle described by *Boethius*, which moves all things; or that nature or infinite power which the true *Chymists* <note>Alchemists</note> have called the bond of the mundane elements, by whose assistance all things are generated, increased, and preserved in the world. I say I do not dare to produce the testimonies of these men regarding the existence of the World Soul against the vain quibbles of *Mersenne*, since their doctrine has been refuted and rejected by him. What then remains to be done, except that we take refuge in the Sacred Font <note>Holy Scripture</note>? But truly, he even tries to deny me—for as he says, I am a "heretic"—the use of sacred authority, as was declared in the *Letter* placed at the beginning of this work. If it were within his power to do so! But let me (kind readers) be granted the license to plead this cause of the World Soul seriously and strenuously, using arguments drawn from my own quiver as well as testimonies drawn from the Sacred Font. I shall manfully and in the manner of *Hercules* defend the life of the world, by which it is accustomed to live, against that hydra <note>A multi-headed monster from Greek myth, representing Mersenne's many-layered errors</note> contaminated with the poison of error and darkness.

<margin>Column 282.</margin>
> *I challenge Fludd* (says *Marin*) *with his World Soul. But I confess that Fludd acknowledged a World Soul in Treatise 1, Book 4, of his History of the Macrocosm. But what kind? A divine power, of course, and as it were the God of nature itself, or that middle Soul of Nature, by means of which God is said to be everywhere in the world, to fill it, to moderate it, and to preserve it continually. And this is that divine power which God liberally rains down upon created things from the perennial source of his spirit, by which all things live, are sustained, grow, and are multiplied in their species; which World Soul the Platonists believed was located chiefly in the sun.* These are the words of *Fludd*, which appear to have so sharply displeased *Mersenne*.

But let us see now if this opinion of *Fludd* regarding the World Soul wanders so emptily from the source of truth as *Mersenne*—that "little saint"—seems to assert and publish to the world through his trifling quibbles.

If we prove that God inspired the world at its creation with his life-giving spirit, by which we observe it to live, move, and be changed by various alternations and altered every year; if we show that his Word or Wisdom, emanating from his sincere splendor, fills the world and continually preserves it; if we clearly demonstrate that the very life of all things is in this Word, and that this Wisdom vivifies all things in the world—since it is the medium through which all parts of the world (and consequently the whole, which is fashioned from the parts) are animated and live—what more is there for mortals to grasp at concerning the World Soul? Moreover, that God fills all things in this world stands out in many places in the sacred writings. "God" (says the Apostle) "fills all things" <note>original: "omnia implet"; likely referencing Ephesians 4:10</note> etc. Therefore, if it is necessary that He fills *all things*, He also fills the world itself. And the Royal Prophet <note>King David</note> says that the spirit of God is in heaven, in hell, in the furthest parts of the sea, in the night, and in the darkness, etc. By these words, he seems to denote that He is everywhere in the world and, consequently, fills it. And *Solomon* affirms that "the holy Spirit of discipline fills the world." And elsewhere it is said: "God poured out his Wisdom upon all his works, and upon all flesh according to his gift." And elsewhere, "heaven is my seat, the earth..."

<margin>
4 Ephesians 8.
Psalm 139:7.
Wisdom 1.
Ecclesiastes 1.
</margin>

->On the Contest, Book II.<-

# BOOK TWO
## IN WHICH THAT FAMOUS QUESTION,
## Whether there is a World Soul, is discussed: and finally, against the
## insane opinion of Marin Mersenne, it is vigorously 
## defended that there is a World Soul.

<margin>Marin’s Text, Columns 1451, 1452.</margin>
<image-desc>Decorative woodcut initial 'V' featuring scrolling acanthus leaves and floral elements.</image-desc>
But since *there is no World Soul* <note>original: "anima mundi"</note>, one must be more diligently on guard against it, for those dealers in darkness <note>original: "tenebriones," referring to those who work in obscurity or deception</note> have an even worse reputation who strive to revive it following the steps of Plato, Iamblichus, Macrobius, Apuleius, Thales, and Agrippa. Indeed, if those monstrous ideas were established which the Pagans asserted—namely, that this celestial soul is the source of all souls who serve under God the Creator, as can be seen in Apuleius regarding the dogma of Plato—then there would be some universal mind through which individuals understand, as the impious Arabs believed along with Averroes <note>Averroes was a medieval philosopher whose "monopsychism" suggested all humans share a single intellect, a view condemned by the Church</note>. Finally, to pass over many other things, not only Magicians but even Atheists might protect and propagate their impieties by means of this "soul" and this "spiritual vehicle."

<margin>Robert’s examination.</margin>
Here the author seems to have read much, but to possess no judgment, nor to distinguish the truth, nor to refute anything in a philosophical manner. Rather, the entire strength of this Monk <note>Referring to Marin Mersenne, who was a member of the Minim order</note> in his refutation seems to be: "it is so, or it is thus, because it is so," or "because it seems so to me," with no other reason given—or simply "because he himself said it." For he has nothing of his own to offer from his own brain. As for why we argue for the World Soul against his mind in this little book, we shall briefly recount here. For it is not our habit to draw the edge of our pen against anyone’s opinions, however false, unless we have been stirred to it and provoked by the violent incitement of others. 

The first reason, therefore, is that we have made that World Soul (which Mersenne denies exists in the nature of things) the principal agent in our **World Harmony** <note>original: "harmonia nostra mundana"</note>. If this is taken away, it must follow that the consonances of our World Harmony are utterly removed, and consequently, that the entire harmony or world-music would lack both tones and symphony. The second reason is that, in express words, this Mersenne denies there is any World Soul in the nature of things, going against both the opinion of **Johannes Kepler** <note>The famous astronomer with whom Fludd also famously debated</note>, a most learned man, and our own. His words are these:

<margin>Marin’s text, Column 1561.</margin>
> Therefore, whether you admit the Keplerian soul concerning the heart of the earth, which corresponds to various angles of the celestial aspects as soon as it perceives them, or the Fluddian soul of the air, which is a part of the World Soul (concerning which Fludd treats in his analysis of the 13th Text of Kepler), or you establish no World Soul—as I truly think there is none of the sort that those New Platonists imagine, etc.

*Beyond this, also in Column 1744, he says: "I challenge Fludd with his World Soul."*

Moved therefore by these reasons, we have decided to descend into the public field of the world to plead the cause of this most noble and divine Soul. In which business, I hope that the learned of the world will deign to look upon the matter with a slightly more accurate gaze, and that I shall lead them away from the false, atheistic, and truly insane opinions of Marin Mersenne, directing them back onto the paths of truth. Whether it be the angel Metatron <note>original: "Mitattron," a high-ranking angel in Jewish Kabbalistic tradition</note> of the Kabbalists (unless he should call me a magician for naming angels), or the invisible fire of **Zoroaster** and **Heraclitus of Ephesus**, from which they said all things in the world were born, or the o-

# Of the Contest, Book II.

<margin>Wisdom 7</margin>
<margin>1 Timothy 6</margin>
<margin>Amos 1 <note>Likely referring to Amos 5:8, describing God’s power over the stars.</note></margin>
<margin>3 Esdras 4</margin>
<margin>Ecclesiasticus 24</margin>

...and the earth is my footstool <note>original: "Terra autem scabellum pedum meorum"; a reference to Isaiah 66:1</note>. Now, among all good Christians, it is accepted from what has been said that the Divine Spirit of God, or the Word (which is God himself), fills the created spirit of the world. Let us now inquire into the nature and condition of this uncreated spirit. By searching through the Holy Bible, we find that it possesses every virtue within itself; it gives life and soul to all things; it grants motion, agility, and power to all. It made the world and everything in it, works all things in all people, serves all things from its own source, and accomplishes everything according to its will—as much in the angels, the stars, and the entire spirit of the world as in the inhabitants of the earth. Solomon says, *The Holy Spirit of wisdom has all power in itself, etc. whence it can do all things, both for the giving of life to the world and its preservation.* And the Apostle <note>Referring to St. Paul</note> holds that *this Spirit gives life to all things.* Why then should it not give life to the world which it fills? For the world is the "whole" made of all the parts contained within it. Just as it is said that the soul of a man gives life to all the parts from which a man is composed—namely, the heart, brain, liver, hands, feet, and so on—it follows by the same clear reasoning that the Divine Spirit animates its own "whole" <note>the universe</note>. Truly, we are taught from the Holy Scriptures that this divine spirit animates the parts of the world in general, generously bestowing motion, activity, and life-giving power upon them, and indeed upon the whole. *He who makes Arcturus and Orion, turns the shadows into morning, changes day into night, calls the waters of the sea and pours them out upon the face of the earth: Jehovah is his name.* And elsewhere: *The most mighty God, whose truth is, turns the heaven in a circuit to its place in one day.* By these passages it is clearly taught that the Spirit of God is the unique internal or central soul of the heavens, by means of which they are led in their circuit. This is also confirmed by the words of the one who is the wonderful worker of these deeds—namely, that Spirit of Wisdom, who says: *I, Wisdom, have circled the vault of heaven alone, I have penetrated the depth of the abyss, I have walked upon the waves of the sea, and in the heavens, so that a never-failing Light might arise, and like a mist I covered the whole earth, etc.* Here, therefore, you have the spirit informing, moving, and animating heaven and earth, and consequently the entire universe. You have (I say) God himself, who works all in all, as the Apostle testifies. Of whom the Prophet also says: *He does according to his will, both among the powers of heaven and the inhabitants of the earth*, where by "powers of heaven" he seems to understand not only the angels, but the stars themselves.

<margin>John 1</margin>
<margin>Ecclesiasticus 1</margin>
<margin>Proverbs 3</margin>
<margin>Ecclesiasticus 6</margin>
<margin>1 Timothy 6</margin>
<margin>Proverbs 1</margin>
<margin>Judith 16</margin>
<margin>Acts 17</margin>
But despite all this, I have dared to say that Mersenne <note>Marin Mersenne (1588–1648), Fludd's intellectual rival</note> will by no means admit that the world is enriched by any soul. But what does this speech of the Evangelist mean? *All things were made by him, and without him nothing; in him was life, etc.* If, therefore, the wisdom that gives life to the entire world is that "life" in the Word, proceeding from the Word into the activity of the world (for, as Ecclesiasticus witnesses, *the fountain of Wisdom is the Word of God on high*), and if that Wisdom fills and animates the world everywhere by its life-giving presence, then it follows that the world itself is filled and endowed with life and, consequently, with a universal soul. Indeed, this Wisdom is most truly the *tree of life*, and even the *fountain* and origin of life itself, as it is that in which the beauty of universal life resides. As the Scriptures assert, if we call this the universal activity of the spirit of the world, and consequently the universal life <note>original: "vitam Catholicam"; here "catholic" means universal/all-encompassing, not the Roman Catholic Church</note>, we do not lie, insofar as we have the Holy Scripture agreeing with this opinion everywhere. For *if the Spirit of God (as has been said) gives life to all things*, then it follows that it also gives life to the world, insofar as the world is a creature; and consequently, no one of sound mind can deny that the world enjoys a soul. If (I say) God, through this Spirit (which is said to serve him), by sending forth his spirit before him, created everything that exists according to the testimony of the Holy Bible, and if that Spirit of his is the *breath of life*, it is necessary that this Spirit, breathed into the world, should act as its specific soul. Thus, this world was made unique, according to the pattern and likeness of the one God who creates. We even have this confirmed in express words where we read: *God, not being in need of anything, gives to all things life, and breath, and all things, etc.*

He cites numerous biblical passages to refute Marin Mersenne's rejection of this concept, portraying the world as a living creature sustained by God's breath.</summary>

->Of Wisdom with Folly<-
A2

From this it follows (if any faith is to be placed in the Holy Page <note>original: "paginæ Sanctæ," referring to the Bible</note>) that God has given both life and inspiration and all things; and consequently, that He Himself—clothed in light, or rather, dressed in the clear spirit of the world (which His own bright presence illuminates)—is the **soul of the world** <note>original: "mundi anima"</note> insofar as He animates, inspires, and vivifies it. Furthermore, that mundane spirit which He puts on like a garment is the **spirit of the world**, and the earth, both celestial (such as the stars) and sublunary <note>The region below the moon, i.e., the changing earthly realm</note>, is the **body** which He occupies by means of that spirit—no differently than the human soul occupies the human body, with the spirit acting as its vehicle. But if, by a slightly more accurate inquiry, we look into the condition of that spirit sent by God into the world at the instant of its creation, we will indeed find that this **Universal Life** <note>original: "vita Catholica," meaning 'universal' in the original Greek sense</note> is the sole actor and author of life-giving power in every particle of the world.

Job 28:25

*The works of God (says the Psalmist) are wonderful, which He made in **Wisdom**, by which He adapts the weight to the air, and weighs out the waters in measure, and makes statutes for the rain, and a path for the lightning of the thunder; by which the depths broke themselves open, and the heavens drop down dew; which, proceeding from the mouth of the Most High like a mist, opened the earth, and alone encompassed the circuit of the heavens, and walked in the depth of the abyss; by which God made the sapphire clouds, and laid low the waterskins of heaven: by which He made the earth in the beginning and stretched out the heavens; which, by running most swiftly, brings forth snow like wool, etc. By which He turns darkness into morning, and changes day into night; by which He turns the heaven in a circle into its place in a single day: by which, as if by the hand of the Most High, the heaven is fitted to revolve in the circuit of His glory: by which God set the measures of the earth, stretched the line upon it, fixed its bases, and chose its cornerstone. Indeed, this is that very Spirit who (according to the Royal Psalmist) placed His tabernacle in the **Sun**, from which God works no less powerfully in the lower and upper parts of the world than that incorruptible Spirit of God acts in the center of the human heart: for He agitates the universal spirit of life—in both the **Macrocosm** <note>the universe</note> and the **Microcosm** <note>the human being</note>—wherever He wishes, moving it to perfect anything according to His good pleasure. And hence it is said of the visible sun: "The sun, illuminating all things, looked upon them, and its work is full of the glory of the Lord," etc. Where, by "glory"*

<margin>
Eccl. 42
</margin>

of the Lord, he understands that **spirit of wisdom** which Solomon called an "emanation of almighty charity" and the "brightness of eternal light." This spirit, placing its seat in the center of the solar creature, animates it so that it serves in the place of a heart and life; it is by means of this that He makes His way through the circuit of the heavens, whence we read of Him elsewhere: "The Lord is great who made the Sun, at whose word it hastened its journey."

But here, in passing, I warn Marin [Mersenne] to rightly understand what sort of "words of Jehovah" <note>original: "Iehovæ sermones"</note> these are, and of what nature and condition they be, before he judges these matters with such confidence. For because of his ignorance regarding the nature of the divine word, he has fallen into as many errors in his swelling volume as there are pages within it. It is from this that his great hatred for the **Cabala** <note>The Kabbalah; Fludd integrated Jewish mystical traditions into his Christian philosophy</note> arises, and why the Kabbalistic characters were condemned by him—poor little Kabbalist—and exiled from his understanding and capacity. He seems to take formal words for artificial and literally written ones, and takes the **fiery characters** <note>The idea that God's creative speech is literally inscribed in the light and matter of the universe</note> inscribed by the finger of God on the margin of dark matter <note>original: "hylæ tenebrosæ"</note> for those exterior ones made by art. Thus his error, and with that error the vehemence of his malice, is openly shown; and indeed not without reason, since (according to that worn-out proverb) *no one is an enemy of knowledge except the ignorant*. But back to our subject. From the aforementioned passage, it is clearly declared that the Sun is moved and agitated not by itself, but by the fiery and lucid impulse of an internal spirit—since that spirit is most rich in divine rays. Consequently, it is clear that the Sun itself enjoys life and soul no less than the heart of the Microcosm [the human]. And then it also follows that, just as the human heart is the origin of life or the vital soul in the human machine, from which the rays of life are dispersed through the arteries into the whole human body: so indeed, from this royal palace of the Macrocosm [the universe], this divine spirit—truly holding the scepter of the world—ejaculates the streams of life everywhere to every part of the world. Thus, this spirit, by means of a mundane vehicle, permeates all beings, and exists in every thing of this world, more or less. Is this opinion of ours discordant with the Holy Page, which says (as was cited above) that the spirit of Wisdom fills the globe? In this way, it is declared that He is the **Soul** of the entire mundane fabric, and then it is proven that He is present in every thing.
