*For (says Solomon) "the incorruptible spirit is in every thing":* whence also elsewhere: *"God gives life and inspiration and all things."* And elsewhere: *"God the Father of all, who is over all, and through all."* And elsewhere: *"God vivifies all things."* And elsewhere: *"In wisdom you have made all things; the earth is filled with your possession,"* that is, by your power and spirit. *"In Him (says St. John) was life."* And elsewhere: *"God works all in all."* Finally: *"From Him, through Him, and in Him are all things,"* as the same Apostle testifies. By these citations, one may see that the opinion of **Democritus**, **Orpheus**, and certain **Pythagoreans** does not seem to stray or wander much—indeed, not at all—from the path of truth; they considered all things to be full of Gods. It is as if they had said with the Wise Man: every single thing is imbued with the incorruptible spirit of God, by means of which those things have life, essence, and existence. But they referred all those particular "Gods" back to the one and only Jove, in—

<meta>This page continues Robert Fludd’s argument that the "Universal Life" or World Soul is the direct instrument of God. # Of the Contest, Book II.

f 3

<meta>catchword: that</meta>

...arguing and understanding that all the spirits of particular living creatures have a continuous relationship to that full and immeasurable fountain of wisdom, from which—like little streams of life—they have flowed forth. Yet, they are not so divided that they can be cut off or separated from their source; for this divine essence is indivisible, and relates to itself just as solar rays relate to the sun. Regarding this, Hermes Trismegistus <note>A legendary Hellenistic figure associated with the *Corpus Hermeticum*, a foundation of Renaissance alchemy and philosophy.</note> rightly speaks in the second book of the *Pimander*: *The Mind is by no means divided from the divine essence, but is rather connected to it in the same way that light is connected to the body of the sun.* <margin>Pimander 2</margin> From these things, therefore, it is right to notice by how strong and essential a bond that divine spirit—giving life to all things—is tied to its creatures, namely the world and those things contained in the world. Hence it was said by the Royal Psalmist <note>King David, traditional author of the Psalms.</note> that the works of God declare His glory. And Job says that God has adorned the heavens by His spirit. <margin>Job 26:13</margin> Similarly, that the works of God are wonderful and vast, all of which He made in His wisdom. <margin>Ps. 104:24</margin> <margin>Ps. 19:1</margin> But among all the visible creatures of heaven, that **solar vessel** is exalted with greater dignity, as it is the place where Wisdom especially placed her tabernacle <note>Original: "tabernaculum," referring to a dwelling place or tent.</note>. And from this, the author of Ecclesiasticus sings in the place cited before: *The sun, when it appears, proclaims at its rising: a marvelous vessel, the work of the Most High.* <margin>Eccl. 43:2</margin>

By these points, anyone consistent with themselves can clearly see that the simplest and purest soul of the world—the **Universal Spirit of Life** <note>original: "spiritus vitæ Catholicus." Fludd uses "Catholic" in its literal Greek sense of "Universal."</note>—has its first and principal seat in the solar vessel. For there is nothing in the very circle of the firmament that displays a greater likeness of God than light itself. Since, according to the testimony of Augustine and others most skilled in both Philosophy and Theosophy <note>Divine wisdom or mystical insight.</note>, any thing claims for itself as much of God as it is capable of receiving light. Indeed, the created sun (than which nothing in the world is more "eye-like") is called the *eye of the world* by the Platonists and especially Orpheus—and not without good counsel. For in it, as if in a most clear mirror, all things are seen and shown; not (I say) through itself, but by grace of the immense, shining power of that spirit which vivifies and illuminates it. *In your light*, says David, *we shall see light.* So also in the light of Wisdom, which is clothed in a luminous spirit like a garment, the world and everything in it are illuminated at the periphery. For that spirit is the fountain of light, the light of lights, and the soul of souls, vivifying and sustaining everything from the foundation. Therefore, concerning this sort of super-celestial sun, which placed its tabernacle in the celestial sun (wherefore it is called a "marvelous work of the Most High"), the Prophet understands these words: *God is a sun, giving life and glory, etc.* <margin>Ps. 84:11</margin> Here the Prophet seems to mean that **central sun**. That primary and uncreated light placed its tabernacle in the external sun, as has been said. Therefore, it should be held as granted, as it seems to me, that this central sun—from its vessel which is "marvelous to look upon" insofar as that internal spirit has adorned it with beauty—shoots out the rays of life and being to every creature. For this incorruptible spirit (as was reported more often before) is said to be in all things. This sun, I say, clearer than all light—being indeed an emanation of the brightness and splendor of the Almighty <margin>Wisdom 7</margin>—gave both life and glory to the external and visible sun. For it is necessary that that light of the world, which is life itself or the fountain and origin of lives, should illuminate all those things by its presence, no differently than Moses' face was adorned and clarified with visible light and his soul with prophetic illumination by the mere sight of that divinity. Much more so is the celestial sun clarified, whose substance that eternal Wisdom chose for its **macrocosmic tabernacle** <note>The "great world" or universe, as opposed to the "microcosm" of the human being.</note> because of its purity and cleanness.

For this reason, several have called the visible sun, being thus animated, the **heart of the sky** or of the world—and they did so not inaptly. For just as in the human heart there exists a living fountain and origin of vital spirits, from which the rays and streams of life are accustomed to gush forth everywhere through the whole human body to vivify its members; so in the sun there appear to be the vegetation, multiplication, and conservation of all things, both lower and higher. For it inspires life and heat into lower things, in a certain way, by its light. Consequently, the entire world is said for certain to be animated, illuminated, and to live by its internal and central spirit, which emits its rays everywhere. For by the sight of this external sun, looking forth from its bright and glorious palace, we gather by common observation that all the powers of all the heavenly bodies gather into one; we believe these powers are finally scattered into this lower world through its bright or fiery breathing-holes. The greatest argument for this thing to us is that we notice the earth growing green and, as it were, reaching puberty as the sun turns toward us, but when it departs beyond the equinox, the earth loses its flowers and, as it were, grows old. Do not also goats and sheep, or sparrows and the rest of the animals—whether flying, terrestrial, or aquatic—observe the statutes of breeding and multiplying according to the various dispositions of the sun in its seasons? Indeed, we are even taught by that "God" of Mersenne’s <note>Marin Mersenne, Fludd's intellectual rival.</note> philosophy, namely Aristotle, that "man and the sun beget man." But in truth, as it seems to me, neither the master <note>Aristotle</note>—nor (what is worst) his Christian disciple <note>Mersenne</note>—truly understood the sun...

<summary>Fludd argues that the sun is the physical dwelling place (tabernacle) of the divine World Soul, acting as the heart of the universe that pumps life and light into all creatures. He uses the analogy of the human heart and cites Hermetic and Biblical authorities to prove that God animates the world through this central solar spirit.</summary>
<keywords>World Soul, Anima Mundi, solar theology, Macrocosm, Microcosm, Hermes Trismegistus, Marin Mersenne, vital spirits, divine light, universal life</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Robert Fludd’s philosophical defense of the "Divine Sun" as the source of all life. He distinguishes between those who see only the physical "husk" of the world (like Aristotle) and those who recognize the divine light dwelling at the center of all things. He uses biblical passages to prove that the sun and weather are not merely mechanical, but are moved by a rational, internal spirit.</meta>

# Of Wisdom and Folly

...ever recognized that internal [light]: For that light was in the darkness and the darkness did not <margin>John 1.</margin> comprehend it; it came to its own, and its own did not receive it, but as many as received it shall have the privilege to be called children of God; the world was made through it, but the world did not know it, etc. Where by "the world" he understands the "children of the world," of <margin>John 12:26</margin> whom Christ speaks thus: "If the world hates you, know that it hated me before you; if you were of the world, the world would love its own. Because you are not of the world, but I choose you out of the world, therefore the world hates you," etc. "They will do all these things to you because of my name." <margin>John 17:14</margin> And elsewhere: "I have given them your word, and the world has hated them, because they are not of the world, just as I am not of the world." And elsewhere: <margin>John 17:25</margin> "Righteous Father, the world has not known you, but I have known you, and these have known that you sent me, and I have made your name known to them," etc. Of this kind (I say) of worldly ones are Aristotle and all the Heathens <note>Original: "Ethnici." Fludd refers to non-Christian philosophers whose wisdom is based solely on reason and the physical world without divine revelation.</note> with their Christian disciples—namely, those who firmly adhere to the philosophy of the Heathens, inasmuch as they occupy themselves with the husk and the letter, and not in the center of the kernel. To these (I say), who are ignorant of and reject the word or divine name offered by Christ, and have embraced darkness or shadow rather than the true Light, <margin>John 3:19</margin> according to that saying of the Savior: "Light came into the world, and men loved darkness more than light, for their works were evil; for everyone who does evil hates the light, and does not come to the Light," etc. By which it is clearly indicated to the children of God that whoever enters the right way will arrive at the Light and will have the fruit of their works and labors, which the Evangelist also testifies in these following words: "He who does the truth comes to the Light so that his works may be made manifest, because they are done by God." Wherefore, the works of the children of the world are most opposite to those of the children of God, since the former are feigned, crooked, and merely imaginary—and thus they wander and will wander—while the latter are true, a golden line, and surely directing toward that luminous blessedness. The former [works] are discerned by the wise to be concerned with darkness and the circumference; the latter with the Light dwelling in the center of the creature. And this is the reason why the children of the world allow themselves so easily to fall daily into the worst kind of Idolatry—namely, by assigning to creatures those things which are to be ascribed to God alone, whence they are accustomed to call upon and venerate the devil and darkness rather than God and His true and luminous emanation. But let us return to the matter.

Concerning this kind of activity of the divine sun in these lower regions, David speaks most clearly to us: <margin>Ps. 135.</margin> "God, illuminating wonderfully from the eternal mountains, does whatever He wills in <margin>Ps. 113:5</margin> the heavens and on earth, through the seas, and through all the abysses, making vapors ascend from the ends of the earth; He makes lightnings with rain, and brings forth the wind from his treasures," etc. "God (says Jehovah) dwelling most high, looks down upon the lowest things in the heavens and on the earth," etc., where by "heavens" he understands the aerial regions, as is held in many places of the sacred scriptures. Thence he declares that God effects all meteors <note>In the 17th century, "meteors" referred to any atmospheric phenomenon, including rain, wind, and lightning, not just falling stars.</note> in the air, as is read scattered throughout the said volume of the Holy Bible. Do we not also observe the sun itself—the internal [spirit] of the visible sun—seizing and moving the universal spirit, or the air, or the worldly air by its life-giving act, just as that life-giving soul in man (whose vessel is the **heart** itself) [moves] the entire spirit of man, which afterwards performs its duty in the members of the body according to the judgment of the divine will or the first mover? But we also have this <margin>Eccl. 1:5</margin> confirmed by the testimony of Solomon himself, which is as follows: "The Sun rises and, after it has set, pants back to its place where it may rise," etc. By this speech he denotes that the sun, led by a certain internal soul, has received life and rational, regular motion, and that by means of that [soul] it has drawn out the function assigned to it by the creating spirit during creation. <margin>Ps. 19:5</margin> Whence also the royal Psalmist: "The Sun, like a bridegroom coming out of his chamber, <margin>Ps. 104:19</margin> has rejoiced as a Giant to run his course; his going forth is from the highest heaven, <margin>Ps. 148.</margin> and his circuit to its height, nor is there anyone who can hide himself from his heat." And elsewhere he says that the Sun "knew its setting"; similarly in another place, he says that the "Sun and moon praise the Lord, and all the stars and lights." By which it may be seen that not only is there life in the sun, implanted in it by that divine sun who placed his tabernacle in it, but even a certain rational instinct is in it to manifest the praises of its creator to the world; which [is] also in the other stars, and indeed in all

Fludd uses various Psalms and Ecclesiastes to demonstrate that the sun possesses an internal soul that follows the will of the Creator.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the argument that the physical world is not a dead machine but is filled with a living, divine spirit. Fludd specifically focuses on the Sun as the "heart of the world" and uses a series of biblical citations to prove that even supposedly "inanimate" things like weather, water, and stars possess life, sensation, and a form of reason.</meta>

### Of the Struggle, Book II

...must be understood regarding other creatures, as will be discussed more extensively below. Since it has been most clearly demonstrated from these points that both life and splendor reside in the sun, as if in the heart of the world, in the center of which it has its site and seat. Let us now see by what testimony of sacred scripture we can indicate and prove that the sun governs, agitates, moves, and gives life to the worldly spirit <note>original: "spiritum mundanum." This refers to the "Soul of the World" (Anima Mundi), a vital force Fludd believed connected the physical world to the divine.</note>, no differently than the human heart moves that microcosmic spirit <note>The "microcosmic spirit" is the vital force within a human being (the "microcosm" or "little world") that mirrors the great spirit of the universe.</note>. Indeed, we gather this from the consequence of the aforementioned text of Solomon, for he proceeds thus in the following verse: <margin>Ecclesiastes 1:6</margin> *The sun hastens to the south, and goes around to the North; in its circuit, the air <note>original: "aer." In some translations of this passage, this refers to the wind.</note> hastens, going around, and according to its circuits the air returns* (as Tremellius interprets it). From this, it seems declared that the life and action agitating and moving the worldly spirit come from the Sun. Furthermore, in the following verse, he seems to recognize the life or soul of the waters and rivers: *All rivers (he says) hasten to the sea; from the place whence the rivers go out, they return to the sea, etc.* In this thread of discourse, he primarily denotes that natural motion is excited in the waters by an internal soul, and consequently they are imbued with a certain divine soul, by means of which a natural order was assigned to them in creation. Furthermore, it is written in another place that <margin>Psalm 77:17</margin> the waters saw Jehovah and feared. Finally, the holy page confirms that every member of the world is not only called a "living soul" by which it is said to live its life, but is also imbued with a certain divinely inspired sense, even if this seems to be beyond the grasp of common men. For in one place we find it written that <margin>Psalm 67</margin> *the heavens and earth praise God, the sea, and all creeping things.* And elsewhere: <margin>Psalm 148:4</margin> *let the heavens rejoice, let the earth exult, let the sea be moved, etc.* And elsewhere: *the heaven of heavens, the abyss, and the entire earth shall be moved in the sight of God and shall be shaken with trembling.* And elsewhere: <margin>Daniel 3</margin> *Let the heavens bless the Lord:* And Jeremiah says: <margin>Jeremiah 2</margin> *Be astonished, O heavens, at this.* And elsewhere: <margin>Isaiah 1</margin> *Hear, O heavens, and perceive with your ears, O earth, for the Lord has spoken.* If there were no <margin>Joshua 10</margin> sense in the celestial sun, how vain and improper would be that speech of Joshua: *Sun, stand <margin>Psalm 148:3</margin> against Gibeon, and do not move.* Furthermore, let the sun, moon, and all the stars (says David) *praise <margin>Baruch 6</margin> the Lord.* Likewise, in another place, it is said that *the clouds, commanded to permeate the whole world, obey His mandate. At the same time, the lightning and the wind do what is <margin>Job 38:39</margin> commanded of them.* And Job says, *Wisdom sends forth the lightnings, and as they return they say, "Here we are."* Furthermore, it would be vain and empty for the three Israelite children, liberated from the fiery furnace by divine power, to have uttered these words if there were no life, soul, or even reason in all the works of God, since they sang these things: <margin>Daniel 3</margin>

> Bless the Lord, all you works: praise and highly exalt him forever. Bless him, heavens; bless him, all you waters that are above the firmament; bless him, sun, moon, stars, rain, dew, fire, heat, cold, frost, ice, snows, nights, days, light, darkness, lightnings, clouds; earth, mountains, hills, all things germinating in the earth, springs, seas, rivers, whales, fish, birds, beasts, and men; praise and exalt him forever, etc.

By this speech, he seems to argue that the power and virtue of praising and blessing the Creator is not in man alone, but also in every other creature, although in common philosophy <note>original: "philosophia vulgari." Fludd often uses this term to disparage the mainstream Aristotelian science of his day, which he felt was too materialistic.</note> that foolish distinction is produced between the true sense and reason of creatures and that which is metaphorical. For from these things recited here, it is indicated more clearly than light itself that every creature has life and sense, indeed even reason and consequently its own proper soul, even if this is not so manifest to the common understanding, but hidden. This is explained even more clearly because after he introduces all creatures—namely the universal heavens, the waters and the earth, as well as particular ones such as the sun, moon, stars, weather, animals, and all plants—he finally introduces man himself, arguing from this that other natures, though they seem inanimate to us, are nevertheless imbued with a soul suitable to their species no less than man himself. Finally, remember these words brought forth by the Wise Man: *How desirable are all his works, and like a spark which is to be considered: All these live,*

He supports this by citing biblical passages where inanimate objects are commanded to obey, fear, or praise God, concluding that the entire cosmos is a living, sentient being.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Robert Fludd’s defense of the "Soul of the World" (Anima Mundi). Following the previous page’s argument that the Sun acts as the heart of the universe, Fludd now uses biblical authority and logical deduction to prove that the entire universe is a living organism rather than a collection of dead matter. He specifically targets the "mechanical" philosophy of his contemporary rival, Marin Mersenne.</meta>

# Of the Contest of Wisdom with Folly

<margin>Ecclesiasticus 42:23.</margin> "and they remain forever, and obey Him in every necessity." Here, he <note>Referring to the author of the biblical book of Ecclesiasticus.</note> seems not only to assign life to all creatures, but also to impart the spark of life by which they live. Since this is the case, what is to stop the entire world from claiming its own soul? Especially since all its parts are endowed with distinct souls derived from a single life-giving source. And this is particularly true since the "whole" is nothing other than what is integrated and forged from all its parts. Therefore, it necessarily follows that the members of the *microcosm* <term>microcosm: "the little world," or the human being, seen as a reflection of the entire universe.</term> require only a single soul, which is recognized by every upright philosopher to be in the whole and in every part. Hence, it is probable (if we observe the scripture rightly) that the divine soul, which is said to fill the world, is in its entirety and in every part of it. In the whole, I say, insofar as <margin>Wisdom 1.</margin> *the holy spirit of discipline is said to be everywhere and to fill all things.* In every part, insofar as <margin>Wisdom 12:1.</margin> that incorruptible spirit of God is in all things: <margin>Acts 17; 1 Corinthians 12; 1 Timothy 6.</margin> *giving life to all, and inspiration, and all things; working all things in all; and finally, giving life to everything,* as was declared above. For this was that *spirit by means of which all things were created, produced, and illuminated by the ray of life and existence.* <margin>Judith 16.</margin> "You sent," says Judith, "your spirit, and every creature was created." <margin>Romans 11.</margin> Finally, I conclude with the Apostle: *From Him, through Him, and in Him are all things; to whom be honor and glory forever.*

Does not the resolution of the aforementioned question shine clearer than the midday sun? Namely, that against the opinion of Marin Mersenne <note>Marin Mersenne (1588–1648) was a French priest and mathematician who criticized Fludd's mystical ideas, preferring a more mechanical, mathematical explanation of the universe.</note>, the "Soul of the World" <term>Soul of the World: known in Latin as the *Anima Mundi*, this is the ancient idea that the entire universe is a single, living organism animated by a divine spirit.</term> is certain and infallible. If not, then it is worthy of wonder and deep inquiry: from whence do so many changes of the elements, the regular motions of the stars, the courses of waters, and the ebb and flow of the seas arise? From where come the power of the air and winds, the agility and action of flaming fire, the alteration of seasons, the multiplication of plants, and infinite other effects in the *macrocosm* <term>macrocosm: "the great world," or the entire physical universe.</term>? If anyone should say—be it Marin [Mersenne] or someone else of his sect, along with the Stoics and Peripatetics <note>The Stoics and the Peripatetics (followers of Aristotle) were ancient philosophical schools. Fludd argues against those who attribute natural phenomena to lesser gods or "angels" rather than the single divine Soul.</note>—that these things are done by Angels or I-know-not-what celestial or earthly gods, I would have them remember these words of the Apostle: <margin>1 Corinthians 8.</margin> *There are indeed gods, as they are called in heaven and on earth; yet for us there is one God, the Father, from whom are all things, and we in Him; and one Lord Jesus Christ, through whom are all things, and we through Him.* 

But if they respond that all these things are done by God through "ordinary reason" and not by a Soul of the World, we concede that all these things are done by God and not otherwise. However, we utterly deny that there is therefore no Soul of the World through which these things are carried out in the world. Instead, we state steadfastly, because of the reasons we have alleged above, that God Himself—clothed in light or spirit illuminated by His presence as if in a garment—is indeed the Soul of the World. Since He fills the cavity of the whole universe, He is said to give life to all and to provide life and inspiration to all. Hence, this Wisdom, or this emanation from the fountain of light, is called the fountain of life <margin>John 1; Ecclesiasticus 1; Psalm 36:9.</margin>, the tree of life, etc. *In the Word,* says Saint John, *was life,* by means of which the world and all things in the world were made. *The Word,* says the Wise Man, *is the fountain of eternal wisdom,* of which it is said elsewhere, *the fountain of the spirit of life is with you.* From these, we can see clearly enough <margin>Ecclesiasticus 6.</margin> that this holy emanation is most fertile and full of life-giving power; wherefore it is written of it: *In wisdom is the beauty of life.* Consequently, it is that life-giving Spirit, animating and giving form to all things in the world, which the Apostle understands when he says "God gives life to the soul." This, therefore, is the uncreated soul of the world, which placed its tabernacle in the created sun—as if in the heart of the world—from which (as has already been said) it looks benevolently both upward and downward with rays most full of life. Hence it is not ineptly said that this divine spirit is a "center of every thing, whose circumference is nowhere" <note>This is a famous Hermetic definition of God, suggesting He is present in the tiniest point of every object while remaining infinite.</note>; that is, it is centrally within all things to grant them life and existence, and nonetheless it is outside all things, insofar as even the *archetypal world* <term>archetypal world: the "blueprint" of the universe in the mind of God, which contains the perfect forms of everything that exists.</term> is most full of His glory <margin>Psalm 30:6.</margin>. And this is the reason why the Royal Prophet <note>King David, the traditional author of the Psalms.</note> so often made mention of this gift of His life-giving benevolence in his hymns...

He challenges the mechanical views of Marin Mersenne, asserting that natural phenomena like the tides and the stars are driven by this all-pervading holy spirit centered in the Sun.</summary>

Let His face shine upon His servant; may He save me by His kindness. Whomever God visits with His salvation shall enjoy good: when He gives to the creatures, they gather; when He opens His hand, they are satisfied with good; when He hides His face, they are troubled; when He takes back their spirit, they expire:* finally, speaking of this spiritual Sun, he says: *Jehovah God is the Sun giving grace and glory:* And Job says: *Your visitation preserves my spirit.* This can also be most aptly understood as the spirit of the world, as is explained by the content of verse 17 of Psalm 31, previously recited.

What does Marinus <note>Marin Mersenne (1588–1648), a French philosopher and mathematician who famously debated Fludd.</note> answer now? Does he still believe that he himself is the soul of the world? Which, since it is uncreated light, animates primarily the created light—that is, the mundane spirit <term>mundane spirit: *spiritus mundanus*, a subtle substance Fludd believed filled the universe as a bridge between God and matter</term> illuminated by the presence of that spirit. And thus it is said that light was created—that is, informed by the appearance and emanation of that divine and luminous spirit and adorned with splendor: whence it is said,

Nevertheless, in her own proper nature, she is a simple and by no means created spirit, for she is from the spirit of Jehovah, and was always with Him and existed before the ages, as the said Sage testifies. And it is for this reason that in many places in the Scriptures she is called an *angel*, which is a thing composed of the created spirit as its body, and the divine and uncreated light as its life-giving soul. Therefore, Dionysius <note>Pseudo-Dionysius the Areopagite, an influential mystical theologian.</note> calls the angels *Agalmata* <note>Greek: "statues" or "images," here meaning "clear mirrors."</note>, that is, the clearest mirrors, immediately receiving the Light of God and representing His image, as he himself testifies in the book *On the Divine Names*. Hence it is that Augustine, in his commentary on Genesis, affirms in express words that *demons are airy and fiery living beings: airy according to their external part, and fiery, or luminous, according to their internal part*. For the formal essence of such beings is nothing other than the light of God, as Dionysius testifies. And we say, from the doctrine of the Holy Scriptures, that this light is the emanation of the splendor of the Almighty, which is the holy spirit of wisdom or discipline itself. This, therefore, is the reason that the Angel appearing to Moses in the bush is sometimes called an "Angel" in that same chapter—namely, in respect to his created portion, which is his spiritual exterior—and sometimes "Jehovah," in respect to the informing interior, which is the divine Word or that emanation leaping forth from the true majesty of God. # On Wisdom with Folly
<meta>catchword: sent</meta>

<margin>Wisdom 10:17.</margin>
...[having been] sent, then its vehicle which is a misty spirit, by means of which it remained in a column of cloud. And elsewhere it is said: *Wisdom was a covering for them by day, and a light of stars by night, etc.* By which the Wise man seems to indicate that this uncreated spirit, within that created angelic spirit, was surely that Angel who was the Leader of the Israelites. This figure was sometimes called an "angel" for the reason related above, and sometimes—namely with respect to itself alone and in the simplicity of its divinity—"Jehovah" and "wisdom proceeding from the mouth of the Most High." It is as if one had said that the uncreated emanation was within that spirit in place of an essential form for the complete and perfect composition of the angelic creature. Even the leader 
<margin>Exodus 23.</margin>
Moses seems to verify this by the thread of this kind of discourse. "Behold," says Jehovah, "*I send my angel before you to keep you in this way... do not provoke him, for he will not bear your rebellion, because my name is in him, etc.*" Where by *my name* is argued the Word of Jehovah God, which is the fountain of wisdom itself, as was declared before. 

By plainly the same reasoning, it is explained what is the primary agent in the creation of the mundane spirit <term>mundane spirit: *spiritus mundanus*, the subtle, vital substance Fludd believes permeates the universe as a bridge between the physical and divine</term>, namely the Word or the bright wisdom proceeding from the mouth of Jehovah; and what is that immediate "patient" <note>In traditional philosophy, the "patient" is the thing that receives the action of an "agent."</note> or created vehicle, namely the air or waters of the entire world, both spiritual and gross, from which the heavens, the earth, and the seas are made. This occurs while that Word, which fills that entire mundane spirit, is present and operating, from which both heaven 
<margin>2 Peter 3.</margin>
and earth are forged together. This is as Moses testifies when he says that *the spirit of the Lord was moved over the waters*, from which then afterwards heaven and earth, the seas, and celestial and terrestrial bodies were made. But this is explained even more clearly from those words of Saint Peter, so often repeated: *The heavens were of old, and the earth consisting out of water and through water by the word of God.* That is, by the presence and power of divine Wisdom. 

From this, therefore, we say that the emanation leaping from that bright fountain into the formless waters of the world rendered them formed and most beautiful to the sight. And because the entire mundane spirit is specified in such an angelic manner by the divine word, the Cabalists <note>Jewish mystical tradition which Fludd frequently synthesized with Christian thought.</note> called it—thus enriched with divine light—that great angel *Metatron* <note>original: "Mitattron"</note>, and the Poets fashioned it as the god *Pan*. The Philosophers called it *universal nature fabricating all things*; the Platonists the *soul of the world* <term>soul of the world: *anima mundi*</term>, which is nothing other than God, or divine wisdom, and uncreated in an illuminated and created spirit, clothed as if with a garment. 

This was the first work of God’s creation, and this was that subtle and celestial composition from which the earthly creatures of the world were then afterwards animated and shaped. For this reason, the sacred writings say that heaven is the principal seat of divine majesty, but the earth is his footstool. On the second day of creation, this illuminated spirit, created on the first day, was contracted into the solar substance of heaven. Here, it is said secondly that this divinity put on a garment, for it, with the spirit carrying it, is said on the fourth day to have placed its tabernacle in the sun; and so that angelic or empyrean nature descended into the celestial nature. 

Then in the work of the third day, this divine light was clothed with a third garment, for 
<margin>
Job 22:15.
Psalm 104.
Psalm 105.
1 Chronicles 6.
Ecclesiasticus 24.
</margin>
*He made a thick cloud His booth*, or as is held elsewhere, *His hiding place*, and elsewhere *His vehicle*, and elsewhere it is said, *He spread thick clouds for a covering:* Indeed, Jehovah also said that He dwells in thick darkness. Do we not also have all this explained in effect by the writer of Ecclesiasticus? *I came out of the mouth of the Most High as a mist covering the earth, and I had my tent in the High places, I alone compassed the circuit of the heavens, and I walked in the depth of the abyss, in the waves of the sea and in all the earth, etc.* 

Here therefore you see how wisdom is the central agent of the mundane soul—or rather the mundane spirit—and consequently its principal life. It is ordained for the existence, preservation, and multiplication of life, both in the great world <note>the macrocosm or universe</note> and the small world <note>the microcosm or human being</note>. About this, 
<margin>Hymn 1. 19.</margin>
Synesius <note>Synesius of Cyrene (c. 373–414 AD), a Neoplatonist philosopher and bishop.</note> sang in his first hymn: *A mind fallen into matter <note>original: "hylam", from the Greek hyle</note>, an immortal mind, the offspring of divine parents—small indeed, but entirely theirs, and everywhere one, wholly infused into the whole—it turns the vast cavity of the heavens, preserving this universe, into various for-*

<vocab>Wisdom, Jehovah, Metatron, Pan, Platonists, soul of the world, Synesius, matter, Metatron</vocab>

<summary>Fludd argues that Divine Wisdom (Sophia) acts as the animating force of the universe, identifying it with the Biblical 'Angel of the Lord,' the Cabalistic Metatron, and the 'Soul of the World' of the Platonists. He traces the process of creation through the 'clothing' of this divine light in progressively denser spiritual and physical garments, from the empyrean to the sun and finally to the clouds and earth.</summary>

# Concerning the Contest, Book II.
**g 2**

distributed is that part of the stars' course, part the assembly of angels; part even, by a heavy bond, has obtained an earthly form and, separated from its parents, has drunk in dark oblivion, etc. But Hermes Trismegistus <note>original: "Mercurius Trismegistus," the legendary Hellenistic figure to whom many hermetic and alchemical texts were attributed</note> best describes the garments of this divine mind, 
<margin>Pimander 10.</margin>
insofar as it exists within man: *The garment (he says) of the mind is the soul, just as the garment of the soul is the spirit; thus the celestial soul is the vehicle of the empyrean light <term>empyrean: the highest, fiery heaven in ancient cosmology, believed to be the home of pure light and God</term>, just as that elemental or airy spirit is the vehicle of the celestial soul, in the middle of which the divine mind itself is situated:* thus he also says: *The human soul is carried in this manner: Mind in reason, reason in soul, soul in spirit, spirit in body.* By these words it seems clearly revealed to the wise man in what way, and with what garments, that naked and simple divine mind is covered, both in the macrocosm <term>macrocosm: the great world or universe</term> and the microcosm <term>microcosm: the "little world," specifically the human being as a reflection of the universe</term>, by means of which the power of life and splendor is ascribed to them. Consequently, it is permitted to perceive what that central acting foundation might be, not only in both worlds, but indeed in any thing contained within them, according to the sense of the Holy Scriptures: as if one were to say that the divine mind or uncreated wisdom was immersed in the empyrean or rational spirit; that the rational spirit, adorned with that divine gift, was immersed in the ethereal or vital spirit; and the vital spirit with that rational one (in which wisdom sits as the first mover, as if in its own more worthy vehicle) was immersed in the elemental; and finally, that ethereal or vital spirit with that elemental or natural spirit—which is the sublunary air—was immersed in the earth or body. 

From this we see that the more profoundly this divine mind immerses itself into the abyss of created matter, descending from its bright source, the more it recedes from unity into the depth of multiplicity. And this is the foundation of the harmony of our two worlds and the principle for dividing the tones on the mundane monochord <term>mundane monochord: a symbolic single-stringed instrument representing the universe, where Fludd mapped the intervals of music onto the distances between earth and heaven</term>, and for reducing them into consonances: since, through that proportion of the divine spirit descending into the mundane multitude, we see different things produced at every moment and a different order produced, both in heaven and on earth. This is done either in a symphonic manner for the happy constitution of the world disposed by our kind God, or in an antipathetic <note>meaning "discordant" or "opposing"</note> manner for the punishment of men in the world by our angry God, produced as much in the beginning as annually—indeed, even on any day and moment. To this assertion of ours, the page of all truth seems to agree everywhere. And this music of the world, produced in its cavity by that unique life-giving author, is clearly indicated by these words of the Wise Man: *You have ordered (or tempered) all things by measure, number, and weight.* <note>A famous quote from the Wisdom of Solomon 11:20, central to medieval and Renaissance science.</note> Therefore, by means of the act or pulsing of that eternal Musician, 
<margin>Job 26.</margin>
*first the North is stretched out over the empty and void space, and the earth was suspended over nothing; the waters are bound up in thick clouds; the heavens are established, they are numbered and rotated in a circuit; the dust descending from heaven thickens into hardness and the clods adhere to one another; the waters are separated from the waters by an intervening spirit distinguishing the moist and segregating it from the dry.* 
<margin>Proverbs 3, 4.</margin>
*A weight is fitted to the air, and the waters are weighed out in measure; the decrees for the rain are made, and a way for the lightning of the thunders; the jars of heaven are poured out; and finally all things, composed most exactly by divine harmony from number, weight, and measure, are ordered in a marvelous way, disposed, and brought to the complete goal of perfection: for God acted through wisdom, and saw that his deeds were good and brought into the light with admirable sympathy:* which also the royal Psalmist seems to confirm thus: 
<margin>Psalm 103.</margin>
*The works of God (he says) are wonderful and great, all of which you have made in wisdom.* 

Therefore, these things being well considered, a response is given to the boastful objection of Mersenne <note>Marin Mersenne (1588–1648), a French mathematician and philosopher who famously feuded with Fludd over the validity of "Hermetic" science</note> himself: by which he seems to challenge all the philosophers of the world to respond directly as to what is the true and genuine form of the Elements, or their "first act." He posed this as if he had proposed a thing impossible for them to answer. And certainly, to men nurtured on pagan philosophy and least versed in those sacred matters, it will seem hard to answer this question: for they have hardly comprehended the true, unique, and simple author of the world—namely, eternal Wisdom—so much are they entangled and ruined by the absurdities of the pagans. But one truly versed in true and truly Christian Philosophy will respond without a roll of the dice <note>meaning "without hesitation" or "certainly"</note> that the wisdom of God (whose fountain is the Word of God) is the true form, the first act, the life, or the unique and sole soul of the Elements and the entire universe: inasmuch as she is the Spirit of the Lord, who was carried over the waters, imparting to them a fiery and life-giving power and vigor; giving life and inspiration and...

He concludes by rebuking Marin Mersenne, asserting that Divine Wisdom is the "true form" and animating soul of the physical elements.</summary>

### On Wisdom against Folly

...all things, working all in all, moving and filling all things by His activity, etc.

Why then is this Wisdom not the unique and sole form of the elements? But if Mersenne, with an impudence hitherto unheard of, still denies that this shining Wisdom is the actualizing principle <term>actum: in scholastic philosophy, the "act" or "actualization" of a thing's potential</term> of the elements, behold, he has the Holy Scriptures speaking for me, to his own scandal and disgrace. And if these things are not enough for him, let us proceed to the very marrow of the matter through true examination. Mersenne will not deny that God is the Father of Lights and the *Act of acts* <note>original: "actus actuum," a philosophical term for the ultimate cause of all action</note>. Yet even the Philosophers acknowledge that the celestial lights act, that aerial bolts and lightning operate, and that terrestrial fire moves by altering things; if this is so, what can prevent God from acting immediately and personally in the stars, in lightning, and in fire—mediated by the worldly air, in the empyrean spirit <term>empyrean: the highest heaven of pure light</term>, and in that ethereal, aerial, and earthly [spirit]? 

Truly, it cannot be otherwise, since God is the *Act of acts*, and consequently the unique principle of all operations. Otherwise, it would follow that there were two active principles, and consequently, it would be necessary for Unity to be excluded from the art of Arithmetic. But this seems impossible even to a fool; therefore, we proceed thus: If God is the fountain of acts and the origin of light, then all action and light proceed from Him immediately. The reason is readily available: namely, because the divine essence is one, and consequently individual <note>meaning "undivided" or "inseparable"</note>. Therefore, such acts proceeding from that light or emanation—namely, lights both empyrean (which are called angelic) and ethereal or stellar, and even those fiery ones in their formal and original aspect—differ no less from their source than solar rays differ from the sun. Hence the Apostle says: *God works all things in all. But God is one.* <note>1 Corinthians 12:6</note>

Truly, indeed, I wish that Marinus <note>referring to Marin Mersenne</note> would be guided by experience, the mother of fools, so that he might see with open eyes that stars act in the heavens, move, and operate on lower things by their influence; that comets, lightning, chasms, "leaping goats" <note>original: "capras saltantes," an early modern meteorological term for a type of streaking or flickering atmospheric light</note>, coruscations, and many other meteors visibly move and act, emitting both malevolent and favorable effects onto these lower realms. He would see fire burn upon the earth, and in its burning visibly operate, move, and have a destructive operation, such that great bodies of trees and masses of terrestrial coal are seen to be reduced to almost nothing. Indeed, a single spark can consume a great city. 

Therefore, in fire expanded everywhere through the air, we perceive a power that is active, shining, motive, and even multiplicative once it is contracted into a visible form. Nor does this shining power exercise its authority in only one place—namely, in **England**, **Germany**, **France**, **Spain**, or **Italy** alone—but also in **America**, **Asia**, and everywhere across the globe, it does not cease to declare its strength and manifest its experience to the world. By these things we are clearly taught that this light or fire, spread invisibly through the air by expansion, sometimes contracts into a visible form, much like subtle and invisible air which is compressed into a visible mist or cloud. This expanded power, thus united or contracted, shows us not only a visible act or specimen of invisible fiery power, but also indicates its infinite capacity and virtue in moving, acting, and refining matter. We see that from one candle, infinite others can be lit, and once lit, we observe them moving and acting. 

But truly, these things draw their power of life, motion, and action from the air. Since this is proven by experience, remember that God Himself is the *Act of acts*, the *Light of lights*, and consequently, He fills all things in His own person, operating immediately in the fire, stars, lightning, and all things. Consequently, the world itself—in which these lights have their life—acts, moves, and lives centrally in God and through God. For the incorruptible Spirit of God, existing centrally in every thing according to the saying of the Wise Man <note>A reference to the Book of Wisdom 7:24, "For wisdom is more moving than any motion: she passeth and goeth through all things by reason of her pureness."</note>, acts from the center of a thing all the way to its circumference. By His act, which is voluntary, He impels creatures (who are dead in themselves) with an involuntary motion; hence, the creature is said by the ignorant to "move," when in truth it is moved by that internal principle. 

From these things, therefore, it is clear that the world is endowed with its own soul, by which alone all creatures and the world itself are animated and quickened. All things in this world are arranged in a proportional harmony, whose acts or tones and consonances—though [ascended] through a geometric pyramid to the disposition of the created worldly spirit in its thinness...

<meta>catchword: and denseness</meta>

<summary>Fludd argues that God is the "Act of acts" and the immediate source of all motion and light in the universe, refuting the idea of independent secondary causes. He asserts that the world is a living organism (Anima Mundi) animated by the Divine Spirit, which acts from the center of all things to their circumference.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Robert Fludd’s vigorous defense of his mystical philosophy against the criticisms of the French priest and mathematician Marin Mersenne. # On the Contest, Book II.

And as we have demonstrated through density, we ought to draw from this the various differences in weight and lightness—or thickness and thinness—between the three regions of the world. These arise from the diverse acts of Divine Wisdom acting in various ways upon those material regions through a **formal pyramid** <term>formal pyramid: in Fludd's philosophy, a triangle representing the descent of divine light or spirit into the world</term> descending in numerical progression, which balances itself against an ascending **material pyramid** <term>material pyramid: a triangle representing the ascent of gross matter or darkness upward from the center of the earth</term>. In the ascent and descent of these pyramids, an admirable harmony is described in a perpetual, spiritual, and sacred chart; for by this means, creatures of contrary natures are united in a concordant peace. By this logic, all things are compelled to look toward the unity of the Creator and to revere Him through a certain wonderful instinct. This is clearly explained by these words of the Wise Man: <margin>Wisdom 17:19</margin> *For as the elements, in changing their order one with another, are like the notes on a musical instrument, where the sound changes its quality, yet all things keep their own sound,* etc. <note>original: "In se elementa dum convertuntur, sicut in organo qualitatis sonus immutatur..." This refers to Wisdom of Solomon 19:18 in modern Bibles, describing how the elements of nature rearranged themselves during the Exodus.</note> And the reason for the passivity of worldly matter (which always becomes thinner as it rises) and the proportional action of the Divine Form animating all things—plunging into that receptive matter from above, as if from its own super-celestial fatherland, into the multitude below—is the cause of the various dispositions and degrees of the elements and celestial spheres among themselves. We have expressed this most aptly through the formal and material pyramids, as far as our intellect can grasp, and it has been explained by us more clearly and at greater length in our works on both the Macrocosm and the Microcosm, sufficiently confounding the empty quibbles of Mersenne.

But now I shall turn the pen of my discourse back to that same Marin Mersenne, whose obstinacy in this dispute we may wonder at not a little. While he acknowledges the soul of the **Microcosm** <term>Microcosm: "Little World," a common Renaissance term for a human being</term> or man, he does not blush to deny that life and a soul—distinct from God, the greatest architect of all—were granted to the **Macrocosm** <term>Macrocosm: "Great World," referring to the entire universe</term> at its creation. But if a soul were not granted to the world by the Creator, why is man called a "Microcosm" by this monk, if not because of his similarity and relation to the Macrocosm? Yet the Microcosm (as he himself agrees) is clothed in an eternal soul. Why then is the Macrocosm not also, if it is entirely similar to the Microcosm? <margin>Column 1739.</margin> Marin testifies that man is called a microcosm. First, because he contains all levels of worldly nature within himself, insofar as he has a relation with the elements (from which his external part is composed); similarly, he shares "mixture" with stones, life with plants, sense with animals, and reason with angels. Second, because he is divided into three regions in the manner of the world: namely, into the intellectual or angelic region; the vital or celestial region in which the Sun rules; and the elementary region, whose fertility in procreating, increasing, and nourishing animals and plants is marvelous. Furthermore, <margin>Columns 1745 & 1746.</margin> he proves that all the meteors of the world are to be found in the human body. Similarly, he placed—or thought there should be placed—five zones in man, hardly dissimilar in nature and condition to those of the macrocosm. And although he stole all of this from my work on the Microcosm (which he so unworthily reviled) with only a few words changed—as we shall demonstrate *below*—and conformed entirely to that opinion in this matter, his inconsistency is nevertheless admirable. For he assigns a living soul to the Microcosm, yet it pleases him to admit or ascribe none to the Macrocosm; nonetheless, he is eager to assign rational, vital, and elementary "heavens" to both. Will he allege on his own behalf that God, on the sixth day of creation, breathed the breath of life into Adam’s face, which He did not wish to grant to the world? Oh, what a most wretched excuse! As if God had not inspired the world with the spirit of life, had not formed it by His own breath, and had not animated it with divine life, when the Spirit of the Lord is said to be carried over the waters, imparting a fiery vigor to them, as Augustine testifies. And what else (I pray) is animation but the concealment or placement of divine light or fire within the watery spirit? Does not the royal Psalmist seem to argue this very thing in these words: <note>referring to King David</note> *By the Word of the Lord (he says) were the heavens made firm, and all their power by the spirit of His mouth?* If, therefore, all their power is from the spirit of His mouth, then so is their soul or life, which is the chief and most essence-forming power of any thing; for the nature *of the Spirit of God is to give life to all things.* <margin>Wisdom 1 & 2 Peter 3.</margin> So also Saint Peter says in the place we have often cited: *The heavens were of old, and the earth out of the water,* through the water by the assistance of the Word: where by "the Word" he denotes the Spirit of Wisdom which proceeded from the mouth of the Most High, of which David spoke in the place cited before: *By the spirit of His mouth all their power...*

<sig>g 3</sig>
<meta>catchword: rum;</meta>

<summary>Fludd argues for the existence of a World Soul (Anima Mundi) by pointing out Mersenne's logical inconsistency: Mersenne calls man a "Microcosm" (Little World) and grants him a soul, yet denies a soul to the "Macrocosm" (Great World) despite their perfect structural similarity. Fludd uses his theory of formal and material pyramids and biblical citations to show that God's Spirit animates the entire universe.</summary>

# On the Conflict of Wisdom with Folly
<meta>catchword: ver-</meta>

...truth: where also through virtue, he understands every form, whether mineral and vegetable or animal, and even elementary: for by the mediation of that unique spirit blown or breathed into the world, both the world and the creatures produced within the world were endowed with life; and consequently the world itself was animated by that unique spirit, from which man is said to have received his life and being.

<margin>Judith 16.</margin>
But we have this most excellently explained in the Book of Judith: *Let every creature serve God, because you spoke, and they were made; you sent your spirit and they were created, etc.* <note>original: "Deo seruiat omnis creatura, quia dixisti, & factæ sunt, misisti spiritum tuum & creati sunt." This is Judith 16:17 in the Vulgate.</note> This is a general statement, and is understood to apply to every creature; from this it is argued that not only man, but every other creature—and especially the world, which is the great parent or mother of creatures—was created through the mediation of the breath and spirit sent forth by God. I say the world especially, insofar as it was first formed in the divine likeness; for its "heaven of heavens" is related to the Father in the Trinity, and is ascribed to Him by Holy Scripture as His seat—*The heaven of heavens,* says the Prophet, *is his seat*—that is, the intellectual heaven. The ethereal heaven is given to the Son, or the emanation from the Father, since He *placed his tabernacle in the sun*; He is the distributor of life and the fountain of life, which is why this heaven is called the vital region of the world. Finally, the elementary region is referred to the Spirit proceeding from the Father and the Son, insofar as it was created after the heavens or superior regions, and receives force and virtue from them. For we observe from the effects that the elementary world abounds with spirits and forms, and is daily filled with new actions, with the Spirit itself assisting and acting—nay, truly, we perceive the Spirit itself "blowing where it wills" and producing new things every year. Finally, just as those three persons intend nothing but virtue in a single essence, and that a divine one, so indeed all those three regions constitute but a single world. This world consists of a single created world-spirit no differently than the essence of the Godhead consists of a single uncreated and individual Spirit existing from all eternity.

And as we said above concerning the constitution of an Angel, so also this world-spirit is like a mirror <term>catoptrum: from the Greek 'katoptron', a mirror or reflecting surface</term> in which the divine splendor and the image of the Almighty—indeed, the very icon of the Trinity—is discerned as if in a most highly polished looking-glass. Without the sight of that bright light, this worldly mirror is empty and dark; for the splendid sight of the Godhead itself informs and adorns it, no differently than was said of the corporeal substance of an Angel. Hence, this spirit, so animated by divine light, is called the Angel **Metatron** <term>Metatron: a supreme angel in Jewish Kabbalistic tradition, often viewed as the celestial mediator or the "Prince of the Countenance"</term> by the Cabalists, and the **Soul of the World** <term>anima mundi: the soul or animating principle of the universe in Platonic philosophy</term> by the Platonists. By Zoroaster and Heraclitus of Ephesus, it is called the *invisible fire generating all things*; by Virgil, the *spirit within that nourishes*; <note>original: "spiritus intus alens," from Virgil’s Aeneid, VI.726.</note> and by Synesius and Manilius, the *divine mind*, taking the animating virtue

for the thing animated. By Moses, it is called the *Spirit of the Lord carried over the waters*; and by the Philosophers, *Nature, nourishing and fostering all things*; and *Wisdom*, of which the son of Sirach also says, *Wisdom was created before all things*; and by Solomon, *Wisdom filling all things, the brightness and splendor of the divine majesty*; and by the Royal Prophet [David], *God clothed in light*; and by John: *The Word in the world by whom all things were made*; and by the Apostle: *God working all things in all*, and *the divine nature giving life to all things. And Jesus Christ, through whom God created all things, and we through him. And Christ as God filling all things. And Judith: The Spirit of Jehovah sent into the world, by which all creatures were made. And again in the Apostle: God the Father of all, who is over all and in all of us. And God from whom all things come: He is the one. Finally, that God who, needing nothing, gives to all life and breath and all things,* etc. But if he gives to all, is the world not included? From these things, therefore, you see that peace and reconciliation have been made between the opinions of the Cabalists, the Philosophers, and the divine Poets, and the language of the Holy Bible.

Therefore, we think it is explained clearly enough to readers how falsely so many good men have been condemned and esteemed as nothing by Marin Mersenne. As if, for example, **Robert [Fludd]** ought truly to be considered a **Cacomagus** <term>Cacomagus: a "bad magician" or practitioner of black magic, from the Greek 'kakos' (evil) and 'magos' (magician)</term> simply because Marin, in his dull-witted sense, has judged it so—and for this reason: because he includes the names of angels in his writings. But concerning these things we shall speak more fully below, and, as we hope, more abundantly for the satisfaction of the world. We conclude, therefore, from the premises, that the world is the immediate image of God—or rather a mirror, or reflecting glass, receiving the icon or likeness of God. Whence **Hermes Trismegistus** said not absurdly: *that the world is the image of God, and man...*

<meta>This page continues Robert Fludd’s defense of the "World Soul" (Anima Mundi) against the criticisms of the French scholar Marin Mersenne. Fludd argues that because man is a "microcosm" or small world, the "macrocosm" or large world must also be alive and animated by the divine spirit.</meta>

### On the Conflict, Book II.

...is indeed the image of the world: for just as the world immediately received the impression of its life and being from God reigning in His **archetypal world**, so indeed man received his life and existence immediately from God commanding in the **typical world**. <note>original: "mundo archetypico... mundo typico." Fludd uses these terms to distinguish between the "Archetype" (the divine idea in God's mind) and the "Type" (the physical reflection or universe we inhabit).</note> Therefore, he who confidently desires to assert that the world possesses a soul seems to wander very little from the truth itself. Finally, by this infallible reasoning, we do not hesitate to confirm (in this manner) that if man were not enriched with a soul, there would be no hope of rising again, or of recovering the new form or super-celestial image. For unless there were a life-giving spirit within us, it would be impossible for us to rise from death to life, and from corruption to incorruption (to speak with the Apostle). <note>Fludd refers here to St. Paul's teachings on the resurrection in 1 Corinthians 15.</note> But such a spirit exists within us, for which reason we are called *temples of the Holy Spirit*; <note>original: "templa... Spiritus sancti." From 1 Corinthians 6:19.</note> therefore, through that life-giving spirit, we believe we shall have eternal life, since by its mediation Christ, the Savior of the world (after whose example we have hope of rising from the dead to eternal life), rose and ascended into heaven. But perhaps Marinus <note>Fludd refers to his opponent, Marin Mersenne.</note> will say: "Why, according to the example of man, is this world not also subject to corruption, and destined to be raised to a new state by resurrection, if such a life as that of man is destined for it by the Creator?"

<margin>2 Peter 3.
Revelation.</margin>
We respond that, according to the sense of the Sacred Scriptures, the heavens and the earth must pass away; *the heavens,* they say, *and the earth passed away, and again they were made new*; <note>original: "cæli... & terra transierunt, & iterum noui sunt facti." A reference to 2 Peter 3:13 and Revelation 21:1.</note> and elsewhere: *Behold, I shall make all things new.* <note>original: "Ecce omnia noua faciam." From Revelation 21:5.</note> This indeed could not happen if the divine breath of life were not within the world; and consequently, we see that both the world and man are to be renewed in the final <unclear>Passing</unclear> from a corruptible state to an incorruptible state.

From these things, therefore (O benevolent and just Readers), one may see how false is the doctrine of Mersenne and in how many errors it is entangled. For he, being utterly ignorant of the being and soul of the world, and consequently denying it in a perverse manner, ignorantly attempts to subvert the foundations not only of all sacred philosophy but even of the true Christian religion. He fails to consider that God (whose nature it is to give life to all things) is everywhere in His creatures, disposing, sustaining, and continually moderating, and animating or giving life to all things in the world, as well as the world itself, in a life-giving manner. Therefore, it is no wonder if he condemns so many men of such distinguished authority and holds their opinions as nothing—opinions which are nonetheless found to be true in themselves and in no way disagreeing with the fountain of all truth.

But now (you, Mersenne), who assert and say all things not taught by your own experience, but either by using the tongues of others—having nothing of weight from your own self—claiming for yourself your own defense or shield by saying "he himself said it," <note>original: "ipse dixit." A phrase used to describe an unproven assertion resting solely on an authority's word.</note> or led by uncertain fantasy, you both speak and write by imagining things in your own way: I would have you know now that I have progressed along a surer path in my works. In these, I have used not only the testimonies of other wise men and especially of the Sacred Scriptures, but also vigorous reasons and infallible arguments, and finally <term>ocular demonstrations<gloss>Visible, experimental proofs that can be seen with the eyes, likely referring to Fludd's alchemical or physical experiments.</gloss></term>, clearly indicating the matter down to its very core. Nor shall I boast that I have openly perceived with my eyes the reason for the world's structure through the "unshelling" of creatures and the <term>chemical anatomization<gloss>The process of breaking down substances through alchemy to reveal their hidden "spiritual" components.</gloss></term>, and have gathered it for certain. Yet nevertheless, I will now tell you something: since it is not impossible for a man to fish out and draw forth that incorruptible spirit from the thing in which it dwells—that spark, I say, and that *mustard seed* signified by Christ, hidden in the earth—and, with divine grace assisting, to extract and reserve it for his use, we believe surely that some secrets can be revealed to others which are entirely hidden from Mersenne.

Let him not say this is impossible because this spirit is not at all to be perceived by mortal eyes; this is indeed true in its simple nature. But insofar as it is clothed in light and a lucid spirit, it was granted to men and even sometimes visibly revealed to them. If this were not so, what does that speech of Solomon mean: *Wisdom is the brightness of the eternal light of the Almighty, which to the sun, or*

<meta>The text cuts off here at the catchword "stellis" (stars), likely continuing the passage from the Wisdom of Solomon 7:26-29, comparing Wisdom to the light of the sun and stars.</meta>

He attacks Marin Mersenne for lacking experimental knowledge and failing to recognize the "mustard seed" of divine spirit hidden within physical matter.</summary>

# On the Comparison of Wisdom with Folly

<margin>Wisdom 7</margin>
<margin>Wisdom 6</margin>
...Is she not clearer when compared to the stars? <note>original: "stellis comparata est clarior?" Fludd is quoting Wisdom of Solomon 7:29, which describes Wisdom as more beautiful than the sun and all the constellations.</note> If, therefore, she were not subject to the life of mortals, why would the wise man speak thus? Furthermore, it is indicated by the wise man in express words elsewhere that *wisdom is easily seen by those who love her, and is found by those who seek her.*

<margin>Ecclus. 6</margin>
<margin>Ecclus. 24</margin>
<margin>Ecclus. 42</margin>

<margin>Isaiah</margin>
And our Savior said, *seek, and you shall find; knock, and it shall be opened to you.* And elsewhere we read: *Investigate wisdom and she shall be made manifest to you.* The manner of her appearance is also found in this text: namely, that **God** sends wisdom *like light, for there is a spark in every creature, from which and through which the creature exists and has life,* as the Wise Man testifies. <note>Fludd is making a Hermetic argument here: that a "divine spark" or "scintilla" resides in all physical matter, giving it life.</note> Moreover, Job explains the place from which she is more easily extracted with these words: *Wisdom* (he says) *is drawn from hidden places.* And the prophet says: *Heavens, drop down dew from above and let the clouds rain the Just One; let the earth be opened and bud forth a Savior; let justice spring up at the same time; I have created it, etc.* <note>Isaiah 45:8. Fludd interprets this "rain" as the descent of the spiritual soul into matter.</note> If this were understood to be about the Christ incarnate, or born from the flesh of the Virgin Mary—as some, or rather the greater part of men, wish—why did the prophet say, *Heavens, drop down dew from above and let the clouds rain the Just One?* As if the incarnate Christ were sent to us from the clouds into the earth? But this refers to that spiritual Christ, namely the Son of God who was from the beginning of the world, of whom John said: *That Word was in the beginning with God,* and the carnal man Jesus was full of this kind of spirit. Whence it is written: *But Jesus, full of the Holy Spirit, etc.*

<margin>Luke 4:1</margin>
<margin>1 Cor. 12</margin>
<margin>Ecclus. 3</margin>
<margin>Gen.</margin>
Hence, therefore, the Apostle says: *Our fathers were under the cloud, and all ate the same spiritual food and drank the same spiritual drink, which was the spiritual rock following them, which rock was Christ.* <note>1 Corinthians 10:1-4. Fludd uses this to argue that "Christ" is a universal spiritual principle present even in the Old Testament before the physical birth of Jesus.</note> Has it not also been said that the throne of Wisdom was in a pillar of cloud? *Wisdom, which came forth from the mouth of the Most High, and which covers the earth like a mist?* I would like you to remember the stone upon which the Patriarch Jacob slept, which he then set up as a monument and poured oil upon, saying: *Surely this stone shall be called the house of God, etc.* <note>Genesis 28:18. To an alchemist like Fludd, Jacob’s stone is a symbol of the "Philosopher’s Stone" or the divine presence in matter.</note> If, therefore, it is the house of God, then God is in it as if in a house, and consequently He is as much in the earth as in heaven: which, if it be opened in the proper way, will bring forth the Savior: for (as the Apostle testifies) Jesus fills all things, and in this way the **spiritual rock** shall be revealed, which (as the same Apostle testifies) was Christ.

<margin>Isaiah</margin>
A *cornerstone* (I say), and of true salvation for the wise, but for the foolish a *stone of stumbling*, and *rejected by the builders*; <note>A reference to Psalm 118 and 1 Peter 2. <margin>Job 38</margin>
The **microcosm** (I say) is where the temple of the Holy Spirit is, just as He placed his tabernacle in the sun, or the heart of the world. The **macrocosm** is when the foundations of the earth were fixed upon this rock, for that was the *Cornerstone cast into the earth by Jehovah.* Most true is that *Cornerstone which the builders rejected, which is the head of the corner. By Jehovah* (says the prophet) *this is done, and it is wonderful in our works.* But if Mersenne wished to understand with an even surer pen who this hidden stone is, which is the "house of God" according to the sense of the Patriarch—which so many men have ignored and rejected for their building—he would find it described thus by the sacred letters:

<margin>Rom. 9:33</margin>

But if you require a more exact condition of that stone: we would have you understand that this is that stone of which the Evangelist speaks: *He who falls upon this stone shall be broken, but upon whomsoever it falls, it will crush him.*

<meta>This page continues Robert Fludd’s defense of his mystical philosophy against Marin Mersenne. Having previously argued that Divine Wisdom is a "spark" in all creatures, Fludd now claims that this spiritual truth is accessible through alchemical experimentation, specifically the "anatomy" of wheat/bread, which reveals the hidden life-force of the world.</meta>

# On the Contest, Book I.

If this one thing is unknown, it is necessary to be ignorant of everything else. And although I acknowledge myself to be by no means worthy of the possession of this immense gift—this supreme good and most holy influence—I nevertheless rejoice in this, and indeed with the greatest exultation of soul: that my mind, once enveloped in darkness, is now gradually emerging from it to behold this Spiritual light from afar, and to gaze with sharp eyes upon its truth, or rather its mystic Wisdom hidden in the lap of nature, just as it truly is. I do this by persuading myself, and knowing for a certainty, that even to this day there are men—both of the Roman and Lutheran religions <note>Fludd is emphasizing that this "true" spiritual knowledge transcends the bitter sectarian divides of the 17th century.</note> (who nonetheless look back to nothing but the single truth as the certain goal of religions)—who are the true possessors of that hidden light, or **hidden Manna**, or shining Wisdom, by whose mediation they can perform wonders in this world.

But truly, since *Mersenne* <note>Marin Mersenne (1588–1648), a French monk and mathematician who was a fierce critic of Fludd’s "occult" chemistry.</note> will also expect something certain from me: I will briefly show him what I have experienced in the **anatomy of bread** <note>original: "panis anatomia." This refers to the chemical distillation of wheat to separate its components.</note> which can prove the existence of the **Soul of the World** <term>Anima Mundi; the vital spirit that Fludd believed animated all physical matter.</term>. We observe that bread, or wheat, is divided into two parts by the violence of fire, as if into a heaven and an earth still hidden. I perceived a most black and, as it were, crude earth at the bottom of the receiver, like the blackest pitch; and I saw (relatively speaking) immense waters rising above it, tinted everywhere with a shining Spirit. I took these waters and divided the lower from the higher: namely, the phlegmatic water <note>In alchemy, "phlegm" is the watery, tasteless, and inactive byproduct of distillation.</note> from the aerial Spirit. Between these two, one may see as great a difference as exists between river water (or rather sea water) and the heavens. We observed that the upper waters appear in the form of a **volatile salt** or a most clear and crystalline liquor. Its unctuous nature was such that it was elevated upward even by the gentle vapor of a heat-bath; some part of this liquor congealed into the form of hail at the top of the distilling vessel <note>original: "alembici," a classic gourd-shaped chemical still.</note> and remained suspended in the head of the vessel, while we saw the remainder, changed into an oily liquor, fall back down into the receiver in the form of crystalline and most clear water. I therefore perceived this liquor to be of the nature of heaven or air, because, contrary to the nature of common water, it was accustomed to mix itself both with the "phlegm" of the bread and with its fiery oil—just as air has commerce and fellowship with both extremes, namely the element of fire and that of water. 

But now I shall dwell upon a certain experiment, unexpected by me and occurring by chance, through which I detected the power of the **Soul of the World** dwelling in the Sun. Having poured that aerial liquor into a small distilling vessel, I exposed it to the living rays of the sun at the very top of a certain tower. And (which is a thing worthy of admiration) we perceived such a great friendship and sympathetic harmony between that liquor (which proves the worldly and life-giving Spirit) and the condition of the shining and formal Sun, that suddenly—namely, in the space of four or five hours—that liquor attracted the solar form to itself by a certain magnetic power in such quantity that it took on the appearance of a most beautiful ruby, and was stained with the color of blood. 

From this, therefore, by diligent contemplation, I perceived the reason for multiplication and growth <note>original: "multiplicationis & vegetationis."</note> in both plants and animals: namely, because of the attractive faculty of the Spirit itself existing in the plant and the animal. Since this Spirit is matter perfectly disposed to receive the life-giving form of heaven, it is seized by a desire for that form and attracts it with a certain magnetic force. Indeed, the form itself, being greatly delighted by such matter, descends by a natural inclination into its moist and aerial substance. From this it happens that the air of the plant, greedily affecting its own likeness in the atmosphere, and this new air being joined to the specific air [of the plant], attracts and entices to itself a portion of the shining form multiplied according to its quantity—provided that the first-born air has been freed from the earth by putrefaction. In this way, a single grain grows into a hundred. We have therefore clearly perceived from these things that the liquor in the wheat or bread, informed in such a way, was the cause of both growth and multiplication; for just as I observed its power of multiplication from the aforesaid, so indeed I discovered its power of growth and the potential for vegetation through another experiment that happened to me by chance...
