# On Wisdom vs. Folly

### Page 58
->**On the Contest of Wisdom with Folly**<-
<meta>catchword: videli-</meta>

...I observed this even as it was emerging. For one day, the dregs <note>original: "fex"; the solid residue left after distillation.</note> of that liquid, having been separated from its clear substance, were thrown into a certain earthenware pot. This pot was covered with a thin iron plate, and the matter enclosed within, seeking to rise upward by its own **Spiritual** power, penetrated that very plate within the space of a few days. The result was that from the upper surface of the plate, a thousand little stalks or spikes—like thick hairs or thin needles—grew out in imitation of growing grain. By this observation, there came to mind not only the origin of growth <term>vegetatio; the power of life and development in plants</term> but also the reason why plants, in their growth, rise as if from the dead back to life. This **Spirit**, therefore, is that part found in both plants and animals whose chief duty is to care for, nourish, and preserve the solid parts of the animal—and indeed the plant—which are composed of the four elements, keeping them in their being and existence. Similarly, this is the reason why that portion of the chyle <note>original: "chyli"; the milky fluid produced by digestion.</note> which is drawn to the liver through the mesenteric veins by the solar heat of both the **Microcosm** <term>Microcosmus; the "little world," or the human body as a reflection of the universe</term> and the **Macrocosm** <term>Macrocosmus; the universe as a whole</term> is tinged with redness and also tastes of saltiness. Hence it is that the serum of the blood or the urine is salty. And in this way, we find the passive nature of life in these lower things.

But then, afterward, I exposed that black **Chaos** <note>original: "Chaos"; Fludd uses this to describe the dark, unrefined matter remaining at the bottom of the vessel.</note> precipitated at the bottom of the waters to the test of fire. From it, I first saw emerging water, then air (sharing in the aforementioned liquid), then a stinking fire or oil with a most unpleasant odor, and finally a certain substance similar to the sight of crystal, or rather hyacinth, both in color and consistency. In this subject, I found a most clear substance displaying the whiteness and clarity of crystal, interwoven everywhere with golden rays, not otherwise than we see the very substance of the heavens adorned with stars and solar rays. Furthermore, although that substance followed immediately after the fire-like nature of the foul-smelling oil, there was such a great difference between those two substances—both in appearance and consistency as well as odor—that instead of the fiery redness of that Element of fire, this celestial substance possessed a golden or amber color; instead of that stench, it took on a sweet odor; and instead of an oily fluidity, it retained a solid consistency. Considering these things, I believed this pleasant mass to be of the matter of heaven, which the Philosophers have called the **Quintessence** <term>Quinta Essentia; the "fifth element" or pure celestial essence beyond the four earthly elements</term>. I formed this opinion of mine for two reasons: one was because it appeared to my eyes in the fifth order of distillation and was divided from the others by its nature and condition; the other was because, through a certain experience that happened to me by chance, I perceived a life-giving power within it.

For, having committed an error in the distillation of this part, I threw some of this matter (which was, as it were, "suffocated") into a stone vessel—intended to catch rainwater falling from the roof of the house—which was full of rainwater. From this it happened that after a space of three weeks, I saw the water (which was once clear and limpid) now swarming in every part with very many worms of wonderful length, the thickness of a straw, and of the whitest color. By this experiment, I observed that a most powerful life-force <note>original: "virtutem"; virtue or power.</note> resides in this Element, and consequently, that a clear Spirit of this kind, made of the matter of heaven, shared in the richest rays of life infused into it by the sun. Instructed by these things, I perceived with open eyes that this life-giving and growing power is abundantly infused into both plants and animals from the Universal Source <note>original: "fonte Catholico"; here "Catholic" means "universal."</note> of the sublunary air (inasmuch as the air is the spice-box and storehouse of the heavenly treasure). Finally, I perceived that air is the passive nature of life and health itself, just as light and solar power in its ethereal vehicle is the active nature; and conversely, that phlegmatic water is the passive author of death, corruption, and disease, and that stinking element of fire in the nutrients is their active driver. And this is the reason why there is such a great stench in rot <term>putredo; the process of decay or putrefaction</term>. Many other experiments regarding wheat and bread worthy of observation can be found in our treatise *On the Triple Anatomy*...

# Book 1, On the Contest

<meta>catchword: as and</meta>
h 2

As we clearly expressed in the first portion, where the **Anatomy of Bread** is discussed: in which we brilliantly declared the logic of our history concerning both the **Macrocosm** <term>Macrocosmus; the "Great World" or the universe as a whole</term> as well as the **Microcosm** <term>Microcosmus; the "Little World," referring to the human being as a reflection of the universe</term>, and also the **Harmony of the World**. And although I know things far more hidden than these, which should by no means be openly published to the common crowd regarding the Harmony of the world: nevertheless, because these things which we have already related are sufficient to confirm the existence of the **World Soul** <term>Anima Mundi; the spiritual force that animates all nature</term>, I did not think it necessary to hasten toward more profound examples.

Finally, I would like you to gather from these experiments of ours that unless such a universal soul existed in the world, plants could neither grow nor multiply, nor could any animal live. For (as the Philosopher <note>likely referring to Aristotle or a similar classical authority</note> rightly says) the power of life for any creature is in the air, since it is from the air that a creature draws its life-virtue; thus, without the constant breathing in of that virtue, both animal and plant would immediately perish and be suffocated. 

But now, to direct the edge of my speech toward Mersenne <note>Marin Mersenne (1588–1648), a French polymath and philosopher who was a fierce critic of Fludd’s mystical views</note> once more—a man who is nothing other than an **Ape** of other learned men, and a sort of **Sponge** of the Republic of Letters, having nothing of his own and being utterly sterile in his own invention, which is why he uses the tongues and pens of others. Indeed, in many places in his volume, he usurps much from my own writings and inventions (while hiding my name), and yet he is accustomed to condemn and slander his own teachers for the sake of a better appearance. To that very man, I say, I will now at last turn my pen, by which I will declare a matter to him confirmed by infallible observation—something he has neither read, nor heard, nor understood to this day. Thus, I begin with him by asking these questions:

### Problematic Question: The First.

->What is the reason (my dear Mersenne) that animals are compelled by an unavoidable necessity to<-
->breathe air at every moment of their lives? Is it only, as you say—led by the doctrine of the<-
->ancient philosophers and physicians—for the **cooling of the heart**? <note>original: "cordis refrigerium"; the traditional Galenic view that the primary purpose of breathing was to vent the heart's heat.</note><-

### Second Question.

->From where do the arteries draw their subtle blood, which so abounds with the Spirits of Life?<-
->Is it from the purer portion of the venous blood, whether through **anastomosis** <term>the connection between blood vessels</term> or<-
->through **diapedesis** <term>the passage of blood cells through capillary walls</term>, or finally through the orifices of the<-
->arteries from the more solid parts of the body, which are irrigated by blood of the venous kind,<-
->as the Philosophers and Physicians suppose?<-

### Third Question.

->Why did nature, or rather God, assign to man in his creation four seminal vessels, of which<-
->two are arterial and just as many are venous? Is it only for the same end, so that the fetus<-
->might participate in the nature of both types of blood?<-

Certainly, for a true resolution of these questions, a far deeper inspection is required than I have ever been able to gather from any philosopher or physician in my experience, or through the reading of their books, or the accounts of others. Therefore, to seek refuge in the **World Soul** we discussed before, we think it necessary—if we wish to have a direct resolution to these questions—that very World Soul which Mersenne tries so hard to utterly destroy.

### Question I.

Marin Mersenne himself (*Columns 1739, 1745, and 1746*), in showing the reason why man is called a **Microcosm**, notes that man communicates with all parts of the world, as was declared above; for this reason, he cannot condemn the opinion of **Hermes Trismegistus** <note>original: "Mercurii Trismegisti"; the legendary Hellenistic figure associated with alchemy and hermetic philosophy</note>, which teaches that the world itself is the Son of God...

# On [the Contest of] Wisdom with Folly

as also the man of the world: for just as this world was immediately formed by God, so too in the representative <note>original: "typico"</note> world, He governs according to the image of the world, which is also for the same reason the image of God. For God breathed the breath of life into two worlds, as was said before; hence we observe both to be endowed with a divine soul, as has been more fully demonstrated from what was said above. Thus, the **lesser world** <term>Microcosm; the human being as a reflection of the universe</term> is the son of the **greater world** <term>Macrocosm; the universe as a whole</term>, inasmuch as it received its matter or substance from that of the world (as Marinus <note>likely referring to the philosopher Marinus of Neapolis or a contemporary reference</note> himself admits) and its life-giving form from the internal soul of the world, the primordial actor, who placed his tabernacle in the Sun. From this it follows that the **Microcosmic Spirit** is nourished by the **Macrocosmic Spirit**, and its soul by that divine and central soul which reigns so wonderfully in the mundane Sun. Therefore, just as the Sun, as if it were the heart of the world, receives its life-giving action from God holding the scepter in His archetypal kingdom—which action the Holy Scriptures call the *Word and Wisdom*—so also the microcosmic heart receives life and the sustenance of its life from the kingdom of the Word, or typical Wisdom; namely, from the mundane Sun, from which its life-giving rays essentially rain down and are imparted everywhere to mundane things.

And this is the reason why the philosopher **Zeno** <note>Zeno of Citium, founder of Stoicism</note> used to call the vital soul in man a *natural fire*, and indeed, he called that virtue of life by that name most fittingly. **Prometheus** likewise called it the *celestial fire enriching men with life*. Hence it is necessary that this fire be nourished and cherished by its like at every moment of time, lest man be suffocated and perish. And this is that which **Parmenides** <note>Pre-Socratic philosopher</note> seems to intimate, saying quite wisely: *Nature rejoices in nature.* For this reason, the virtue of life situated in the very center of the heart, by a certain magnetic power, strives to draw to itself through inhalation its like, which is sent down from heaven and floats everywhere in the air, without which it cannot live; for there is a hidden food of life in the air. Hence it is that this author, a most profound observer of the mysteries of nature, breaks out into this speech of admiration: *O celestial Nature, multiplying the natures of truth by the nod of God.* And again: *O strong Nature, nature conquering and surpassing, and making its own natures to rejoice, etc.* By these words he seems to argue that by means of that celestial nature, the vital spirits and the flames included within it are increased and multiplied, as it is by its virtue that not only life, but also the fruits of generation are produced unto infinity. Indeed, greater mysteries consist in the union of the fire of human life (or Microcosmic nature) with the fire or celestial light emanating from the shining source than the human tongue can express. For by their connection, the universal nature of the world is known to have participation and communion with that of man, through which, in **Magic** <term>Magia; here meaning "Natural Magic," the study of the hidden powers of nature</term> (I do not say *Cacomagia* <note>original: "Cacomagia"; meaning evil or black magic</note>, but that which is good and natural), stupendous things can be performed.

We have said, therefore, that the elementary air is most full of the influences of the life of vegetation and of the formal seeds in generation, insofar as it is filled with divine rays and is a most exuberant sanctuary or treasury of celestial gifts, as has been declared in the preceding pages. Hence **Moses** said, *and he saw that they were good*, that is, full; for that Spirit of Wisdom, most rich in life and fertility, fills and cherishes it by its presence and virtue. Whence **Hermes Trismegistus** <note>The legendary Hellenistic figure associated with the Hermetic corpus</note> says: *The Word standing by the moist nature cherished it.* In which (says Saint John) *was life, and that light of men, by which the world was made, etc.* And the royal Psalmist says that *God is the light from which comes life, that is, the breath of lives.* Has it not also been said that from this *Treasury* or Ark of God, that *manna*, the *hidden* food of both life and the angels themselves, has flowed out? I mean the *hidden manna*, of which it is said: *He gave them the bread of heaven to eat.* And **Christ** says: *Our Father gives you the true bread from heaven, for the true bread is that which descends from heaven and gives life to the world. I am the bread of life.* And elsewhere it is declared that this bread descending from heaven is the *Word*: our Savior understands this food when he replied to the tempter in these words: *Man does not live by bread alone, but by every word that proceeds from the mouth of God.* Where by the *Word*, Moses intends the *manna*. Indeed, this is that *Daily Bread* for which [He] teaches us...

<summary>Robert Fludd explains that the human heart acts as a microcosm of the Sun, drawing in a "celestial fire" or "hidden manna" from the air that sustains life. He synthesizes classical philosophy and Biblical scripture to argue that the air is filled with the divine Word, which acts as the essential "food" for all living spirits.</summary>
<keywords>Microcosm, Macrocosm, World Soul, Sun, Heart, Manna, Word of God, Natural Magic, Zeno, Prometheus, Hermes Trismegistus</keywords>

--- Page 72 ---
<meta>Continuity: Following the previous discussion on how the Microcosm (human) is nourished by the Macrocosm (universe), Fludd now explains the specific mechanism of how the divine "Bread of Life" is drawn from the air into the heart to sustain life.</meta>

# On the Contest, Book I.

...the Savior, [taught] that we should pray daily for, namely, **Supersubstantial Bread** <note>original: "Panis videlicet Supersubstantialis." This refers to Jerome’s translation of the Lord’s Prayer in the Vulgate (Matthew 6:11), where "daily bread" is rendered as "supersubstantial," implying a spiritual or divine nourishment beyond mere physical food.</note>, as Jerome interprets it.

<margin>A new light.</margin>
By which it is indicated that the air itself is most rich in this kind of bread of life. And in this sense the Philosopher <note>Likely referring to Michael Sendivogius, who wrote under the title "New Light of Alchemy."</note> seems to speak:
> *There is in the air a hidden food of life, as has been said, etc.*

For this is that hidden food, or bread, by which the vital Spirits of the human heart are nourished every minute of time, and consequently, it is the fountain and wellspring of the Spirit and the virtues of life. For it is not the air itself that nourishes, but a certain seed of celestial life floating in the air; nor does the air lead only to the cooling of the heart, as the common people hold, but it performs the office of a vehicle for that ethereal Spirit. This Spirit contains within itself the little sparks or atoms of life distilling downward from the bright source; for just as the empyrean Spirit <term>the highest, most refined fire of the heavens</term> is the vehicle of the mind, so the celestial Spirit is the receptacle of the empyrean, and the sublunary air, acting as the carriage for the higher things, conducts all those celestial mysteries into the fleshy temple <note>meaning the human body</note>.

<margin>Pymander, 12.</margin>
But this is also excellently explained by Hermes Trismegistus in this way:
> *Air is in the body, the soul is in the air, the mind is in the soul, and the Word is in the mind; and he asserted the Word to be their Father.*

Wherefore he concludes in the same place that the *Word is the image of God, the mind the image of the Word, the soul the image of the mind, air the image of the soul, and the body the image of the air.* It follows, therefore, that the purest portion of matter is air; of air, the soul; of the soul, the mind; and of the mind, God. These are his words: by which he seems to suggest that the mind is clothed in many garments of nature, varying in dignity, before it enters the human body.

<margin>Ecclesiasticus 1.</margin>
Where it must also be noted that by "mind," which he makes the image of the Word, he understands the Divine Wisdom so often cited by us in the preceding pages. Concerning which it is said: *The fountain of Wisdom is the Word of God on high.* And by "soul," he intends the ethereal Spirit, adorned, illustrated, and animated by the divine soul or mind. Furthermore, Hermes describes the manner and duty of this air—enriched with all the gifts of heaven—entering into the human body in the succinct flow of these words:

<margin>Pymander, 10.</margin>
Trismegistus:
> *The soul of man,* he says, *is carried in this manner: the mind in the reason, the reason in the soul, the soul in the Spirit, and the Spirit in the body; the Spirit, diffused through the veins and arteries and the blood, moves the living being everywhere, etc.*

Where by "reason" he intends the empyrean Spirit (which is also called rational), and by "Spirit" he intends the air.

Here, therefore, you have matter prepared by God—who is present everywhere—not only for the lives of animals but for all other lower creatures according to the logic of their species. This is the **Universal Soul**, unique, simple, and benevolent in its own nature; yet, in respect to the multiplicity of creatures which it informs, it is called manifold, to use the words of the Sage:
> *The Spirit of Wisdom,* he says, *is unique or simple, manifold, kind, possessing all virtue, reaching everywhere because of its purity, overseeing all things; which, since it is one, can do all things, and remaining in itself, it renews all things, etc.*

I say it is "manifold" insofar as it changes itself into the nature of any thing, and according to the condition of any species produced in creation, it makes generations either more noble or more base; yet it remains nonetheless untouched in its own nature, its simplicity not changed in the least. Thus also it is the preserver of lives and the sustainer of hearts. Now, listen briefly to how these sparks of life, or atoms floating in the mundane Spirit, are introduced into the human lodging or temple.

Sublunary air, in which the sparks of life and generation float (as was said), and without which a living creature (among whom we establish man as the chief) cannot live for even a moment—it is necessary, I say, that this air be inspired every minute of time to the heart by means of the instrument of the lungs. Unless this occurs, the animal must inevitably be suffocated; for when the aerial Spirit is taken away and excluded from the heart of the animal—along with its enclosed treasure—its soul is also removed. This soul is the ethereal light, which, together with such a Spirit, nourishes and refreshes the **innate heat** <term>the vital warmth or "pilot light" of the body</term>, and the **radical moisture** <term>the fundamental fluid or "fuel" that sustains life</term>, which would otherwise decrease, is successively restored. Therefore, the animal—but more effectively the human—breathes this Spirit into the left chamber of the heart...

<meta>Continuity: Following the previous discussion on the "bread of life" and the "hidden food" in the air, Fludd now transitions to the anatomical process by which the heart (the "Microcosm’s Laboratory") extracts this spiritual nourishment from the inhaled air to distribute it throughout the body.</meta>

# Of Wisdom with Folly

...at length <note>original: "lamū" (likely "jam" or "demum")</note>, for the reason explained by us in our common anatomy—namely, by the contraction of the straight fibers of the heart, and consequently the dilation of the oblique and transverse ones—this motion is called **<term>Diastole</term>** <note>The expansion of the heart's chambers. In Fludd's time, this was believed to be the moment the heart "sucked in" vital air.</note>. During this interval, the heart introduces air—containing its "pearls of life"—through the lungs via the pulmonary vein into its left ventricle, for the nourishment of both vital and generative spirits. This is observed to happen just as the liver draws the juice of nourishment through the mesenteric veins to its own cavity, in order to regenerate the natural spirits destined for growth and vegetation. 

In this **Laboratory of the Microcosm** <note>The "Microcosm" refers to the human being as a small-scale reflection of the entire universe (the Macrocosm).</note>, nature attempts to perform a complete "anatomy" of the air: that is, it proceeds to divide it into its equal parts. Here, nature distinguishes the fiery substance of heaven and the ethereal spirit (which is its vehicle) from the smoky and useless impurities of the air. It stores these celestial parts in its sanctuary—namely, the great artery called the **<term>Aorta</term>**—while it converts the elementary parts into smoke for the most part, as they are not as required for the business of life, being corruptible and subject to change. These are emitted like smoke through the same path by which they were introduced, as if they were superfluous and hardly necessary for the work of life. 

However, the bright atoms of life are retained within the ethereal spirit that holds them, along with the purer portion of the air. These are enclosed in the aforementioned "microcosmic storehouse of ointments" and reserved like a most precious treasure. Indeed, these are those **Phoebean seeds** <note>referring to Phoebus Apollo, the sun god; these are "seeds of light" from the sun</note> flowing down from the bright source of the sun, which are said to be of the sun's nature and essence, just as a ray is said to be part of its whole. For in the sun, the spirit of **Wisdom** has placed its tabernacle; hence Plato, not at all unwisely, frequently called the sun the "invisible world," just as he called the created sun the "visible world."

<margin>
Psalm 84:11 <note>original: "84. 14."</note>
Psalm 103:1
Psalm 104:2
</margin>
King David also seems to agree with these words, asserting that **Jehovah** is a sun, giving grace and glory; in another place, he says God is "clothed with light as with a garment." This is the reason why the Hebrew and Egyptian Doctors say that the **Soul of the World** <note>original: "animam mundi"</note> is that emanation which first shone forth from the **Tetragrammaton** <note>The four-letter Hebrew name of God, Y-H-W-H.</note> at the creation of the world. We do not say that the visible sun is something divine or God-shaped because it is a creature of God; but we do say that a divine and truly bright spirit exists within it, which is its primary act and divine soul—that **Wisdom** which was created first, as the Wise Man <note>referring to Solomon</note> testifies.

<margin>
Ecclesiastes 1.
</margin>
Therefore, as soon as that matter was separated from the impurity of the sublunary air in its most noble "chemical workshop" or laboratory of the Microcosm—namely, the left chamber of the heart—it is introduced into the great artery or **Aorta** through three semilunar valves (which open inward and close outward), as if into the sanctuary or treasure chest of the microcosmic nature. By means of this main trunk, it is dispersed everywhere into the entire body through infinite small branches: specifically, through the **Carotids** to the brain, through which the more super-celestial part of the solar substance is raised to the "cerebral heaven" or that rational and empyrean part of the Microcosm, as it is more eminent in dignity at the vital source. 

Next, those **Cervical** and **Brachial** arteries, and those that water the parts of the chest, give them a spiritual impression of life. The **Coronary** arteries vivify the internal part of the heart with a portion of that same spirit, and so on for the rest. From this it is clear that every part of the body receives and greedily attracts its own proper spirits of life from a particular branch of the Aorta destined for its vivification. Indeed (according to the confession of **Marin Mersenne** <note>A French polymath and contemporary of Fludd who often debated these hermetic ideas</note>), just as the Microcosm has a relationship in all its parts to similar parts in the Macrocosm, so the purer portion of any part of the Macrocosm serves that vivifying light or celestial fire as a vehicle. It conducts life to that member in the Microcosm which is closest to it in nature and condition: namely, the **Empyrean spirit** of the world to the intellectual or rational spirit of man; the **ethereal spirit** to the vital spirit; and the thinner part of the **elementary spirit** to vivify the "natural man," whose source is the liver itself—although this latter region is observed to be nourished chiefly by composite food.

<summary>This page describes the heart as a "chemical laboratory" that extracts divine solar spirits from inhaled air and pumps them through the Aorta to nourish the body. Fludd links this biological process to the theological "Soul of the World" and the biblical "Wisdom" emanating from God.</summary>

<meta>Continuity: Following the previous page's discussion on the heart as the "Microcosm’s Laboratory" which extracts spiritual nourishment from the air, Fludd now addresses three specific doubts or questions to further prove that the human body functions through celestial influence rather than purely mechanical or elementary means.</meta>

# On the Contest, Book 1.

Having correctly considered these things, you see (as I believe) with open eyes—contrary to that common answer to the first question—that air is introduced into the left chamber of the heart through breathing at every moment in time, not for the cooling of the heart as is the common rumor, but for a far worthier cause and a more sublime business. Namely, since the air (as has been said) is the receptacle of the **rays of life** emitted divinely from heaven, it is necessary that man use it as a vehicle by means of which the gifts of life are introduced through the passages of the lungs. These gifts are so subtle and exist in such a small quantity due to their rare expansion throughout the whole air, that unless they were introduced every minute into the left chamber of the heart by means of the air, it would not be possible for anyone to exist in this life or remain among the living for even the shortest span of time.

## The Second Question.

The second doubt will be removed once the preceding answer is carefully weighed. For we strongly affirm, and have dared to defend and maintain against anyone, that this **arterial blood** is not extracted from venous blood—as is the opinion of common men—but from a purer portion of air and spirits together with a **celestial tincture** <note>original: "tincturæ cæleftis"; in Fludd’s vitalist philosophy, this is a spiritual essence or "dye" that gives life to matter</note>. This tincture is the most fruitful life, which I can indeed confirm through various experiments proven by me. For if the artery extracted its substance from the venous kind, then this would happen either through its own openings from the openings of the veins, or by some other hidden passage unknown to us, unless it occurred through **<term>diapedesis</term>** <note>The oozing of blood cells through intact vessel walls</note> or some **<term>anastomosis</term>** <note>A connection or opening between two vessels</note>. But that is not at all likely, since by this reasoning the thick elementary blood—destined for the creation of solid limbs—would mix itself with the spirits of life, and confusion in the human work would follow, as will be clearer in what follows. For just as all impurity is expelled from the ethereal heaven of the **<term>Macrocosm</term>** <note>The universe at large</note>, so the matter stands in the **<term>Microcosm</term>** <note>The human being as a reflection of the universe</note>.

## The Third Question.

As for the common resolution of the final question: first, it must be known that it is not as easy a thing as the common people imagine. To resolve a doubt of this kind, it is necessary to extract and explain the greatest mystery in nature, which indeed until this day has been openly revealed to the world by no one (in my opinion). But since its disclosure pertains to the confirmation of the **<term>World Soul</term>** <note>Anima Mundi; the vital spirit that Fludd believed permeated the entire universe</note> against the false opinion of **Mersenne** <note>Marin Mersenne (1588–1648), a French mathematician and philosopher who was a fierce critic of Fludd’s mystical and alchemical views</note>, I will reveal it to you in this place.

Just as it cannot be hidden from anyone of sound mind that man himself is composed of the body as the external part and the soul as the internal, so it is evidently manifested (and recorded in public writing by the more skilled) that the human sperm itself is fused from two things: namely, the external and visible, and the internal and invisible. That which is drawn from the purer substance of the elements for the consistency of the solid limbs takes the place of the body in the sperm, and is called by the proper name **<term>sperma</term>**. However, that which descends from heaven by means of an aerial vehicle (which we discussed above at greater length) takes the place of the soul in the spermatic composition, and is called by the name **<term>semen</term>** <note>Fludd distinguishes between the physical material ("sperma") and the vital, spiritual spark ("semen")</note>. This will appear more clearly to us if we look with a more accurate gaze at the number, condition, and origin of the **preparing vessels** <note>original: "vasorum fpermatis præparantium"; the spermatic arteries and veins</note> of the sperm. As for their number, we are taught even by common experience that it is fourfold: two of which correspond to each side. Of these two, one is a vein or branch arising from the venous kind or trunk, and the other is an artery or process descending from the body of the **great artery** <note>The aorta</note>. Both arteries, or arterial vessels, arise like wings from the same place on the great artery along its length, one of which from the part...

<summary>Fludd refutes common medical ideas by arguing that the heart extracts divine "rays of life" from the air and that arterial blood is a spiritual substance distinct from venous blood. He begins explaining the dual physical-spiritual nature of human reproduction as a defense of the World Soul against Marin Mersenne.</summary>

<meta>Continuity: Having established that the heart acts as a laboratory for extracting spiritual nourishment from the air, Fludd now transitions to the reproductive system. He argues that the creation of life is not merely a physical process but a divine act where the "World Soul" and the "Divine Spirit" shape the human form within the womb.</meta>

# On Wisdom with Folly

For, just as in the eagle there is an "eagle-ness," and so on, so too does the Divine Spirit make an impression upon the **spermatic mass** as if upon wax or clay. This spermatic clay is irrigated by its own seminal spirit, like **living water**, and by the divine power hidden within that spirit, it is led back into a **living soul** by means of its **philosophical egg** <note>original: "ouo philosophico"; here Fludd refers to the womb as a vessel of transformation, similar to an alchemist's vessel</note>. Thus, they are tempered together according to the laws of uncreated nature—the sperm to its seed—and the seed, hidden by divine power within the sperm, is stirred within it, much like a breath from a breather, to form the sperm according to the fertile image of its created species, as originally intended by the Creator’s design. It is as if a certain mole were setting out to build and fashion its own proper and comfortable home in the earth. Yet, the internal agent of this work is God Himself, or the Divine Spirit described above by the authority of the Sacred Page. King David also seems to confirm this in a general discourse of this kind:

> ->*By the word of the Lord the heavens were established, and by the spirit of his mouth all their power:*<-

So too the life of man (which is his specific power insofar as he is endowed with reason) has its origin from the Spirit of God. Indeed, it consists in His act; it is moved, governed, and driven by Him, seeing that He "breathes where He wills," as St. John testifies. Hence it was said by the Wise Man that *no man has mastery over his own spirit*. By the Spirit (says David) *from His mouth comes all the power of the world*, or of the heavens, and consequently life—and much more so the life of man. Insofar as man is the noblest of creatures, he does not merely possess a mundane spirit within himself, but is also clothed and enriched by that Divine Spirit, acting as the ruler and governor of the mundane.

> ->*In man* (says Job) *there is a spirit, but the inspiration of the Almighty gives him understanding,*<-

from which he argues that although a created spirit exists in man, there is also present in him an uncreated spirit, which is the power of life and the primary act within him. Therefore, St. Paul asserts quite deliberately that the *human spirit is the temple of the Holy Spirit*. We conclude, therefore, that the mixing of the **celestial spirit** (which is the vehicle of the mind) within the sperm, together with the **elemental essence** (by which mixture the generative spirit partakes of both sexes), is perfected in a marvelous entanglement of this kind. 

In this process, the aerial spirit—previously barely perceptible or distinguishable from the venous blood—as well as the ethereal spirit, selected from the arterial blood by the **chemistry of nature** <note>original: "chymia naturæ"; Fludd views the body's biological functions as natural alchemical processes</note>, is reduced into the form of a whitening mist or cloud. The divine will ordained that this division of the generative spirit from the blood mass, and the mingling of the corporeal sperm with its soul or vivifying spirit, should become more exact and exquisite. Thus, this entanglement descends all the way to the **testicles**. It passes through the **epididymis** by **diapedesis** <note>original: "perforataq; per diapedesim"; here meaning the oozing or passage of fluid through the pores of a membrane</note> and penetrates into the very substance of the testicles. 

Once this misty substance is perfectly purified and mixed to the smallest degree, it contains a "wind" within itself. By means of this internal and central spirit, it is agitated and moved, not otherwise than angels are said to move at the command of God, whose ministers they are. To each testicle, then, every such "entanglement" descends. In these places or vessels, the serous and superfluous watery substance—which exhaled with the generative spirit from the blood mass—is purged and separated from the mist or mass dedicated to generation. Thus, the **potential man** <note>original: "homo potentialis"; man in the state of a seed or possibility before physical formation</note> is now ready to be emitted from the father's preparing vessels by a suitable ejaculation into his natural vessel, or his **philosophical egg**—namely, the female womb. There he is enclosed until complete maturation—that is, until he is brought back into a human act or an **actual man**. 

Once this action of generation is complete, we shall briefly recite what usually happens to him afterward. By this, we may better reveal to you the immediate operation of the **World Soul** <note>original: "Animæ Mundanæ"</note> immersing itself into the spermatic mass, its succession, and the exact completion of so noble a creature. We shall show its continuous progress in generation, both in the adaptation and composition of the human sperm from the internal and external, and in its perfection into a human act.

--- Page 76 ---
<meta>Continuity: Following the previous discussion on how the Divine Spirit impresses itself upon the "spermatic clay" to form a living soul, Fludd now provides a detailed timeline of embryonic development, blending the medical authority of Galen with the scriptural authority of Solomon and Job.</meta>

# On the Contest, Book II.

<margin>Galen, *On Anatomy*</margin>
The sperm, therefore, possessing its own heavenly soul and filled with the highest divine power, is cast into the womb (by means of the two operations we described in Book 3, Chapter 5, *On our mystical Anatomy of the Sperm*). After a space of six days, it appears in the form of foam (as Galen <note>Galen of Pergamum (129–216 AD) was the preeminent medical authority in the Renaissance; his theories on the "three bubbles" of the embryo were widely accepted.</note> testifies). Then, after another three days have passed, three bubbles <note>original: "ampullæ tres"; these represent the foundational stages of the three primary organs</note> rise up from the mass, from which the principal members of the fetus—namely the brain, the heart, and the liver—later arise. Then, through the next three days, the internal outlines and "pointings" of the other members begin. For the first six days, nothing is drawn from the womb, because little or nothing of that material is lost; but through the six following days, as the windiness <note>original: "ventositate"; referring to the vital spirits or gases that drive growth</note> and spirits penetrate toward the interior, the branchings of the veins and arteries are completed. Through the twelve days that follow, blood enters through all the fleshy members—which is why, in a miscarriage at that time, fleshy pieces appear—and in this same span of time, the spinal marrow is divided from the brain through the backbone. Likewise, in the nine following days, the members are distinguished from one another: namely, the sides from the neck, the neck from the brain and head, the hips from the buttocks, and so on for the others. Thus, in total, there will be 35 days for most, although sometimes it happens later (in 40 days) and sometimes faster (in 30 days), so that a 5-day difference may always occur. These are the views of Galen. Therefore, by the appearance of the foam, it is argued that an internal act of immense power is working within the spermatic mass toward the goal of specific perfection, acting little by little and step by step—a process we have mentioned so often in the preceding pages. The effect of this generative operation is explained most excellently by Solomon in this discourse:

> A man is fashioned as flesh in his mother’s womb, curdled in blood for the time of ten months from the seed of man, and that through pleasure: and being born, he drew in the common air, etc. <note>A paraphrase of the Wisdom of Solomon 7:1-3.</note>

If, therefore, the drawing in of common air constitutes living, then it follows that a hidden force of life exists within the air. Furthermore, that Actor who produces such a marvelous effect is most clearly described by Job:

<margin>Job 10:9</margin>
> He who formed the members of man, when none of them as yet existed, etc.

By this, he seems to suggest that the Spirit of God labors within the spermatic mass before any likeness of a limb emerges. Then he proceeds:

<margin>Job 10:10-12</margin>
> Did you not pour me out like milk, and curdle me like cheese? You clothed me with skin and flesh, and knit me together with bones and sinews. You have granted me life and favor, and your visitation has preserved my spirit. But these things you hid in your heart; I know that this is with you, etc.

With these words, the entire mystery—both in the generation and in the preservation of the life of the one born—is clearly revealed and explained. For in this phrase ("you poured me out like milk"), he seems to argue that the spermatic mass was well-ordered by the divine spirit, from which man was later formed. Then he proceeds to the second stage in the progress of generation, saying, "you curdled me like cheese." By these words, he argues that the divine spirit contracted that fluid milk or sperm into a sort of cheese—that is, a solid mass. This is also in accord with those other words of Job:

> Remember, I pray, that you have made me like clay.

Then he proceeds: "you clothed me with skin and flesh, and composed me with bones and sinews," etc. Now, at last, he hastens toward the completion of the particular parts of man, from which the external man is fully constituted. Here he indicates that the divine spirit worked all these things in His own person. Therefore, the human body having been produced in this manner unto perfection, that divine Architect now proceeds to bring it to life by His own spirit. The following words of Job argue this most aptly: "you have granted me life and favor," etc. Here he indicates that once this bodily work was finished and adapted to receive its royal guest, God fills it with the divine mind—namely, the image of the Creator swimming in the world-spirit—so that by means of it, man can live and move. It is as if he had said:

> You breathed into my face the breath of life, and I became a man with a living soul,

according to that passage in Genesis, or according to Job:

> The Spirit of GOD made me, and the breath of the Almighty gave me life.

And under the term "life" or "vivification," the intellect is also included, for these are the inseparable virtues of the human soul. Whence Job says elsewhere:

> There is a spirit in man, but the inspiration of the Almighty gives him understanding.

<meta>Continuity: Following the previous page’s discussion on the physical development of the embryo, Fludd now shifts toward the metaphysical animation of the human being, explaining how Divine Wisdom (Sophia) serves as the "Universal Soul" that connects the individual human (microcosm) to the entire universe (macrocosm).</meta>

# On Wisdom and Folly

Whence, it is not only by the operative virtue of that spirit of Wisdom that it is imbued with the motion and agitation of life, but intelligence and reason are also given to it from on high. Finally, in his aforementioned discourse, he <note>Likely referring to Job or Solomon, the "Wise Man" frequently cited by Fludd.</note> seems to conclude thus: *And your visitation preserves my spirit, etc.* <note>original: "Et visitatio tua præseruat spiritum meum" (Job 10:12)</note>. It is as if he had said: You, being present everywhere through the spirit of the world, visit that spirit of life which you made; and by the bread of life sent forth from the holy heavens, or your tabernacle—namely, by the Word or the spirit of wisdom introduced to the chamber of the heart through an aerial vehicle <term>aerial vehicle: "aereum vehiculum," a Neoplatonic concept describing a subtle, vapor-like medium that allows the immaterial soul to interact with the physical body</term>—you refresh it. And hence it is that Christ commanded us to pray to the Father for our daily bread, as shall be discussed more widely below.

<margin>Wisdom 9</margin>
By these things, therefore, we are taught that there is a divine spirit which created not only the external part of Adam from the most excellent slime of the earth, but also his internal part from the breath of life—as if it were the most exact substance of heaven, purified—and indeed created all men coming into this world from the subtle part of the paternal body (which nonetheless remains leprous and defiled to this very day because of Adam’s sin) and from the celestial spirit mixed with divine light. Regarding this, John said: *That was the true Light, which lighteth every man that cometh into the world* <note>original: "Erat Lux vera quæ illuminat omnem hominem venientem in hunc mundum" (John 1:9)</note>. Because the father is leprous and the mother is leprous, the body was therefore said by the Wise Man <note>Refers to the author of the Book of Wisdom.</note> *to weigh down the soul and depress it, burdened with cares.* <note>original: "aggrauare animam eamque curis oneratam deprimere" (Wisdom 9:15)</note>.

<margin>Ecclesiastes 1</margin>
<margin>Wisdom 7</margin>
<margin>Wisdom 7:22</margin>
<margin>John 1</margin>
<margin>Wisdom 7</margin>
By these things, therefore, you see that one and the same soul or life-giving spirit is common to both worlds—namely, the macrocosm <note>The Great World or Universe</note> and the microcosm <note>The Little World or Human Being</note>; I call it the universal <note>original: "catholicam," meaning universal or all-encompassing in this philosophical context</note> and unique soul. Hence, therefore, Wisdom (which is called by the Wise Man *the first creature produced before all things*) is described by Solomon as being *a holy spirit of intelligence, unique, manifold, having all power, overseeing all things, which contains all intelligible spirits, etc.* <note>original: "spiritus intelligentiæ sanctus, vnicus, multiplex..." (Wisdom 7:22-23)</note>. This is that light which is said to be in the world, *and the world knew it not, and finally the world was made through him, and every man coming into the world was enlightened.* <note>Paraphrasing John 1:10-11</note>. Wherefore it is evident that it is one and the same life-giving spirit that animates the world as much as the human being, since life is in it. Whence it is *unique* and *simple*; but truly, insofar as it fills all things and vivifies any animal, vegetable, or mineral of the world according to its species, it is called *manifold*. Hence, although Orpheus <note>A legendary Greek poet and prophet; Fludd frequently blends "pagan" Orphic hymns with Christian theology.</note> sang that *all things are full of gods*, he nevertheless referred them all to the one Jove <note>Jupiter/God</note>, no less than infinite rays of the sun are continuous with the one sun. Hence, therefore, it was said: *God vivifies all things.* And elsewhere: *By the spirit from his mouth, all the power of the heavens* <note>original: "Spiritu ab ore eius omnis virtus cœlorum" (Psalm 33:6)</note>. Thus, although the incorruptible spirit of GOD is said *to be in all things*, yet it is not divided, being so dispersed from the immense fountain of eternal wisdom. Whence it is most certain that there is only one soul of the world, from whose fountain all lives are said to have flowed.

<margin>John 1</margin>
<margin>Proverbs 3</margin>
Here it must be diligently considered that this Universal Spirit must be contemplated in its manifold disposition: namely, either in its dilated nature, where it thus gives momentary life to creatures; or in its contracted and multiplied nature, where it thus brings eternal life to the possessor. In its dilated nature, *it fills the worldly heaven on every side*, as the Wise Man testifies; and thus anything created by it has its temporary existence or life either through inspiration or magnetic attraction. Whence the Philosopher says: *There is in the air a hidden food of life; thus man and other animals cannot live longer than they can draw in the air, which is full of the spirit of life.* In its contracted nature, it dwells within a contracted or dense body; whence Solomon said: *The holy spirit of discipline fills the terrestrial globe.* In this nature, however, it sometimes appears to certain chosen sons, concerning whom John intended to speak in these words: *As many as received this light, to them he gave power to become the sons of GOD.* And in this way, it is called the *Tree of Life*, of which the Wise Man says: *He who attains Wisdom is blessed; she is more precious than carbuncles, and all your delights cannot be compared to her; length of days is in...*

<meta>catchword: her right hand</meta>

<summary>Fludd explains the "Universal Soul" (Anima Catholica) as a divine, vivifying spirit that connects God to both the universe and the human body. He argues that this single spirit exists in two states: "dilated" to sustain the physical world and "contracted" within the individual to provide spiritual and eternal life.</summary>
<keywords>Universal Soul, Macrocosm, Microcosm, Divine Wisdom, Spiritus Mundi, Bread of Life, Orpheus, Solomon, John the Evangelist, Tree of Life</keywords>

--- Page 78 ---
<meta>Continuity: Following the previous discussion on the divine spirit animating the human body through the heart, Fludd now elaborates on the nature of Wisdom (Sapientia) as both an invisible cosmic force and a visible spiritual reality, equating it with the "Spiritual Rock" and the "Bread of Life."</meta>

### On the Conflict, Book I.

*In her right hand is length of days, in her left hand are riches and honor; her ways are ways of pleasantness, and all her paths are peace. She is a tree of life to those who lay hold of her; finally, everyone who holds her fast is blessed.* <note>Proverbs 3:16–18</note> *By Wisdom Jehovah founded the earth, he established the heavens; the depths were broken up, and the clouds above drop down dew, etc.* <note>Proverbs 3:19–20</note> Where, by the "heavens established by Wisdom," the Wise Man <note>Referring to Solomon</note> intends Wisdom herself in her expanded and invisible nature—as a thing filling and giving form to invisible reality—while by the "earth," he proves her contracted nature. Concerning this, the Prophet <margin>Isaiah 45</margin> speaks: *Let the earth be opened, and let it bring forth a Savior, etc.* <note>Isaiah 45:8</note> For this aforementioned passage of the Wise Man agrees perfectly with that of the Prophet: *Drop down dew, ye heavens, from above, and let the clouds rain down the Just One; let the earth be opened and sprout a Savior.* Similarly, the Wise Man intends this visible gift when he speaks in these words: <margin>Wisdom 9</margin> *Who has known the counsel of God, unless God has given Wisdom? Thus the paths of those on earth were corrected, and men were taught the things that please God, etc.* <note>Wisdom 9:17–18</note> But that such Wisdom, or such a gift granted by God to Solomon, would also at some time be visible to human eyes, is clearly taught in the 7th chapter of Wisdom from verse 22, where it is declared to be the *brightness of eternal light*, to be preferred far above the Sun or stars in clarity. 

We do not intend the corporeal Christ <note>Fludd distinguishes between the historical, physical Jesus and the eternal, cosmic "Christ" or Logos</note> who was subject to death, of whom it is also written that he was <margin>Luke 4:1</margin> *full of the Holy Spirit.* Rather, we mean the Christ who is the **Spiritual Rock**, the true life of the world, who (witness the Apostle) <margin>Ephesians 4; Ephesians 3:9; 1 Corinthians 8</margin> *fills all things and through whom God created all things.* For he is the true Light of the world; indeed, we mean that life-giving Spirit by means of which the dead Christ was made into the **Spiritual Rock** through a true resurrection, for the consolation of honorable Christians—a Rock which shall remain forever. This is not common bread, which is subject to change and corruption, but the *Bread descending from heaven*, and *that hidden manna*, and *that white stone*, and *that morning star*, and *that tree of life placed in the middle of Paradise, which is promised to the one who overcomes.* <note>These are all alchemical and mystical symbols drawn from the Book of Revelation, signifying spiritual perfection.</note> Wherefore, one must "overcome" by working with the Spirit and by knocking with the hands, and by removing the filth of sin and darkness, so that the glory of truth may appear visibly in its beauty, both inwardly and outwardly. 

The Apostle also seems to admonish us of this in these words: <margin>1 Thessalonians 4:10</margin> *We ask you, brothers, that you abound more, and that you strive to be quiet and to do your own business, and to work with your hands as we commanded you, so that you may desire nothing from anyone, etc.* And **Morienus**, <note>Morienus Romanus, a legendary hermit and alchemist often cited in Hermetic literature</note> that good but shrewd hermit, wrote: *He who has everything with him needs no outside help.* Furthermore, that this **Spiritual Gem** is visible to those who take hold of it, the word of the Evangelist proves: *That which was from the beginning, which we have heard and seen with our eyes, which we have looked upon and our hands have handled, concerning the Word of life—and the life was made manifest, and we have seen and bear witness and announce to you the life, etc.* <note>1 John 1:1–2</note> This does not seem to be understood regarding the corporeal Christ, who was subject to death (since he says *that which was from the beginning, and our hands have handled*), but concerning the **Spiritual Christ**, <margin>1 Epistle of John 1</margin> by means of whom that mortal man was raised from death into a spiritual rock; I speak of the life-giving Spirit, which he afterwards depicts in a visible form thus: *God is Light and in Him there is no darkness at all.* 

And this is the reason why Christ addresses his disciples in this thread of discourse: <margin>Luke 10:23</margin> *Blessed are the eyes that see what you see; for I tell you that many prophets and just men have desired to see what you see and did not see it, and to hear what you hear and did not hear it, etc.* This cannot be understood regarding the corporeal Christ, whom many thousands of men both saw and heard; but our Savior was occupied with two things in this world: namely, in preaching the Gospel of the kingdom (in which doctrine he spoke openly to the disciples, but in parables to the common people) and in opening the mystery of the kingdom of God to the worthy. Hence he said elsewhere: <margin>Matthew 13:11</margin> *To you it is given to know the mystery of the kingdom of heaven, but to them it is not given. Therefore I speak to them in parables, because seeing they do not see, and hearing they do not hear, etc.* Finally, as we have said according to the teaching of the Wise Man, Wisdom and the Spirit must be given from the high heavens, which he describes as a holy emanation brighter than the Sun and stars, which is to be expected solely <margin>Wisdom 9</margin> from the hands of God, so that men, enriched by its possession, may understand what is pleasing to God. Thus indeed, the man Christ, most full of such divinity, in this way...

<summary>This page discusses Divine Wisdom as a dual nature—both an invisible cosmic force and a visible spiritual "Gem" or "Rock." Fludd distinguishes between the physical person of Jesus and the eternal "Spiritual Christ," emphasizing that spiritual sight is a gift reserved for the worthy who "work with their hands" to remove the filth of sin.</summary>

# On Wisdom and Folly

[In the per]son of that divinity, he addresses his chosen ones: *I will send the promise of my Father* <margin>Luke 24:49</margin> *upon you; but stay in the city until you are clothed with power from on high.* And elsewhere: *I will ask the* <margin>John 14:15</margin> *Father, and he will give you another Counselor <term>Paraclete: from the Greek 'Parakletos', meaning an advocate, helper, or comforter</term> to remain with you forever, the Spirit of truth, whom the world cannot receive, because it neither sees him nor knows him; but you know him, for he will dwell with you and will be in you, etc.* The Acts of the Apostles teach us that this Counselor Spirit will appear in a visible form. Therefore, Saint John <margin>1 John 1</margin> did not understand the passage cited above to refer to the physical body of Christ, but to that Spirit of Wisdom with which he himself was filled. *It is the Spirit,* (he says) *who gives life; the flesh* <margin>John 6:63</margin> *profits nothing.* Therefore, Christ was not merely fleshly, but Spiritual, dwelling in a "house of flesh"; he fills, animates, and gives life by his presence not only to the earthly globe, but to the entire Spiritual world and all things. Through this presence, Christ the man—rising from death as the first of mortals—left us the hope of rising again and of attaining Salvation through the resurrection.

Thus, you have here the universal Soul of the World <note>original: "universalem mundi animam". This is a central concept in Fludd's philosophy, where a single divine spirit gives life to the entire cosmos.</note> giving life to all things within it; I mean God, Christ, who (as the Apostle witnesses) *gives life to all things and works all in all, and* <margin>1 Timothy 6; 1 Corinthians 2; 2 Corinthians 5</margin> *from whom are all things; for God is one. From him, through him, and in him are all things* (says Paul), to whom be honor and praise. You have, I say, that divine Spirit, lacking nothing, who gives life and inspiration and all things to everyone; in whom we live, move, <margin>Romans 11:32</margin> and have our being, who (according to the Apostle) *will be all in all* <margin>Acts 17; 1 Corinthians 15</margin> when Christ has offered up the kingdom of the Father. This, therefore, is the life of the world, the true Christ God, the true Light, the true *Word <note>original: "Verbum", referring to the Logos or the creative utterance of God</note>, without whom nothing was made.* He is the very same Wisdom coming forth from the mouth of the Most High, the Spiritual rock, the Cornerstone which the builders rejected, which has become the head of the corner. This is from Jehovah, <margin>Psalm 118:22; Romans 9:33; Isaiah 18:16; Wisdom 16:20</margin> *and it is marvelous in our eyes;* a stone of stumbling and a rock of offense, and everyone who believes in him shall not be put to shame.

The true bread descending from heaven is here, the food of Angels: *that mystical Wisdom which* (as the Apostle witnesses) *God predestined in the glory of the chosen and the Apostles.* The mystical and Spiritual body of Christ, the spiritual <margin>1 Corinthians 10</margin> cliff or rock from which they ate and drank. For the Rock was Christ. That sincere and uniquely Spiritual, essential, and real Sacrament—it is not, however, a sacrificial host made of common bread, nor wine pressed from corruptible and transitory grapes. Rather, it is the mystical Bread, or the food of Angels, and the mystical wine which is drunk in the kingdom of heaven, concerning which the Savior says: *I will not drink henceforth of this fruit of the vine until that day when I drink it with you in my Father’s kingdom.* From this, it is clear that this wine does not come from a mortal parent, but a Spiritual one; for as it is said elsewhere, flesh and blood shall not enter the kingdom, and elsewhere: it is the Spirit that gives life, but the flesh profits nothing. Thus, much less shall common bread or common wine—which are subject to alteration and change—enter the kingdom of heaven. Therefore, Christ undoubtedly meant that mystical bread which came down from heaven, and such wine as the Israelites ate and drank while they were under the cloud, as was said above. But if it is argued that the Savior spoke of the "fruit of the vine," and consequently of the common vine and common wine, I respond with the words of the Savior himself: *I am the* <margin>John 15:1</margin> *true vine, and my Father is the vinedresser, etc.* From these words, it is right to gather which vine the Savior meant, and consequently which wine—namely, the mystical vine and such Bread.

For it is observed throughout the whole mystery of the New Testament that Christ sometimes speaks in the person of his humanity, and quite often in the person of his divinity and internal mystery, which was the true Light of the world, in which the life of the world consists. For this reason, he is called by the Apostle: that God-formed <note>original: "Deiformis"</note> Spirit both within and without <margin>1 Corinthians 2</margin> man, the mystical Wisdom: *We speak the wisdom of God in a mystery,* he says, *which is hidden.* Furthermore, he himself teaches us that the letter kills, but the Spirit gives life. And Christ says: *speak in parables.* By these things we are taught that the bread and wine of the Lord’s Supper were Spiritual, and that the Body was that of the mystical Christ, or the Spiritual rock, which was the *true Bread descending from heaven.* It was not those sacrificial hosts used by the Romans, which, after their ceremonial and external consecrations, are nonetheless subject to alteration and corruption no differently than they were before; for if they were put to the fire...

<summary>Fludd argues that Christ is the mystical "World-Soul" and "Spiritual Rock" rather than a merely physical being. He interprets the Eucharist not as common bread and wine (criticizing the Roman Catholic view of transubstantiation), but as a spiritual, incorruptible substance identical with Divine Wisdom.</summary>

<keywords>Paraclete, Holy Spirit, World-Soul, Anima Mundi, Mystical Wisdom, Spiritual Rock, Eucharist, True Vine, Logos, Robert Fludd, Anti-Catholic polemic</keywords>

<meta>Continuity: This page continues Robert Fludd’s defense of the "World Soul" (anima mundi), identifying it with the Holy Spirit and the Spiritual Body of Christ. The previous page ended with the idea that Christ gives life to all things; this page begins by contrasting the physical elements of the Eucharist with the eternal, spiritual reality of the Word.</meta>

# On the Contest, Book II.

[...are] entrusted, they are consumed, which does not happen regarding the Bread of Life <note>original: "Pane vitæ"</note> descending from heaven, who, insofar as he is the Word, will remain for eternity untouched, and immune to any alteration or the trial of fire. Nor yet do I doubt that among the number of the ancient Fathers in the Church there were some wealthy in this kind of real Sacrament, from which it arose that by its power they performed miracles stupendous to themselves and incredible to the men of this age: and we say that the host commonly used is nothing but a type <note>original: "typus"</note> of the true rock and Spiritual body of Christ, but not the Essential reality itself, as has been more clearly demonstrated from the above. But indeed in our British Churches the matter is handled far otherwise, because they distribute bread and wine in their Lord’s Suppers in memory of his body and blood suffering death, who first offered to the world, through the mediation of that divine Spirit, and revealed the mystery of the resurrection; and who by his resurrection (that life-giving Spirit assisting) is to us sinners believing in him the bread of life, the Spiritual rock, and the perfect hope of future resurrection in us: nor indeed are we accustomed to do this without a due preceding consecration.

These things indeed the persistence of *Mersenne* <note>Marin Mersenne (1588–1648) was a French philosopher and priest who was a major intellectual rival of Fludd, attacking Fludd's chemical and mystical theories as unscientific or heretical.</note> compels me to relate to you—a persistence which has incited me to defend the true soul of the world <note>original: "veram mundi animam"</note> against him who denies it, and to speak so many words concerning its ineffable power in the world, both for completing the momentary life and that eternal life, even beyond the duty of a Physician. I would not wish you to say that I, like the cobbler, have now overstepped my limits and my last <note>This refers to the Latin proverb "Ne sutor ultra crepidam" (let the cobbler not go beyond his shoe-last), meaning one should stay within their own area of expertise. Fludd, a physician, is defending his right to speak on theology and physics.</note>, since we learn from the Apostles themselves and especially from *Luke the Evangelist*, that a true physician ought to look to the care of both the soul and the body. And in truth, we Christians, instructed from the Holy Bibles, cannot fundamentally act concerning the true soul of the world if we do not look toward the internal and living acts of God who gives life to all things, just as they are handed down to us by the most holy page. Nor indeed was it, or will it be, my purpose to act regarding any controversy in religion. But only so that I might sustain the integrity of the soul of each world against one who denies it, from the foundation of truth. 

Oh, that some true Christian were so inspired by God, and animated and governed by the power of that Spirit of Wisdom, that he might be able to introduce peace and unanimity among all the sects of religion—which differ so monstrously from one another—after the image of the Father in heaven, who is one in essence; or of Wisdom, which is everywhere yet single and simple; or of truth, which is sincere and indivisible, and consequently the only and solid foundation of all Religions and of the life of the world. But since this is not in the power of any mortal man, however enlightened, to effect and bring to a happy end, God, who is just and almighty, will without doubt out of his mercy impose an end to this evil in his own time; for as the Prophet witnesses <margin>Dan. 2.</margin> *a stone cut from the mountain without hands shall strike the statue on its iron feet and crush all things, and at last that stone which had struck the statue shall be made into a single great mountain that will fill the entire earth,* by means of which justice, the true and only knowledge of the living God, and the true worship of religion and divine science shall be held in price and most worthy honor throughout the universal world (according to the presage of the holy page). *Amen.*

On the Contest of Wisdom with Folly

---

# BOOK THREE.

## IN WHICH ROBERT FLUDD PROVES THAT THE RASH AND ILL-CONSIDERED CENSURE BY MARIN MERSENNE REGARDING HIS PHYSICAL AND TECHNICAL HISTORY OF THE MICROCOSM IS UNJUST, ERRONEOUS, AND FALSE, AND TURNS HIS IMPIOUS AND UNJUST CALUMNIES BACK UPON THE DETRACTOR HIMSELF.

### CHAP. I.

->*In which Fludd is sharply accused of Magic by Mersenne, and finally the malice and insults of Mersenne himself (hardly to be endured by an upright and innocent man) are turned back upon the slanderer: and the detractor, with his deceits full of malignancy, is exposed to the world and deservedly punished.*<-

<image-desc>Ornamental woodcut initial 'Q' featuring a central avian figure surrounded by scrollwork and foliage inside a square border.</image-desc>How great is the envy of this world toward the lovers of virtue and true science, and how great is the hatred (just men, benevolent readers); I believe the persecution of truth can remain hidden from no one truly knowledgeable, since we have this very thing confirmed by the Sage <note>Fludd refers here to Solomon, traditionally credited with the book of Ecclesiastes.</note>, namely, that in much knowledge there is much indignation; whence it has come to pass that he who adds knowledge, also adds sorrow. Lo and behold, I bear witness to a man tossing in the waves of deep sciences, and desiring to immerse himself in the liquid of Wisdom, agitated by the emulation and envy of others (preceded by no just reason), and endangered on all sides by the waves of a slanderous sea: and unless truth stood by me in place of a shield and a bronze wall <note>original: "muri ahenei"—a classical metaphor for an impenetrable defense or clear conscience.</note>, it would be most difficult to find where I might turn, or by what means I might redeem myself, surrounded as I am by the snares of the malevolent. 

Johannes Kepler (a man indeed not only learned but also modest) attacked my world harmony and also my Physics, not with brawling, quibbling, or insults, but as befits a Philosopher, with reasons and sufficiently valid arguments: But he at last, quieted by our reply, restrained himself and did not proceed into further dispute. Now this Monk <note>Marin Mersenne (1588–1648), a Minim friar and priest. Fludd uses "Monk" here with a tone of dismissiveness toward Mersenne's religious station compared to his perceived lack of philosophical charity.</note> rises against me; a man swelling more with the outrages of his tongue—or rather his pen—with quarrels and restlessness, than with reason or arguments of any value. This man, as you see, cries out, slanders, fashions monstrous fabrications, and as far as it concerns me, there is almost nothing of truth in him. He is accustomed to refute merely by denying, but he neither brings forth nor proves anything of importance on the contrary side, as befits a Philosopher. Like some God he speaks, and immediately (giving no other reason) believes that what has been said or affirmed by him is without controversy true, as if the man's mouth were to be esteemed by men as an infallible oracle. 

For example, he says my Physics and my worldly harmony are of no weight but are false; yet he is entirely ignorant of how to bring forward a just cause against them. Similarly, he calls me an impious Magician <note>Mersenne famously labeled Fludd an "evil magician" and a "heretic" in his 1623 work *Quaestiones celeberrimae in Genesim*.</note>, yet he knows not how to return any other suitable reason for such a rash judgment of his, except because I either expressed the names of some Angels in my Writings, or I put forward the superstitious method of the ancient magicians in acquiring their genius, not so that it should be held in any value by Christians, but so that it might be esteemed by them as a diabolical ceremony and of no weight, as will be more clearly described below. Suspicion of Atheism...

Fludd specifically addresses and dismisses Mersenne's accusations of magic and heresy.</summary>

<meta>Continuity: Fludd continues his defense against Marin Mersenne, transitioning from the general introduction of Book Three to specific grievances regarding accusations of heresy and black magic. He identifies the root of the conflict as intellectual jealousy from Mersenne and Johannes Kepler.</meta>

73.
On the Contest, Book III.

---

attempts to cast suspicion upon me because I called the soul in some manner mortal <note>Fludd often distinguished between the immortal "mind" (mens) and the lower "soul" (anima) or vital spirit, which he viewed as being subject to the corruption of the body.</note>, yet he can neither assign nor prove any just reason for that calumny. He values my Cabala <note>The "Cabala" refers to Fludd's Christianized version of Jewish mystical traditions, which he used to interpret the hidden workings of God in nature.</note> as nothing, as something obscure; and then he concludes that whether it be true or false, it should by no means be read. Indeed, he is unable to produce any other reason why it should be condemned. He relegates my entire *History of the Microcosm* to exile because I speak in it of a "genius" <note>original: "genio"; here referring to a guardian spirit or guiding intelligence assigned to an individual.</note> proper to each person, and because I produce the names of Angels within it. He attempts to bring forward infinite other useless and futile things against my good name and reputation. All of these, as I hope, I shall turn into chimerical smoke in your sight—not by the power of "Cacomagy" <note>term: Cacomagia (from Greek 'kakos' and 'mageia'). Meaning "evil magic" or black magic, as opposed to "Theosophia" or divine wisdom.</note>, but by the assistance of true magic or Wisdom <note>original: "Sophia"</note>—since they are clearly of no value and are born of an impious and lying seed.

But first, I shall set about uncovering the solid root of the malevolence and contention of this little friar <note>original: "Religiosuli," a diminutive and disparaging term for a member of a religious order, here referring to Mersenne.</note>. Look, therefore, at what kind of men these are who have acted so strongly against my physical and harmonic science. Specifically, the first was Johannes Kepler, who produced a full discourse on worldly Harmony, which he published in the year 1620, *after* my *Physics* and *Macrocosmic Harmony*. After him, this Marin Mersenne arose, who published both a Physical History of the world and a Harmony of the same (in the Greek and Hebrew manner, as he says) in the year 1623. However, the fact that both my Physical and Harmonic as well as my Technical History of the world were published in the year 1617 is clearly proven by the edition of our first volume. Here then is the grief, there are the tears; from this comes the origin of that twin-headed contention. Those who read it will perceive that I made no mention of them, whether in my *Physics* or my *Worldly Harmony*, nor did I take any occasion against their writings. Therefore, they began this litigious matter against me unexpectedly, in place of the true Harmony which we had at hand. Whence, as it seems to me, the occasion of this sort of contention (for I confess I am entirely ignorant of any other reason) is perhaps because they, hearing of the good reputation of my works published to the world, and thus thinking that their own works—sounding off on the same subject—might perhaps be of less value and esteem in the world than mine, they therefore attempted (in the manner of skilled painters) to smear the exteriors or margins of their own harmony's proportions with a darker and more abject color. This was done so that they might appear more pleasant and visible to the eyes of the world: I say, he attempted to hide my Harmony with a veil of absurd ignorance and to hide its face (hardly perceived at first glance) with the darkness of envy, and to stain the author himself with detractions. He hoped that by this obscuring of our labors, his own works (indeed, the works of both of them) might be rendered clearer and more splendid to the world. And this is the whole reason why this latter man is turned toward such great malice against me. But I shall depict his ill-heaped cleverness and his unworthy and useless deceits with vivid colors in this little book: for he shall not bear his injuries against me with impunity. Wherefore, we briefly begin a strict investigation and examination of his text as follows.

How strenuously this Monk, destitute of a good conscience, attempts in many places of his volume to have Fludd regarded as a "Cacomagus" <note>An evil magician or practitioner of black magic.</note>. For in *Column 1744* he says: *So that you might perform better, Fludd, summon a Genius or a Demon, for you teach the form for summoning this, etc.* And in *Column 1743*: *That Robert Fludd, the heretical-magician, seems to me to be insane, etc.* And in *Column 40*, in Problem 5: *O the wonderful blindness of the man, who with impunity not only treats of such foul and horrendous magic, but also etc.* And in the same place: *Will anyone call you a Catholic, James, as long as you see nefarious books and magicians roaming in your kingdom? etc.* <note>Mersenne is here addressing King James I of England, Fludd’s patron, questioning the King's religious commitment for allowing Fludd's works to circulate.</note> And in *Column 222*, in Problem 5: *In which place that Robert betrays himself to be enough of a magician, or to write magical things, etc.* Furthermore, in many other places he seems to accuse Fludd of the vice of depraved magic: but whether justly and deservedly or not, will appear from what follows.

<sig>k</sig>
<meta>catchword: I. Marin's [I. Marini]</meta>
