<meta>Continuity: This page continues the confrontation between Robert Fludd (Robertus de Fluctibus) and his critic Marin Mersenne (referred to here as Marinus). Having previously defended his views on the soul and "Cacomagy" (black magic), Fludd now addresses Mersenne’s specific attacks on his "History of the Microcosm" and his theories regarding guardian spirits and angels.</meta>

# On Wisdom with Folly

<meta>catchword: Robert’s</meta>

### 1. Text of Mersenne. Col. 222. In Pr. v.

Robert Fludd <note>original: "Robertus de Fluctibus"</note> says that the Great Magus warned Mark Antony, who was joined in a singular friendship with Augustus, to flee from him, saying: *“For although you are older in years, and more excellent in the experience of things, and even more illustrious in lineage, and commander of many, and have endured many wars, nevertheless your guardian spirit <term>genius: in the ancient world, a 'genius' was a protective spirit or guiding intelligence assigned to a person at birth</term> dreads the genius of this young man; your fortune flatters his, and unless you flee far away, your fortune seems destined to fail entirely before his.”* Such, says Fludd, were without doubt the Genii of Pompey, Caesar, Darius, and Alexander the Great. In this passage, that Robert betrays that he is quite the Magician or that he writes magical things—so much so that the second volume of his *History of the Microcosm* seems to be entirely Magical. This is most clearly evident from a reading of the fourth Problem, Section 2, Tract 1, Book 3, in which he teaches the method by which the proper Genius of each individual can be found. Indeed, Chapter 13 contains the words cited. But eventually, God willing, we shall deal more extensively with his impious errors when we treat of Prophecy, divinations, and Angels.

### 2. Text of Mersenne Col. 40. in Pro. v.

Robert Fludd, the Physician from Oxford, fell into exactly the same error as the Venetian <note>original: "Veneto"; likely referring to Francesco Giorgio (1460–1541), a Franciscan friar and Christian Cabalist whose works Fludd admired</note> in Tract 1 of his *Genethlialogy* <note>term: Genethlialogy; the branch of astrology dealing with the casting of birth charts to determine a person's destiny</note>, Section 2, Part 4, Book 3, Chapter 5. There, he wrongly thinks that Adam lived in familiar conversation with Raziel as with a guardian Angel, just as Shem did with Jophiel, Abraham with Zadkiel, Jacob with Peliel, and Joseph with Gabriel (about whom it is written in Genesis 48), who also presided over Joshua, Daniel, and Mary; and Metatron over Moses; Elias with Maltiel, David with Cerviel, Ezra with Uriel, Solomon with Michael, and Tobit with Raphael.
This is the "distinguished doctrine" which Fludd will soon propagate—unless he seriously repents—before being submerged in eternal floods <note>original: "fluctibus"; Mersenne is making a mocking pun on Fludd's Latinized name, "de Fluctibus" (of the floods)</note>; perhaps because Agrippa <note>Heinrich Cornelius Agrippa (1486–1535), a famous occult philosopher</note> had not yet revealed the fellowship of Demons clearly enough.

### 3. Text of Mersenne Col. 222. In Pro. v.

Meanwhile, those names of Angels and Demons of which Holy Scripture makes no mention must be exploded <note>original: "explodenda"; in the sense of being hissed off a stage or rejected</note> as having been fashioned according to one's whim.

### 4. Text of Mersenne 40. In Prob. v.

And indeed, while for others it is enough to set one Angel and one Demon over one man, he [Fludd] wants to set one royal and principal Archangel over the mind, another "Olympic" spirit over the vital soul, and a third "Elementary" spirit over the body. Why does he not admit as many Demons into one man as there are in the whole world, since, as he himself confesses, man has drawn a certain portion from all parts of the world, on account of which he is called a Microcosm? <note>term: Microcosm; the "little world." The philosophical idea that a human being is a miniature map or reflection of the entire universe (the Macrocosm).</note> Would he not have earned far greater praise from the Devils if he had proposed ethereal and fiery Demons for the vital spirit and natural heat; airy ones for the blood, lungs, and breathing; aquatic ones for phlegm; terrestrial ones for melancholy; and for the imagination, ghosts and spirits of the dead, and Harpies, and Fates, Furies, Gorgons, Lamias, Centaurs, Hippocentaurs, Arimaspians, Dog-milkers <note>original: "Cynomolgas"; a mythical race of people with dog-heads</note>, Satyrs, Fauns, Nymphs, or a crowd of the rest, whether real or fictitious?

->O the wonder of man's blindness, that in Europe, among Christians, he has dared<-
->not only to treat of such foul and horrendous magic with impunity,<-
->but even impudently to<-
->release it into the light.<-

<header>On the Contest, Book III.</header>

### An Examination of Robert’s Quoted Passages.

Indeed, from these contexts of his, it is possible to gather the mind of this giant-like man—what sort of mind it is, and how it is disposed toward me (a miserable pygmy) as I fall into the deepest dangers and snares of such a philosopher and Catholic; or rather, of an eminent slanderer and impostor. Is he not like the one who said, *“I shall ascend into the heavens, and I will be like the Most High”*? <note>original: "ascendam in cælos, & ero similis altissimo"; a reference to the fall of Lucifer in Isaiah 14:14.</note> But nothing that is merely "like" something is the same as that thing. For the Most High said, *“Let it be, and immediately they were made.”* <note>original: "fiat & protinus sunt facta."</note> This impostor says "let it be," or "it is," as if his will stood in place of reason; and yet, when his speech is done, there is almost nothing in what he says. At first glance, it seems to be something, but in the end, nothing at all appears. He is like a boastful mist <note>original: "Nebula... Thrasonica." "Thrasonical" refers to Thraso, a braggart soldier in Roman comedy.</note> (not to call him a scoundrel <note>original: "nebulo"; Fludd is punning on "nebula" (mist) and "nebulo" (a worthless person or scoundrel).</note>), which will be suddenly reduced to nothing by the appearance of the solar truth. For by truth, as if by a Lydian stone <term>Lydian stone: a touchstone used in antiquity to test the purity of gold</term>, virtue is distinguished from vice, light from darkness, and God from the Devil. But the outcome will prove the deeds.

You see, O men most adorned with justice, that the whole aim of this monk <note>referring to Marin Mersenne, who was a member of the Order of Minims.</note> is to drag Robert Fludd into the circle of magic, or rather of Evil Magic <term>Cacomagia: from the Greek 'kakos' (bad/evil) and 'magia' (magic)</term>, by his false slanders, accusations, and vain curses. But he will find Fludd’s conscience to be firmer than can be shaken or brandished by his futile little arguments and false aspersions.

#### The reasons by which Mersenne strives to defile Fludd with the suspicion of Evil Magic in the texts cited above—contaminating the truth with a lie—are six, of which the first is:

->*First.*<-
->*Because he introduces the example of the magus who gave magical counsel to Mark Antony.*<-

->*Second.*<-
->*Because he himself confirms this counsel by assigning to Pompey, Caesar, Alexander, and Darius—to each of them, namely—his own proper Genius <term>Genius: a personal protective spirit or guiding intelligence assigned to a person at birth</term>.*<-

->*Third.*<-
->*Inasmuch as Fludd, through the whole of his third book (Part 4, Section 2, Treatise 1 of his Microcosmic History), teaches the method of acquiring each person's own Genius; from which Mersenne also concludes that, for the sake of that one book, that entire Volume ought to be considered Magical.*<-
->He assigns these reasons in his 1st text.<-

->*Fourth.*<-
->*Because he assigns three demons or Angels to every man.*<-
->This reason is found in the 4th text.<-

->*Fifth.*<-
->*Because he names the Angels with fictitious names that have no significance, which are also not found in the Scriptures.*<-
->This reason is included in the 2nd and 3rd texts.<-

->*Sixth.*<-
->*Because he assigns a proper Angel to every Patriarch and Prophet, and indeed even to every individual man.*<-
->And this reason is contained in the 2nd text.<-

To each of these, therefore, we shall respond in their order, and (with God assisting) we shall prove them to be vain and of no value.

To the first, therefore, we say: before Robert is condemned as an Evil Magician for this reason, we think it would be worthwhile first to consider what the word "Magic" truly means, and consequently what that "Magical" thing is. This must be especially noted: that if a single species of Magic...

<meta>Continuity: On the previous page, Fludd began his rebuttal against Marin Mersenne, who had accused him of being a sorcerer and a fraud. He continues here by defining the true nature of Magic as Wisdom, distinguishing it from the "diabolical" arts his critic associates with the term.</meta>

# On Wisdom with Folly

If we were to completely exclude this appearance from our schools, we would be removing the most excellent portion of all natural philosophy. Therefore, it is the mark of a wise man to inquire into what a thing is before rashly condemning it. It is a common opinion, then, that this word, **Magic**, is a Persian term, meaning the same thing in the Persian language as **Wisdom** <note>original: "Sapientia"</note> does among the Latins. And although the nature of that word has been corrupted by the impiety of men and defiled by diabolical filth (inasmuch as it confuses human wisdom, which is false, vain, and superstitious, with that which is divine), there is nonetheless only one certain Wisdom, one true Magic, which is simple and always tending toward the good. This true magic is occupied with natural things and acts powerfully and miraculously within them; and this kind of Wisdom is that very **Spirit** of which we have made mention so many times in our description of the **Soul of the World** <term>Soul of the World: "Anima Mundi," the concept that a spiritual essence infuses all of nature</term>. 

It was by means of that magic or Wisdom, I say, that Solomon confessed he possessed knowledge and understanding of the fabric of the world, the forces of the elements, the changing of the seasons, the position of the stars, the natures of living creatures, the fury of wild beasts, the power of the winds, the properties of roots and plants, and even the thoughts of men, and all things both hidden and manifest. It was in such true Magic, therefore, that Solomon was well-versed.

<margin>Wisdom of Solomon 7.</margin> Should he not, then, and rightly so, be held and esteemed as a magus himself? Just as those **three Wise Men** <note>original: "tres illi Sapientes," referring to the Magi of the Nativity</note>, who came from the East to Judea for the purpose of worshipping the recently born Christ, are frequently called "magi" by the Holy Scriptures because of their singular skill in this science. Does Mersenne know whether that diviner cited by Plutarch and mentioned by Fludd belonged to such a society of Magi or not? Certainly, if we look at the core of the matter: it is not within the Devil's power to detect a thing so secret and hidden for Mark Antony as was revealed to him by that Magus. <margin>Wisdom of Solomon 7.</margin> *“By Wisdom,”* says Solomon, *“I had knowledge of the thoughts of men, and of all things, both hidden and manifest.”* 

Was it therefore impossible for that Magus to see such a great matter in that light? Is the Devil not a liar, with no truth in him? What, then, is to be expected from the Devil except the false and the deceptive? Robert <note>Robert Fludd refers to himself in the third person here</note> accepts the man as such a Magus; and Robert desires to be such a Magus rather than merely to be considered one. If Mersenne understands Robert to be such a magus, then all that ill-will of Robert's toward him will be turned into friendship and pity. But if he asserts that Robert is a **Cacomagus** <term>Cacomagus: an "evil magician" or sorcerer who works with demons</term>, and has ever been involved in magic that is either poisonous or necromantic, I reply boldly that Mersenne has brought forth a monstrous and absurd lie against Robert. Consequently, we boldly affirm that he acts on behalf of the Devil and not God, or the true Catholic Church (however much he seems to boast of himself in vain).

<margin>To the second [reason]</margin> Regarding his second reason, I confess that I have by no means denied that not only Pompey, Caesar, Alexander, and Darius, but also all others living in the world, are presided over by a certain **Angelic Genius** <term>Genius: a tutelary or guardian spirit assigned to a person at birth</term> who assists them in the necessary affairs of their lives—and this with all suspicion of "evil magic" set aside and excluded. This I also hope to sustain and prove vigorously against the vain and tasteless quibbles of Mersenne, when I come down to the examination of his fourth and final reason.

<margin>To the third [reason]</margin> But now it seems to me that Mersenne, nodding to himself and silently muttering, says: "But how will he avoid the third reason, where he spoke openly and used not only the magical names of Angels, but even their characters, for the discovery of the Genius?" To this, Fludd is not at all afraid to respond with reciprocal derision. In every work, the **intention of the worker** must be observed: namely, whether that work is intended for good or for evil. It must be seen, therefore, whether Fludd, in an effort to establish or defend superstitious Magic, or rather by way of exposing its errors and vanity, brought it into the

[one] should bring the matter to light before proceeding to a rash judgment. Therefore, let those books of Fludd speak and answer for their master regarding the contents of this case.

<margin>Chapter 1, book 1.</margin>
In the beginning of that section where he deals with Natal Astrology, he speaks specifically in these words: *The fourth and last species of Astrology is called <term>genethlialogical</term> <note>original Greek: γενεθλιαλογικὴ; the study of birth charts to predict a person's destiny</note>, which consists in the fashioning of characters, seals, and statues, the wondrous effects of which are celebrated in many kinds of histories. But since the diabolical art mixes itself with this—as we very often see a good thing stained and defiled by the abuses of the impious—we shall reject it, etc.* Behold, you have Fludd's very own words, by which it can be clearly enough discerned how far his soul stands from that impious species of Magic, and with what little regard his faith is placed in it. This is discussed more broadly in the following sections, where he first examines the controversy so sharply held between Iamblichus and Porphyry <note>Iamblichus and Porphyry were influential Neoplatonic philosophers of the 3rd and 4th centuries who debated the nature of the soul and divine communication</note> concerning the natal <term>Daemon</term> <note>original: "Dæmone"; in this context, it refers to a guiding spirit or "Genius," not an evil demon</note> or the "Genius" proper to every man. And then afterward, he describes the superstitious inventions of the mages used to identify that Genius and to attain familiarity with it. Finally, at the *end of that small book*, he makes an apologetic conclusion for himself, in which he removes from himself all suspicion of such **Evil Magic** <note>original: "Cacomagiæ"</note>, recognizing it as vain, empty, and futile; there he also explains the reason why he dealt with that discourse in so many words, as will be explained by a more particular inquiry.

<margin>Chapter 1, book 3.</margin>
In the first chapter of the third book, he says: The opinion of Porphyry concerning the acquisition of one's own Genius is recited—namely, that it was a Spirit or Genius proper to each person, proceeding from the Lord of the Nativity (that is, from the stars) and descending to the man. But Iamblichus seems to oppose this, for he denies that the Spirit proper to each person descends from the figure of their birth, but says instead that it flows from a more ancient principle than the stars or the rulers of the stars. Chapter 2 deals with the naming of the Genius, and similarly treats the opinions of Iamblichus and Porphyry concerning the descent of the spirit.

### CHAPTER III.

It is discussed according to their opinions whether the Genius is a part of the soul, and whether fates are to be avoided through knowledge of the Genius.

### CHAPTER IV.

Whether there are multiple Spirits for each person, according to that contention of Iamblichus and Porphyry, is argued.

Whether an evil Spirit or Devil is given to every man, and whether the souls of the impious become Demons after their separation from the body. In this question, testimonies from the sacred text, Hermes Trismegistus, and Porphyry are introduced.

<margin>Part 2, chapter 2.</margin>
Concerning the Angels of the Empyrean heaven, the leaders of the mind, it is discussed according to the opinion of the Hebrews and Abbot Trithemius <note>Johannes Trithemius (1462–1516), a German abbot and polymath known for his works on cryptography and angelic communication</note>, in which their names as uttered by them are also described.

*Chapter 3.* Treats the opinion of ancient and modern Mages concerning the Olympic Spirits.

*Chapter 4.* Shows their characters and natures according to the opinion of the Mages themselves.

# On Wisdom and Folly

If we were to utterly exclude from our schools a subject of such appearance, we would be removing the most excellent portion of all natural Philosophy. Therefore, it is the mark of a Wise Man to inquire into what a thing is before rashly condemning it. It is a common opinion, then, that this word, **Magic**, is a Persian term, sounding in the Persian tongue the same as *Wisdom* does among the Latins. And although the nature of this word has been corrupted by the impiety of men and defiled by Devilish filth (inasmuch as it confuses human wisdom—which is feigned, vain, and superstitious—with that which is divine), there is nevertheless only one certain Wisdom, one true Magic, which is simple and always tending toward the good. This Magic occupies itself with natural things and acts within them powerfully and miraculously; and the Spirit of this kind of Wisdom is that very one of which we have made mention so many times in our description of the <term>soul of the world</term> <note>The "Anima Mundi," a concept Fludd championed suggesting a divine spirit permeates all nature</note>. <margin>Wisdom 7</margin> It is that magic or Wisdom, I say, by means of which Solomon confessed he possessed knowledge and understanding of the world's structure: the forces of the elements, the change of seasons, the position of the stars, the natures of living things, the fury of beasts, the power of winds, the properties of roots and plants, and even the thoughts of men, and all things both hidden and manifest. Solomon, therefore, was practiced in such true Magic.

Should he not, then, and rightly so, be held and esteemed as a mage himself? Just as those three Wise Men, who came from the East to Judea to adore the newly born Christ, are called "Magi" by the Holy Scriptures because of their singular expertise in this science. Does Mersenne even know if that diviner cited by Plutarch and alleged by Fludd belonged to this society of Magi or not? Certainly, if we look at the matter to its very core, it is not within the Devil's power to detect and reveal a matter so secret and hidden to Marc Antony as that which was revealed to him by the Mage. <margin>Wisdom 7</margin> *By Wisdom*, says Solomon, *I had knowledge of the thoughts of men, and of all things, both hidden and manifest.* Was it then impossible for that Mage to see such a great thing in that light? Is the Devil not a liar, in whom there is no truth? What, then, is to be expected from the Devil except the feigned and the deceptive? Indeed, Robert [Fludd] accepts the man as a Mage of this sort; and Robert desires to be such a Mage rather than merely be considered one. If Marin Mersenne understands Robert to be such a mage, then all of Robert's perceived ill-will toward him will be turned into friendship and mercy. But if he asserts that Robert was ever practiced as a <term>Cacomagus</term> <note>A "bad mage" or practitioner of evil, black magic</note>, or in magic that is either venomous or <term>Necromantic</term> <note>original: "Negromantica"; magic involving communication with the dead</note>, I reply boldly that Mersenne has brought forth a monstrous and absurd lie against Robert. Consequently, we boldly affirm that he acts on behalf of the Devil and not God, nor the true Catholic Church (however much he seems to boast of himself in vain).

<margin>To the second</margin> As for his second reason, I confess that I have by no means denied that a certain Angelic <term>Genius</term> <note>A guardian spirit or guiding angel assigned to an individual at birth</note> presides over—and assists in the necessary affairs of life for—not only Pompey, Caesar, Alexander, and Darius, but also all others living in the world; and this is stated with all suspicion of "Cacomagia" set aside and excluded. This I hope to sustain and prove vigorously against the vain and insipid quibbles of Marin [Mersenne], when I descend into the examination of his fourth and final reason.

<margin>To the third</margin> But now Mersenne seems to me, by signing off and silently muttering to himself, to say: "But how will he avoid the third reason, where he spoke openly and used not only the magical names of Angels, but even their characters, to discover the Genius?" But Fludd, with reciprocal derision, is not at all afraid to answer him thus: In every work, the intention of the worker must be observed—namely, whether that work is intended for good or for evil. It must be seen, therefore, whether Fludd, in an effort to establish or defend superstitious Magic, or rather by way of uncovering its errors and vanity, brought it into the light...

*Chapter 9.* Concerning the Angels being more in one region of the earth than in another
<insert>they act according to the institutes of the ancient Magi and Cabalists.</insert>

The remaining chapters treat and discourse upon the method of obtaining one’s own Genius and rendering it familiar, scattered throughout according to the superstitious and ceremonial institute of the ancient Sages.

->On the Contest of Wisdom with Folly<-

But indeed, it is easy to see from the entire doctrine of that same third part that this very superstition of the ancient Magi was entirely contrary to the opinion of Robert Fludd, and most opposed to the truth itself. Finally, since he is to be excused from that superstitious and fantastical Magic and his own empty opinion by every reason, and is to be held as innocent, he purged himself *at the end of the said book* with an Apologetic discourse of this kind. His words are these, *as can be seen at the end of that little book*:

### *Apologetic Epilogue.*

> Do not believe (kind Reader) that I deserve the name of an impious <term>Magus</term> <note>original: "Magi"; here referring to a practitioner of forbidden or demonic arts</note> because I have spoken of the names of Spirits and some of their characters. For I would have you know that I have spoken only with the mouths of others, and have committed to letters those things which I myself refuse to follow or to which I deny certain faith. Nevertheless, I wished to treat them in this place, partly for the sake of method, and partly for the reason by which Logicians are accustomed to teach fallacies—namely, not so that they might deceive men, but lest they be deceived, that they might be cautious and forewarned. So also in this place we have depicted the empty or rather uncertain wisdom of the ancients: so that you, instructed in a more certain way, may follow the true, simple, and pious path with better hope and confidence, find the genuine <term>Genius</term> <note>original: "genium"; in this context, a personal guardian spirit or guiding intelligence assigned at birth</note> of each thing, and know the true secret of abstruse Astrology. We have done, therefore, in the preceding part of this book as painters are accustomed to do when they arrange inferior colors at the margin of some distinguished portrait, so that in the inner part of the sketched image, the beauty might be more adorned. Attend therefore (good Reader) and if you will have considered matters diligently as they are, perhaps you will not reject these exhausted labors, nor will you lose your own effort and "oil" <note>An ancient idiom, "to lose oil and labor," meaning to waste one's time and resources on a difficult task</note>. Farewell.

These are the words of Robert Fludd, in which he has sufficiently explained the sincerity of his soul and the aversion of his mind from spurious and superstitious Magic to men endowed with right reason and an upright conscience. But what, I pray, is further to be expected from me by such contentious men (their religion being at odds with them), when he does not in the least blush to bring into suspicion and the crime of <term>Cacomagia</term> <note>original: "Cacomagiæ"; from Greek, meaning "evil magic" or "black magic"</note> even the most grave men of his own religion, who are by far—indeed a thousand times—to be preferred in doctrine and Wisdom? And what is more, he falsely accuses, judges, and condemns Roman Catholics, even those clothed in the Priestly habit and most profound in both mystical Philosophy and occult Theology, of wicked Magic in his rabid and envious way. He glories in publishing and "trumpeting" them as such to the world with <term>Cyclopean impudence</term> <note>monstrous or crude boldness</note>; such men include Thomas Aquinas and Albertus Magnus (*Column 1151*), Roger Bacon (*Column 971*), Marsilio Ficinus (*Column 1704*), and many other very learned and religious men.

Therefore, Fludd shall not be totally dejected in spirit, since he has such learned and innocent men as companions in his condemnation.

<margin>To the Fourth</margin>
To his fourth reason, we say boldly, and it will not be difficult to demonstrate with sufficiently firm arguments, that every human being has his own principal Genius, who also possesses two other Angels serving under him by nature and condition, differing among themselves no differently than the ethereal heaven differs from the elementary. Therefore, we establish a Prince as the presiding guard of the mind and reason, and we prove this against the mind of Mersenne as follows.

What are you doing (O Mersenne), or why do you live among Christian Ecclesiastics when you attempt to persuade the children of Christ that no angelic Genius is present with them? Away with you, devil, who rejects the fellowship of the good Angels! But you will answer that you have God for your leader; why, you will say, what need have I of the assistance of any peculiar Angel? Have you so forgotten the words of the Royal Prophet, by which we are taught that God acts in these lower things—nay indeed, everywhere—through the mediation of Angels, and consequently upon every creature? His words are these: <margin>Psalm 103:20</margin> *Angels of God, most mighty in strength, performing his word, hearkening to the voice of his speech, his ministers doing his pleasure, etc.* By which it may be seen that the Word of God is performed by the mediation of Angels: yet the Word arises immediately from the mouth of Jehovah. Have we not clearly demonstrated in our discourse on the <term>Soul of the World</term> <note>original: "anima mundi"; the concept of a spiritual force that animates all nature</note> that Angels...

### On the Contest, Book III.

[An]gels are said to be called *Divine Images* <note>original: "Agalmata"; a Greek term used by Dionysius to describe angels as "statues" or "mirrors" reflecting God</note> by Dionysius because, since they are created Spirits, they receive an emanation from Jehovah, by means of which they are formed and are seized away to fulfill the will of God. But we have called this emanation—which works all things through the mediation of the Angelic and intellectual Spirit, taken in a general sense—**Wisdom** <note>original: "Sapientiam"</note>. <margin>Ecclesiastes 1</margin> This is why the Wise Man said she was *created before all things.* Insofar as she effects various things in the world specifically through her various rays, according to the divine disposition and will, she is said to have produced and procreated various Angels, distinct in nature and property. 

From this, therefore, it is clear that if this Angelic light is taken away from Marinus <note>referring to Marin Mersenne, Fludd's intellectual rival</note>, it is necessary that he perish entirely. And indeed, let him perish if he holds the minister of his life and reason in contempt; for even if God gives life, He gives it through the ministry of angels. Whence Reuchlin <note>Johannes Reuchlin (1455–1522), a German humanist and scholar of the Kabbalah</note> most rightly says that *an Angel is like a certain middle power between God and nature, in which operations occur in things that nature either would not do, or would do in such a way that others say they come from an "occult property," and others because of such-and-such a cause.* <margin>On the Art of the Kabbalah</margin> These are his words. 

And Rabbi Moses <note>Moses Maimonides, the preeminent medieval Jewish philosopher</note> says that *the active intellect, from which forms flow, is named "Angel," and he says that the governor of the universe is called Metatron* <note>original: "Mitattron"</note>, *by whom indeed all singular human virtues are governed, both animal and natural, which are likewise called Angels, the multitude of which is [vast] to us. These are his words.* From this, it is argued that the intellectual Spirit, insofar as it is illuminated or formed by divine light, becomes of an Angelic composition, and consequently is named an Angel by Him. 

Yet, over all these Angels, He places Metatron as prefect, through whom He proves the Soul of the World, or Wisdom herself, having come forth from the mouth of the Most High and having been immersed into the "moist created Spirit," or infused through the smallest parts. From her and through her are all the virtues of both the microcosm <note>man</note> and macrocosm <note>the universe</note>, which, being diverse and distinct in property, operate in different ways in man: namely, one toward reason, another toward life, and a third toward the vital faculty, as will be discussed more broadly below. 

And this is why the Rabbi in *Bereshith* <note>Hebrew: "In the beginning"; referring to a commentary on Genesis</note> says, *the creator daily creates a heaven of Angels, whom others call "new forms," inasmuch as they are formal substances, with which the entire sphere of things that can be generated and corrupted is full beyond numbering,* etc. What else does this Doctor understand, except that all the souls <note>original: "bhimae"; likely a Latinization of the Hebrew "behemoth" or a similar term for animal life-forces</note> of the world, as well as the angelic virtues, depend on that universal Angel, Wisdom—namely, the one called Jesus and Christ? 

Therefore, Georgius Venetus <note>Francesco Giorgio (1453–1540), a Franciscan friar and Christian Kabbalist</note> speaks most directly on this point (even though Marin Mersenne presumes to falsely accuse and judge him along with the others of his sect) by saying, *Angels find their end in Christ their king, who contains and embraces the nature of both Angels and of God, in whom also all other things find their end, insofar as he is the highest of Angels.* Whence he is called by the Prophet the *Angel of the Covenant.* 

But that all the particular virtues of the world proceed from one universal Spirit is witnessed by the Psalmist in these words: <margin>Psalm 33:6</margin> *By the word of the Lord the heavens were established, and by the Spirit from his mouth, all their virtue,* etc. <note>original: "virtus eorum"; often translated as "their host"</note> Where by "virtues" he understands the angels themselves, the souls of things, and the forms of all things. Hence he says elsewhere: *Who makes your Angels spirits <note>or "winds"</note>, and your ministers a burning flame.* And in the *Gate of Light* <note>original: "Porta Lucis"; referring to the Kabbalistic text 'Sha'are Orah' by Joseph Gikatilla</note> we find that from the earth even unto the firmament there is no empty place, but all things are full of forms, of which some are pure, others impure, foul, and tempters—all of which are ministers of God, ordained either for the beneficence of the good or the punishment of the impious.

But so that we may approach this matter of ours even more closely, leaving behind arguments taken from the treasury-chest of other authors (inasmuch as they have all been quite imprudently condemned by Marinus), we shall take refuge in the testimony of the Holy Bible. We read that the Patriarch Jacob, in his final blessing, fell into words of this sort: <margin>Genesis 48:16</margin> *That Angel, who delivers me from all evil, bless these boys,* etc. From which speech it is clearly evident that Jacob had a prince and guardian Angel, through whose mediation he was preserved from evil and sustained in his ways; and consequently, that preservation and protection from all evil is from a prince and guardian Angel. Furthermore, what does Marinus say...

<summary>This page defends the role of Angels and Divine Wisdom as intermediaries between God and the physical world, citing Christian Kabbalists and Jewish philosophers to argue against Marin Mersenne's skepticism. It identifies the "universal Angel" and "Wisdom" with Christ and asserts that the universe is entirely filled with spiritual forms rather than empty space.</summary>

<meta>Continuity: This page continues Robert Fludd’s defense of his cosmological system against the criticisms of Marin Mersenne (Marinus). In the previous section, Fludd argued that an "Angelic Light," which he identifies with Divine Wisdom, is the mediator of all life and reason. Here, he moves to specific scriptural proofs for the existence of guardian angels and their role within the hierarchical structure of the universe, drawing on the Bible, the Kabbalah, and the writings of Dionysius the Areopagite.</meta>

# On Wisdom with Folly

Does Marinus oppose the Apostles? Does he believe they were left without guardian angels? What, then, is the meaning of that speech by the chosen ones, who were praying to God in the house of Mark? When they were told by a girl that the Apostle Peter was knocking at the gate, they replied: *“You are mad; it is his Angel,” etc.* <note>Acts 12:15. Fludd argues that if the disciples believed Peter had a specific "Angel," they must have been taught this by the Apostles themselves.</note> These disciples of the Apostles would not have wished to utter something of such profanity, as Marinus thinks it, if they had not been previously and sufficiently instructed by their masters, namely the Apostles. Furthermore, <margin>Acts 12:13.</margin> we read in the same chapter that while Peter was thinking about the vision, the Spirit said to him: *“Behold, three men seek you,” etc.* And elsewhere: <margin>Acts 12:7.</margin> *“Behold, the Angel of the Lord stood by him, and a light shone in the room,” etc.* 

But I shall produce a testimony still stronger and more powerful, with which I know that I, a little David, shall lay low the great Marinus—as if he were Goliath—with a stone cast from the depths of reason, prostrating him upon the cold ground of ignorance. From the Evangelist we gather these words, spoken by the very mouth of Christ: *“See that you do not despise one of these little ones; for I tell you that their Angels in heaven always see <margin>Matthew 18:10.</margin> the face of my Father, who is in heaven.”* From this, it is clear first that guardian angels are assigned to every person from birth; and second, that this Angel is one of those seven principal ones, of whom the Sacred Scriptures speak thus: *“I am <margin>Tobit 12:15.</margin> Raphael, one of the seven Angels who stand before Jehovah.”* And elsewhere: <margin>Luke 1:19.</margin> *“I am Gabriel, who stands before God.”* And elsewhere: <margin>Daniel 10:13.</margin> *“Behold, Michael, one of the chief princes, came,” etc.* 

From these passages, it is clear that there are only seven prince-angels, also called Archangels, who stand before the face of God. Yet Christ says, *“for their Angels always see the face of my Father.”* Here, therefore, you have the principal and prince-Angel of every human being, who always stands before the face of God: that is, *one of those seven Spirits* who are said by John in the Revelation to *“stand around the Throne.”* These are the most translucent empyrean Spirits who—according to the <term>Sephirotic Kabbalah</term> <note>Fludd refers to the Sephirot, the ten attributes or emanations in Jewish mysticism through which the Infinite (Ein Sof) reveals itself.</note>—receive emanations from each Divine Name, each with properties distinct from one another. They pour these down step by step into the angels of the lower orders subject to them, who in turn rain down a lucid, active power and force upon others still lower in nature and condition. For just as Jacob saw Angels descending from heaven, so he also saw a ladder built with various rungs, one higher than the next. 

The Archangels, I say, like most highly polished mirrors (as Dionysius testifies <margin>Dionysius, *On Divine Names*, ch. 6.</margin>), receive light immediately from the face of God. Whence such an Angel is defined by him as *“being the image of God and a manifestation of the hidden Light, and a most pure mirror of the Most High, without blemish.”* Do we not also notice that Wisdom is described by Solomon in these very same terms? For he says in express words <margin>Wisdom 7.</margin> that *“she is a certain pure emanation of the glory of the Almighty God, and the brightness of eternal light, and the mirror without blemish of the majesty of God, and the image of His goodness.”* 

Indeed, whatever difference exists between the rays and the sun is the same difference that exists between universal Wisdom and her Angelic lights. Truly, we see one effect of divine emanation in the Empyrean Spirit—namely, rendering it intellectual and bringing it into an Angelic state (whence this is called the intellectual and angelic heaven); we see another in the Ethereal Spirit, insofar as it fills it with the gifts of life; and finally another in the air and the sublunary elements subject to change, to which is attributed natural vegetative power and the virtue of mixed composition. Thus the Archangels, receiving the Light of God immediately and as if intoxicated by divine nectar, pour out their influence into the Angels of the ethereal heaven subject to them, so that these in turn may impart their lucid power to the elemental Angels. For the Spirit of the Empyrean heaven, adorned with divine gifts, is seen to animate the ethereal one, just as by equal reason the ethereal is observed to flow into the elemental every day and every moment, giving the faculty of life, growth, and multiplication. By means of these influences, creatures—whether simple or composite—are finally refreshed. The influx of the Archangel is the life of the Olympic Angel <note>Fludd’s term for angels associated with the planetary spheres</note>, just as the Elemental angel is known to draw its perfection and action from that ethereal or Olympic source.

<meta>catchword: And so [Atq;]</meta>

<summary>Fludd argues for the existence of guardian angels by citing the New Testament and linking them to the seven Archangels of the biblical and Kabbalistic traditions. He describes a hierarchical "ladder" of light where divine energy flows from God through "mirror-like" Archangels down to the celestial and elemental realms.</summary>

In the previous section, Fludd cited the New Testament to argue that every person has a guardian angel. Here, he expands this into a systematic hierarchy of the universe, explaining how divine light and commands descend from the highest "Empyrean" heavens through the planetary and elemental spheres, finally reflecting this same structure within the human body (the Microcosm).</meta>

# On the Contest, Book III.

And this is the reason why, just as the ethereal spirit of the world is subject in dignity and perfection to the Empyrean or angelic spirit, and the elementary spirit is subject to the ethereal, so too are the Olympic spirits <note>spiritus Olympici: In Fludd's system, these are spirits associated with the celestial spheres or planets.</note> subject in power and dignity to those Empyrean spirits, and the elementary spirits to the Olympic. From this, it is clear that the angelic spirits—being more worthy and fuller of divine light—hold dominion over those into whom they pour the light drawn from the divine fountain. Hence, some of the Fathers of Theology conclude that superior angels are masters over the inferior, and they say that angels are more "superior" the closer they are to the source of light. And Dionysius <note>Dionysius the Areopagite, the primary historical source for the "Celestial Hierarchy" of nine choirs of angels.</note> says that the superior angels always teach and lead the inferior. And Gregory <margin>Gregory I, Morals.</margin> says: *“Some of these are closer to God and are occupied in contemplation, while others are sent out to perform their duties, receiving their commands from the superiors.”* <note>original: "Horum alij sunt Deo propinquiores, & in contemplatione versantur, & alij emittuntur ad officia sua præstanda mandata accipientes à superioribus"</note> Dionysius further testifies that angels of a lower order do not participate equally in the illumination of the superiors but receive it in part from them. *“For,”* he says, *“the superiors have their illuminations immediately from God, and thereafter they conduct these illuminations or emanations, as messengers of the first order, to the inferiors—who are therefore called messengers of the latter order—who finally, by virtue of their office, conduct the effects of that illumination to men.”*

By these things, it is right to see that the Angels are subject to the Archangels, insofar as the angels receive the powers of their operation only from the Archangels, who are messengers of a higher and prior order (that is, "princes of messengers," as Isidore <note>Isidore of Seville, a 7th-century encyclopedist who wrote on the nature of angels.</note> says). For they hold the principal place among those angels called leaders, conductors, and princes, under whose order specific duties are assigned to every lower angel. This is clearly declared by the Prophet in these words: <margin>Zechariah 2:3.</margin> *“And behold, the Angel who spoke in me went out, and another angel went out to meet him and said to him: ‘Run, speak to this young man, saying,’ etc.”* The Prophet would never have said this unless he was arguing from the fact that, in the angelic office, a superior order commands an inferior one.

From these points, it is clearly evident that those Empyreal angels standing before the face of God—that is, the angels receiving immediate illumination from the face of God—hand down their commands to be carried out in the elemental region to the angels of a lower order, who are sent downward to perform duties in the world. Hence, Reuchlin <note>Johann Reuchlin (1455–1522), a German humanist and key figure in Christian Kabbalah.</note> says: <margin>Reuchlin, Art of the Kabbalah.</margin> *“The Peripatetics <note>Followers of Aristotle’s philosophy.</note> themselves attribute to every heaven, besides its essential form, an 'Intelligence' or 'mover' of the sphere, which is called an angel, inasmuch as it is sent by office to fulfill the commands of the Creator.”* And this is what the Prophet seems to intend in that speech: <margin>Isaiah 45.</margin> *“The Lord visited the host of heaven on high, and the kings of the earth who are upon the earth.”* Here, by "host of heaven," he seems to mean the angels, both celestial and elementary. And elsewhere: *“And suddenly there was with the angel a multitude of the heavenly host praising God.”* By these examples, one may see that those angels sent for service to worldly things are, for this reason, called <margin>Hebrews 1.</margin> *“ministering spirits”* by the Apostle. And elsewhere he says: <margin>Ephesians 3:10.</margin> *“That through the Church the manifold wisdom of God might be made known to the principalities and powers in the heavenly places,”* from which you see that even "celestial angels" are named in the scriptures.

That there are also angels who have command over the aerial world is testified by the Holy Scripture, speaking thus: <margin>Ephesians 2:1.</margin> *“God visited you when you walked according to the course of the world, and according to the ways of that prince who has dominion in the air, who works in the children of disobedience.”* Here, then, you see that every region of the world has, in its creation, its own proper angels or spirits destined for it by the Creator. These are ministering spirits, used as much for executing punishment upon the impious as for bestowing benevolence upon the just and godly, <margin>Hebrews 1.</margin> according to that saying of the Apostle: *“All angels are ministering spirits.”*

Therefore, since it has been demonstrated and frankly confessed by Marinus <note>Marin Mersenne, Fludd's intellectual rival.</note> himself in many places in his volume (but especially <margin>Column 1739.</margin> in the place cited), that Man is called a "Microcosm" because he participates in every region of the world—wherefore his intellectual spirit in the brain, as if in the supreme region of the microcosm or its "Empyrean heaven," is related to the angelic or Empyrean heaven in the macrocosm—and that vital spirit in the microcosm, which is contained chiefly in the thorax and the heart, corresponds to the heaven or...

<meta>catchword: spirit-</meta>

<summary>Fludd outlines the celestial hierarchy, arguing that divine light flows from God through higher angels to lower spirits governing the elemental and aerial realms. He concludes by applying this "Macrocosm" structure to the human "Microcosm," mapping the brain to the highest heaven and the heart to the vital celestial spirits.</summary>

<meta>Continuity: This page continues Robert Fludd’s argument regarding the spiritual hierarchy within the human being (the Microcosm), mirroring the structure of the universe (the Macrocosm). Having established that humans have guardian angels, he now explores the distribution of these spirits across different "regions" of the body and the conflict between good and evil spirits.</meta>

# On the [Contest] of Wisdom with Folly
<meta>catchword: Absur-</meta>

...to the spirit in the ethereal macrocosm; finally, that which is situated beneath the Diaphragm of the microcosm, as if beneath the sphere of the moon, is dedicated to the natural faculty and is compared to the elemental spirit in the macrocosm. Since (I say) things are so, one might rightly doubt whether each region of the microcosm, according to the nature of those parts, claims its own proper spirits—that is, whether it enjoys a birth-spirit <term>Genius natalitius: A guiding spirit assigned to a person at the moment of their birth.</term> agreeable to itself, and a genuine one with the spirits subjected to it, or not? 

But to arrive at a proof of this matter, I would have you know that if every human being has their own proper angel, who stands before the face of God (as demonstrated above), it follows that this Angel—who is most full of intellectual spirit—has under him his own angels sent into the world for ministry. Some of these have the care of Life, while others work toward the growth and preservation of the limbs. Moreover, that there are two good angels in every human being appears from the words of the Prophet Zechariah cited before, which are as follows: 

<margin>Zechariah 2:3</margin> *“Behold, the angel who spoke in me went forth, and another angel went out to meet him: And he said to him, Run, and speak to this boy saying, etc.”* 

Here, therefore, you see revealed and manifested two angels dwelling in one man, of whom one appears to be the Lord of the other, inasmuch as he commands him, saying, *“Run, speak to this boy, etc.”* But indeed, that these two angels were "good" appears first because they benevolently proclaimed the will of the word and will of the Lord, and then because they had their dwelling-place in so holy a man and true a Prophet.

Finally, that every human being has an evil angel, the Apostle <note>Referring to St. Paul.</note> himself seems to assert in various places of his epistles: 

<margin>Romans 7:23</margin> *“I see,”* he says, *“another law in my members <note>original: "membris"; referring to the physical parts of the body.</note> fighting against the law of my mind and taking me captive in the law of sin, which is in my members.”* 

Indeed, the Apostle would by no means have uttered this unless he had perceived an evil in the spirit within his members. Concerning this evil spirit, which is most hostile to the flesh and to life, he speaks elsewhere thus: 

<margin>Ephesians 2:1</margin> *“God visited you, who were dead in sin, in which in times past you walked according to the course of the world, and according to the ways of that prince who has dominion in the air—that is, the spirit of him who works in the children of disobedience.”* 

And elsewhere: 

<margin>Ephesians 6:12</margin> *“We struggle not against flesh and blood, but against the princes of the darkness of this world, against spiritual wickedness; therefore, for this cause, take up the complete armor of God, with which you can resist the invasions of the Devil on the unlucky day.”* 

Thus says he. By which it is clear that if the "prince of the air" is evil, then insofar as the air is the external environment of the internal man, the spirit of darkness also has dominion and power within him. Unless God, by His good grace and mercy, resisted him and his associates through the mediation of good angels, it would be all over for the life and soul of man. (I do not say the internal soul, insofar as it is of a primarily angelic and empyrean substance; and likewise for the celestial ether, over which portions of the human soul the evil spirit has no dominion, since he was expelled from the kingdom of heaven, as the sacred page testifies). Almighty God, I say, has so ordained in His mercy toward the human race that the forces of these princes of the air and darkness are tempered and restrained by the power of His good angels, so that no evil can be plotted against pious and just men. Whence we find it written: 

<margin>Matthew 11:10 <note>The original margin notes "Matth. I", likely a shorthand or error for chapter XI.</note></margin> *“Behold, I send my angel before your face, who shall prepare the way before me.”* 

And elsewhere: 

<margin>Psalm 91:11</margin> *“He has given His angels charge over you, to keep you in all your ways.”* 

And elsewhere: *“The angels shall bear you up in their hands, lest perhaps you dash your foot against a stone.”*

But let us return briefly to the matter, and more closely address that subject treated by us in our *Treatise on Genethlialogy, Book 4* <note>Genethlialogia: The study of casting nativities or birth charts.</note>, where the triple Genius is discussed. In that place, we have the opinion of Porphyry <note>A Neoplatonist philosopher (c. 234–305 AD).</note> opposing that of Iamblichus <note>A Neoplatonist philosopher (c. 245–325 AD) who disagreed with Porphyry on the nature of the personal daemon.</note>. Porphyry's view is that one Daemon <note>In this context, a "daemon" is a guiding spirit or genius, not necessarily an evil entity.</note> presides over the body, another over the soul, and a third over the mind. But the opinion of Iamblichus seems contrary to this, namely that the whole man is ruled by only a single daemon, saying it is absurd to assign a multiform overseer to the same living being. But I shall first take up the part and cause of Porphyry, and then afterwards I will attempt to effect a reconciliation between these two.

<summary>Fludd argues that humans contain both good and evil spirits, mapped onto the different regions of the body just as spirits are mapped onto the heavens and elements. He cites scripture to prove the existence of multiple angels and a "prince of the air," while introducing a philosophical debate between Porphyry and Iamblichus over whether a person has one daemon or three.</summary>

<meta>Continuity: On the previous page, Fludd established that there is a hierarchy of spirits in the universe (the Macrocosm) and that superior angels lead and instruct inferior ones. He now transitions into how this celestial hierarchy is mirrored within the human body (the Microcosm), arguing that man is inhabited by both good and evil spirits that influence different parts of his being.</meta>

# On Wisdom with Folly

...to the ethereal spirit in the macrocosm; finally, that which is situated below the diaphragm of the microcosm <term>microcosm: A "little world"; the Hermetic and Renaissance idea that the human body is a miniature map of the entire universe.</term>, as if beneath the sphere of the moon, is dedicated to the natural faculty and is compared to the elemental realm in the macrocosm. Since (I say) things are so, one might rightly wonder whether each region of the microcosm, according to their nature, claims its own specific spirits—that is, whether it enjoys a birth-genius <term>Genio natalitio: A guardian spirit or "guiding light" assigned to a person at the moment of their birth.</term> consistent with itself and genuine, along with the spirits subject to it, or not? 

But to reach the proof of this matter, I would have you know that if every human being has their own personal angel who stands before the face of God (as demonstrated above), it follows that this Angel, who is most full of intellectual spirit, has under him his own angels sent into the world for administration. Some of these have the care of Life, while others work toward the growth and preservation of the limbs. Moreover, that there are two good angels in every person is apparent from the words of the Prophet Zechariah previously cited, which are as follows:

<margin>Zechariah 2:3</margin>
> "Behold, the angel who spoke in me went out, and another angel went out to meet him: And he said to him, 'Run, and speak to this boy, saying,' etc." <note>original: "Ecce angelus qui loquebatur in me, egrediebatur, & angelus alius egrediebatur in occursum eius..."</note>

Here, therefore, you see revealed and manifested two angels dwelling in one man, one of whom seems to be the Master of the other, inasmuch as he commands him, saying, *"Run, speak to this boy,"* etc. Indeed, it is clear that these two angels were good within him, primarily because they benevolently proclaimed the will of the word and the will of the Lord, and then afterward because they had their dwelling-place in a man so holy and a true Prophet.

Finally, that there is an evil angel for every man, the Apostle <note>Referring to St. Paul.</note> himself seems to assert in various places in his epistles:

<margin>Romans 7:23</margin>
> "I see," he says, "another law in my members fighting against the law of my mind and capturing me in the law of sin, which is in my members."

The Apostle would by no means have uttered this unless he had perceived an evil spirit in his members. Of this evil spirit, which is most hostile to the flesh and to life, he speaks elsewhere in this way:

<margin>Ephesians 2:1</margin>
> "God visited you, who were dead in sin, in which in times past you walked according to the course of the world, and according to the ways of that prince who has dominion in the air; that is, the spirit who works in the children of disobedience."

And elsewhere:

<margin>Ephesians 6:12</margin>
> "We struggle not against flesh and blood, but against the princes of the darkness of this world, against spiritual wickedness. Therefore, for this cause, take up the complete armor of God, with which you can resist the invasions of the Devil on the unlucky day."

These are his words. From which it is clear that if the prince of the air is evil, then insofar as the external part of the internal man is air, the spirit of darkness also has dominion and power within it. If God, out of His good grace and mercy, did not resist this spirit and his companions through the mediation of good angels, it would be the end of man’s life and soul. (I do not say the *internal* soul, since that is composed primarily of angelic and empyrean <term>empyrean: The highest, most spiritual part of the heavens, made of pure light and fire.</term> substance, and then of celestial ether, over which portions of the human soul the evil spirit has no dominion, for he was expelled from the kingdom of heaven, as the sacred page testifies). 

Almighty God, I say, has so ordained in His mercy toward the human race that the powers of those princes of air and darkness are tempered and restrained by the virtue of His good angels, so that they can devise no evil against pious and just men. Hence we find it written:

<margin>Matthew 11:10 <note>The source identifies this as Matthew 1, but the quote corresponds to Matthew 11:10 or Mark 1:2.</note></margin>
> "Behold, I send my angel before your face, who shall prepare the way before me."

And elsewhere:

<margin>Psalm 91:11</margin>
> "He has given His angels charge over you, to keep you in all your ways."

And elsewhere:

> "The angels shall bear you up in their hands, lest perhaps you dash your foot against a stone."

But let us return briefly to the matter at hand, and more closely gird ourselves for that proposition discussed by us in our *Treatise on Genethlialogia, Book 4* <note>Genethlialogia: Fludd’s work on the casting of astrological charts and the spirits associated with birth.</note>, where the threefold Genius is addressed. In that place, we have the opinion of Porphyry <note>A Neoplatonic philosopher who argued that humans have different spirits for different faculties.</note> opposing that of Iamblichus <note>A later Neoplatonic philosopher who argued for a single, unified guardian spirit.</note>. Porphyry’s view is that one demon <note>original: "Dæmon" - here used in the classical sense of a tutelary spirit or "Genius," not necessarily a malevolent demon.</note> presides over the body, another over the soul, and a third over the mind. But the opinion of Iamblichus seems contrary to this: he holds that the whole man is protected by only a single demon, saying it is absurd to assign a multifaceted ruler to the same living being. For my part, I will first plead the side and cause of Porphyry, and then later I will attempt to effect a reconciliation between the two of them.

<meta>catchword: Absurd-</meta>

He introduces a philosophical debate between Porphyry and Iamblichus regarding whether a person is guided by multiple specific spirits or a single unified guardian.</summary>

<meta>Continuity: This page continues the argument that the human being, as a microcosm, contains multiple spiritual influences. Fludd is now specifically critiquing the Neoplatonist philosopher Iamblichus, who argued for a single guiding spirit (daemon), by contrasting this with the visible conflicts between the higher mind and the lower instincts.</meta>

### On the Contest, Book III.

It is absurd (says Iamblichus <note>Iamblichus (c. 245–325 AD) was a Neoplatonist philosopher who wrote extensively on theurgy and the relationship between the soul and the divine.</note>) to assign a multiform ruler to the same living being. Iamblichus himself seems to acknowledge that the world possesses a soul, and that it was made according to the likeness of its Creator. Yet, it cannot escape Iamblichus's own notice that the world is moved in many different ways throughout its various regions, and that it contains parts that are entirely contrary to one another in their nature. For the empyrean spirit <term>Empyrean spirit: The highest, most refined spiritual substance in the universe, associated with divine fire and light.</term> is governed and ruled differently than the ethereal spirit; and again, the ethereal spirit—insofar as it is incorruptible and imbued with a certain and stable motion—behaves differently than the elemental or "airy" spirit, which is corruptible and subject to all manner of alterations. 

Does not the same logic apply to the microcosm <note>microcosm: "The little world," referring to the human being as a reflection of the entire universe (the macrocosm).</note>, in which the intellectual region is ruled by different laws than the vital region, and the vital region by different laws than the natural? And yet, Iamblichus assigns to it only a single, non-multiform Spirit <term>Dæmon: In this context, a "daemon" refers to a guiding spirit or guardian, not necessarily an evil entity.</term> by which it is ruled and governed. But if, according to his opinion, there is only a single, uniform, and simple spirit present, why does this uniform angel produce such various and mutually contrary effects? It is just as the Apostle [Paul] previously put forth: *there is another law in the members, fighting against the law of the divine mind.* <note>Reference to Romans 7:23: "But I see another law in my members, warring against the law of my mind."</note> If (I say) a single spirit governs the whole body, why does it not act uniformly with itself and within itself? Or how has it come to pass that one part of the whole is opposed to another part of the same thing? Certainly, it will be difficult for Iamblichus—and even more so for Mersenne <note>Marin Mersenne (1588–1648), a French mathematician and philosopher who was Fludd’s contemporary and fierce intellectual rival.</note>—to answer this. 

But Iamblichus, being a Platonist, could not be ignorant of the fact that Plotinus, all the Platonists, and even Hermes Trismegistus <note>Hermes Trismegistus: The legendary author of the Hermetic corpus, central to Fludd’s philosophy of nature and divinity.</note> himself established three parts within the internal man. These they call the supreme, the middle, and the lowest. 

1.  **The Supreme:** They establish this as the divine part, which they call the Mind, or the "active and illuminated intellect." In this resides that brilliance which proceeds from the face or mouth of the Creator; hence it was called by Moses the "breath of lives" <note>Genesis 2:7, often translated as "breath of life."</note> inspired into us by God or the spirit of wisdom. 
2.  **The Middle:** They created a vital spirit that participates in reason, whose duty is to connect and bind the two extremes together—namely, the natural soul (which, with the Apostle, they call the *animal* soul <note>The word "animal" here relates to "anima" (soul) and refers to the physical, biological life force.</note>) and the Mind. This middle spirit seems to understand the nature of both extremes. It is said to differ from the supreme extreme (the active intellect, mind, and light) just as it differs from the natural or animal part, from which (as the Apostle testifies) a separation is made by the power of the Word. His words are these: *The word of God is living and effective and sharper than any two-edged sword, reaching even unto the division of the soul and the spirit.* <note>Hebrews 4:12.</note> 
3.  **The Lowest:** This appears to be the natural or sensitive soul, which the Platonists call the *idol* <gloss>idolum: image or reflection</gloss> and Saint Paul calls the *natural man*. 

Therefore, their judgment regarding these three portions of the human soul is this: the **supreme** part never sins, never consents to any evil, always resists error, and exhorts us toward the best things. For this reason, they conclude that this mind can never be damned; rather, upon the death of the man, it departs unharmed to its super-celestial homeland, leaving its companions to be punished. 

But the **lower** portion, conversely—which they called the natural, sensitive, and animal man—is always submerged in bad sin and lust by its own natural inclination, and it drags the person toward the worst things, according to that saying of the Apostle: *I see another law in my members, taking me captive in the law of sin.* 

Finally, that **middle** portion, which is vital and rational, is free by its own nature. It can adhere to either extreme according to its own pleasure. Thus it happens that if this intermediate spirit constantly adheres to the higher portion, it is united to it, introduced into the gate of blessedness, and finally transformed into a divine nature. But if it is more prone to lean toward that lower portion than the higher, it then becomes depraved and allows itself to be stained more and more by sin, until it takes on the nature of an evil demon <gloss>cacodæmon</gloss>.

If this is the case, how uncertain, inconstant, and entirely self-adversarial is the disposition of that single daemon established by Iamblichus, which seems to confound good things with evil, and evil with good, in the same subject? But what will Mersenne say to this? He who seems only to acknowledge God in the bo— <meta>catchword: body</meta>

<summary>Fludd argues that the human soul is tripartite (Supreme Mind, Middle Reason, and Lowest Senses) rather than a single unified spirit as Iamblichus claimed. This internal division explains the moral struggle between divine aspiration and physical sin.</summary>
<keywords>Iamblichus, Mersenne, Plotinus, Hermes Trismegistus, tripartite soul, active intellect, animal soul, microcosm, spiritual conflict, Apostle Paul</keywords>

# Of the Contest, Book III.
### l 2
<meta>catchword: pore [likely 'corpore' on the following page]</meta>

*“It is absurd,”* says Iamblichus <note>Iamblichus (c. 245–325 AD) was a Neoplatonist philosopher who wrote extensively on theurgy and the soul's relationship to the divine.</note>, *“to assign a multi-faceted ruler to the same creature.”* Iamblichus himself seems to acknowledge that the world possesses a soul, and that the world was created in the likeness of its Creator. Yet it cannot escape the notice of Iamblichus himself that the world moves in various ways within its different regions, and that it behaves in ways that are in every respect contrary to reason. For the empyrean spirit <note>original: "spiritus empyreus"; the highest, most refined spiritual substance of the heavens.</note> is governed and ruled differently than the ethereal spirit; and again, the ethereal spirit—insofar as it is incorruptible and endowed with a certain stable motion—behaves differently than the elemental or airy spirit, which is corruptible and subject to all manner of changes. 

Does the same logic not apply to the microcosm <note>The "microcosm" is the human being, viewed as a miniature reflection of the entire universe (the macrocosm).</note>, in which the intellectual region is ruled by different laws than the vital region, and the vital by different laws than the natural? And yet, Iamblichus assigns to the human only a single, and not a multi-faceted, Guardian Spirit <note>original: "Dæmonem"; in this context, it refers to a guiding spirit or tutelary genius, not necessarily an evil demon.</note> by which he is ruled and governed. But if, according to his opinion, only a single, uniform, and simple spirit is present, why does this uniform angel produce various effects that are contrary to one another? For as the Apostle <note>Saint Paul the Apostle.</note> previously set forth: *Is there not another law in the limbs fighting against the law of the divine mind?* <note>original: "alia sit lex in membris repugnans legi mentis diuinæ"; a reference to Romans 7:23.</note> If (I say) a single spirit governs the body, why does it not act uniformly with and within itself? Or how does it happen that one part of the whole is opposed to another part of the same whole? Certainly, it will be difficult for Iamblichus—and much more so for Mersenne <note>Marin Mersenne (1588–1648), a French mathematician and philosopher who was a fierce critic of Fludd’s mystical and alchemical theories.</note>—to answer this. 

But since Iamblichus was a Platonist, he could not have been ignorant of the fact that Plotinus, all the Platonists, and even Hermes Trismegistus <note>original: "Mercurius Trismegistus"; the legendary Hellenistic figure to whom the Hermetic writings are attributed.</note> themselves placed three parts within the internal man, which they call the supreme, the middle, and the lowest. 
1. The **supreme** part they establish as that divine element which they call the mind, or the active and illuminated intellect <term>Intellectus agens: The "active intellect," a concept in medieval and Renaissance philosophy describing the part of the mind that makes knowledge possible by "lighting up" abstract ideas.</term>. Within it is that brightness which proceeds from the face or mouth of the Creator; hence it was called by Moses the "breath of life" <note>original: "spiraculum vitarum"; Genesis 2:7.</note> breathed into us by God, or the spirit of wisdom. 
2. The **middle** part they made the vital spirit, which participates in reason. Its duty is to connect and bind the two extremes to one another—namely, the natural soul (which, with the Apostle, they call the *animal* soul <note>original: "animalem"; here meaning "pertaining to the breath or life-force," rather than "beastly."</note>) with the mind, as it seems to understand the nature of both extremes. This middle part is said to differ from the supreme extreme (which is the active intellect, mind, light, and the highest portion) just as it differs from the natural or animal part, from which (according to the Apostle) a separation is made by the power of the Word. His words are these: *“The word of God is living, and effective, and more piercing than any two-edged sword, reaching even to the division of the soul and the spirit.”* <note>Hebrews 4:12.</note>
3. The **lowest** part seems to be the natural or sensitive soul, which the Platonists call the "image" <note>original: "idolum"; often used in Neoplatonism to describe the lower, reflecting part of the soul.</note> and St. Paul calls the "animal man." 

This, therefore, is their judgment concerning these three portions of the human soul: namely, that the supreme part never sins, nor consents to any evil, but always resists error and encourages the best things. For this reason, they conclude that this mind can never be condemned at any time, but upon the death of the man, it departs unharmed from its punishable companions into its super-celestial homeland. 

But the inferior portion, conversely—which they called the natural, sensitive, and animal man—is always submerged by its own natural inclination in bad sin and concupiscence <note>Concupiscence: Strong desire or lust, often used in a theological sense to describe the human tendency toward sin.</note>, and it drags one toward the worst things, according to that saying of the Apostle: *“I see another law in my members, taking me captive in the law of sin.”* <note>Romans 7:23.</note> 

Finally, that portion which is vital and rational, since it is free by its own nature, can adhere to either extreme according to its own pleasure. From this it usually happens that if this intermediate spirit adheres constantly to the higher portion, it is united to it, introduced into the gate of blessedness, and finally transformed into the divine nature. But if it is more prone to incline toward the lower portion than the higher, it then becomes depraved and allows itself to be stained more and more by sin, until, by the opposite turn, it takes on the nature of an evil spirit <note>original: "cacodæmonis".</note>.

But if things are so, how uncertain, inconstant, and in every way adverse to itself is the disposition of that single Guardian Spirit established by Iamblichus, which seems to confuse good things with bad, and bad with good, in the same subject? But what will Mersenne say to this? He who seems only to acknowledge God in the body...

### On Wisdom with Folly

...in the human body, and he refuses to admit that any angel presides over it. Does he not see that God is unique, firm, and stable in His own actions, and yet he himself perceives so many contrary conditions within his own body? But if there were only a single ruler for the body, what need, I ask, would there be for **free will** <term>Free will: (Latin: *liberum arbitrium*) The capacity of the human soul to choose between different courses of action, positioned here as a middle ground between divine light and earthly darkness.</term>, which possesses the liberty to choose between either extreme—namely, between the bright source of life on one side (pointing upward), and the dark, earthly place, which is like a bottomless pit, on the other? What need, I say, is there to look toward that intermediate spirit which is indifferent to both good and evil? 

Yet, because of the seductive allurements of the prince of the air and of darkness, we observe that this spirit is sometimes more prone toward lower things. Are we not also admonished by daily experience, which teaches us that this matter cannot be otherwise? Indeed, do we not have this confirmed and proven within ourselves at every moment? For example, this reveals itself in the very disposition of **Marin Mersenne** <note>Marin Mersenne (1588–1648) was a French priest and mathematician who was a fierce critic of Robert Fludd's mystical and alchemical philosophy. Fludd is here using Mersenne's own character as an example of spiritual struggle.</note>: he has the scriptures openly speaking against him.

<margin>Romans 2:1</margin>
For (says the Apostle): *You are inexcusable, O man, whoever you are who judges, for in that which you judge another, you condemn yourself; for you who judge do the same things. For we know that the judgment of God is according to the truth against those who do such things. Do you think, O man, you who judge those who do such things and yet do them yourself, that you will escape the judgment of God?* <note>original: "Inexcusabilis es ô homo omnis qui iudicas..." This is a quote from the Vulgate, the standard Latin Bible of the time.</note> And elsewhere

<margin>
Proverbs 20:3
Proverbs 12:18
Proverbs 15:4
</margin>
it is said: *It is an honor for a man to cease from strife*, and elsewhere: *There is one who speaks words that are like the piercings of a sword, but the tongue of the wise is health.* And elsewhere: *A healing tongue is a tree of life, but perverseness in it is like a breach in the spirit.* Countless healthy lessons of morality and piety of this kind are taught to us by that **good angel**, who has the power of guardianship over the mind. 

But in truth, the rational part of Marin, leaving behind this sound doctrine, listens to the voice of him who belongs to the side of the prince of darkness. This dark spirit, in the manner of the Serpent deceiving Eve, seems to speak to him with such talk: "Condemn and judge everyone; be most hostile to all learned men; call them atheists, heretics, magicians, impious, blasphemous, ignorant, shameless, and fools. Be exceedingly contentious in the world, for thus you will live as an object of wonder to men and equal in eternity to God himself. For your works will show for ages to come what kind of man you were and how great a philosopher." 

Surely all worldly people will say: "It must be that this man is the greatest Theologian, a huge Cabalist <note>Cabalist: A student of the Kabbalah, a tradition of Jewish mysticism often adapted by Renaissance Christian thinkers to explore the hidden meanings of the universe.</note>, a preeminent Philosopher, who has finally and successfully revealed the errors of all theologians—even some of the Church Fathers—the vanity and folly of the Cabalists, and the unskillfulness and ignorance of philosophers, both ancient and modern, which had never been detected or observed by the worldly-wise until this day." 

By these persuasions, I say, led by that vain sensitive soul and by his "animal man" <note>animal man: (Latin: *homo animalis*) A Pauline term referring to a person living only on a physical or emotional level, lacking spiritual insight.</note>, and leaving behind celestial counsels and the "food of the good angel," he is entangled on all sides in the snares of the devil. For while he gloriously condemns others for their ignorance, he himself sticks in the very tenacious mud of lack of knowledge. Consequently, he is destined to undergo the sentence pronounced by the Apostle: *O man, who judges those who do such things and yet you do them, will you escape the judgment of God?* Indeed, God's judgment will undoubtedly be even heavier upon him because he also judges falsely. 

Here, therefore, we clearly have three distinct natures: namely, **free will** in the middle, and two **spiritual natures** at the extremes. One of these attracts toward the good (the nature of light), and the other toward evil (the nature of darkness). It is as if someone intends in his mind to kill a man because of some injury, or to unjustly cause him harm, but immediately, by collecting himself, he sees that this is against the laws of the kingdom; thus, terrified by the punishment for such a crime, he abstains from the impious deed for a time. But a little later, something seems to whisper in his ears and persuade his appetite that it would be good to commit that crime (the law of the kingdom notwithstanding), as the act could bring him some private advantage or the friendship of some man of eminent honor. Furthermore, the hope of escape will perhaps urge him toward this impious undertaking. By these reasons, this pensive schemer will perhaps change his...
