# On the Conflict, Book III.

...his intention to the contrary. Finally, from this last intention of his, perhaps by some lucky chance, or by a pious whispering of the mind, or of the angel presiding over the mind, he is opportunely moved and withdraws—the one who, in order to distract that free spirit from such a deed, proposes that divine command, forbidding anyone to kill another, and so at last he will abstain from the premeditated crime.

Is it not clearer than light that in the same human there are three spirits, different in condition and nature? And consequently, if all actions in man and everywhere also in the **macrocosm** <term>Macrocosm: The "great world" or the universe, which Fludd believed was mirrored in the "microcosm" of the human body.</term> proceed from the spirit or word, and from the will and divine pleasure—which pleasure (as the Royal Psalmist testifies) the angels perform <margin>Psalm 103:21</margin> as ministers of God, doing His pleasure—and if such ministers are ordained as much for harmfulness and punishment as for exercising beneficence and mercy toward mortals, it follows from this that these various actions in man proceed from and are ordained by these various ministers of God, destined in the world for various duties. 

I say they proceed from the **Olympic** <note>original: "Olympicorum". In Fludd's system, these are intermediate spirits associated with the celestial spheres, acting as a bridge between the highest divine realm and the physical world.</note> spirits acting as intermediaries, who instill the force of free will, insofar as they are situated in the middle between the intellectual heaven and the elementary one; from those **empyrean** <note>The highest, "fiery" heaven, the dwelling place of God and the highest angels.</note> or highest spirits, who stand before the face of the Creator, as Christ testifies, and are consequently inclined toward every good; and from the elementary spirits who are subject to the prince having power in the air and darkness, from which they always incite toward sins and dark actions. But indeed, that highest angel is a prince, and first over the others subject to him, just as the empyrean spirit possesses the powers of all the other subject heavens. This we can also gather from human faculties. For even Philosophers, both Pagan and Christian, seem to agree on this, namely that:

We have this distinction of human action or virtue from **Galen** <note>A Greek physician and philosopher (c. 129–216 AD) whose theories dominated Western medicine for over a millennium.</note> in *On Affected Places* and *On the Differences of Symptoms*. Having established that different angelic influences govern different parts of the human being, he now focuses on how the "Microcosm" (the human being) mirrors the "Macrocosm" (the universe) through the physical and spiritual actions of the heart.</meta>

# On the Contest of Wisdom with Folly

By these arguments they seem to show that, just as the **primary will** <term>Primary will: The supreme, original intent of God that resides in the highest heaven and governs all creation.</term> exists with God dwelling in the "heaven of heavens," so too the motion and action of the heavens in the ethereal or vital region (and that of the elements) is subject to the disposition of that first and supreme will. Consequently, these motions are called "involuntary," meaning they are ruled, swept along, and moved according to the will of the supreme mover or actor. 

By a similar logic, within the **microcosm** <term>Microcosm: The "little world," referring to the human being as a reflection of the entire universe.</term>, that "mental angel" ruling in the brain—the supreme heaven of the body—acts like a high king and first emperor. Under his empire, he holds the vital and natural realms. 

However, there is no small doubt concerning **free will** in the vital or middle portion of man, and also regarding the manner and reason by which it is filled with joy and good passions at the reminders of a "higher genius" <note>original: "superioris genij". Fludd refers to a guardian spirit or higher intellectual faculty that guides the soul.</note>. One must also ask by what occurrence that spirit is enticed by the lowest extreme, whereby the path to grace is usually intercepted by inherent sin. 

Therefore, if I may speak briefly to you according to my own findings and capacity—drawn from the words of the three-part distinction mentioned above—concerning what I conjecture about free will as it relates to the rational life and its two extremes, I shall explain it in an unheard-of manner. I pray you do not find this tedious because it is perhaps unfamiliar; for this is the **Mystery of the Internal Man**, worthy of great admiration. 

In the **dilation** <gloss>expansion</gloss> of the heart alone, the first mystery is situated, which the good passions serve—namely joy, hope, confidence, love, mercy, and humanity. In its **contraction** <gloss>tightening</gloss> alone, the second mystery is found, which the evil passions follow—namely sadness, despair, fear, hatred, anger, and so on. Then, afterward, a third mystery consists in the rational method of action, balancing both expansion and contraction together as ordained for the necessity of a composed life. From these, you may perceive most fully the two extremes of a certain "free intermediate motion." Although I have explained this very matter elsewhere, nevertheless, because it pertains to our present purpose, I believe it is worthwhile to measure it out again in this place.

We say, therefore, that the mystery of the heart's voluntary and involuntary actions is wonderful if we explore the matter a little more accurately, down to the very core. For if, in the first place, we contemplate the disposition of the heart's expansion alone, we will perceive from its effect that it is concerned only with **super-celestial beatitude** <note>The state of supreme spiritual happiness or "blessedness" found in the presence of the Divine.</note>, since by its means the spiritual eyes of the heart are opened. 

I say the "spiritual heart," or the spirit dwelling in the very center of the heart, looking out from the darkness through this expansion and rejoicing in a super-celestial vision. Looking upward, as it were, it yearns to behold the divine **Idea** of the Divine Mind. By such contemplation, it draws the entirety of human life from lower things to higher ones. From this it happens that the whole man is snatched away from the cares of "evil demons" <note>original: "cacodaemonis". In this context, these are spirits or influences that cause distress, vice, or earthly obsession.</note> toward the happiness of a good angel, and is lifted on high. It was by means of this kind of cardiac action that Saints John and Paul were "rapt" into the third heaven, where they confessed to having seen things that should by no means be revealed to the world. 

For by the spacious expansion of the heart, the celestial spirits of life enclosed within the heart seek their freedom. They strive to visit their "celestial brothers" wandering everywhere in the air through this expansion. In their light, they behold not only the fountain of the soul, but also the mind within it, the **Word** within the mind, and in and through the Word, they behold God the Father, surrounded on all sides by a choir of Graces and the service of archangels. They are snatched away by the very sight of this vision. This also seems to be confirmed by these words of the divine **Synesius** <note>Synesius of Cyrene (c. 373–414 AD), a Neoplatonist philosopher and bishop whose hymns Fludd frequently cites to bridge Hermetic and Christian thought.</note> speaking of the Mind:

<margin>Synesius, Hymn 1.</margin>
> *Yet there is, yes, there is some light in covered eyes; there is even in those who have fallen here a certain power calling them back to heaven, so that having emerged from the waves of life, they may enter the sanctuary; one goes to the palace of the parent. Blessed is he who, after labors, after bitter earthly cares, having entered the paths of the mind, has seen the height, shining with divine light. And elsewhere: From the perennial fountain of good things, having conceived prayers, to ascend suppliantly to the parent, nor to delay leaving the earthly things of the earth, for soon, joined with the father, a goddess in God, you will exult.* 

But truly, this power of expanding the heart is proved by its effect...

<summary>Fludd explains his theory of the "Internal Man," arguing that the physical expansion (dilation) of the heart is a spiritual mystery linked to joy, divine contemplation, and the soul's ascent to God. He correlates the heart's physical movements with the moral and spiritual state of the individual, using the Neoplatonic poetry of Synesius to support his claims.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Robert Fludd’s exploration of the heart's role as a spiritual bridge. Having previously discussed free will and the "mental angel" in the brain, he now details the physiological and spiritual mechanics of how the heart "expands" to receive divine grace or "contracts" in sin. This reflects his core theory that physical states of the body mirror the spiritual state of the soul.</meta>

### On the Contest, Book III.

It seems to be proven that through this <note>The "this" refers to the expansion of the heart mentioned on the previous page.</note>, good and delightful passions are introduced to the soul by reason of that hidden vision tending toward joy and blessedness. Although these things cannot be fully discerned by a man looking spiritually because of the "shadow of his flesh," nevertheless his internal spirit seems to exult from such a sight. From this it happens that this **expansion of the heart** is followed by joy, hope, confidence, love, mercy, and humanity, etc., which are pious actions and closest to divinity. Regarding this kind of expansion of the heart, as well as its contraction, Solomon speaks thus:

<margin>Proverbs 21:4</margin>
> *Exalted eyes are the expansion of the heart, but the lamp of the wicked [is sin]* <note>original: "Exaltatio oculorū est dilatatio cordis, lucerna impiorum"</note>

It is as if he had said: the eyes of the heavenly spirits contained in the heart are exalted toward God by the expansion of the heart; but indeed, by its contraction, *their lamp is sin*, which is to say, darkness and error. Whence God also said: *Who is he who would apply his heart to approach me?* This speech can be turned toward this sense: Who is he who, by my power, fear, hope, charity, and joy, will so adapt his heart that he reaches the vision of me? For the heart is expanded by good passions and habits, but it is constricted by evil and impious ones. Hence David says exultingly:

<margin>Psalm 107:1</margin>
> *My heart is ready, O God, my heart is ready; I will sing and play psalms in my glory,*

which is to say: my heart is expanded, and by that expansion, it is made ready so that, by the vision of You who are my glory, I may sing and play psalms. Here, therefore, is present the joy of the spirits because of the expansion of the heart which arose from the intuition of the divine mind. Through this, the entire spirit of the body is refreshed, whence the Wise Man says:

<margin>Proverbs 15:13</margin>
> *A joyful heart makes a cheerful face, but by the sorrow of the soul the spirit is cast down,*

which can indeed be explained in this way: An expanded heart cheers the spirit through the vision of invisible joy, and by the contraction of the heart, sorrow and sadness are produced, through which the **spirits of life** <term>Spirits of life: In Fludd’s medical theory, these are subtle, vaporous substances that carry life and sensation through the body.</term> are oppressed. Piety also, faith, and religion are produced in the expansion of the heart; for the fire of life, looking forward with the greatest pleasure from its spirit fixed in the heart toward the vision of the [divine] mind and His glory, is rendered humble, pious, and just. Whence it is said:

<margin>Sirach 1:18</margin> <note>The text cites "Eccles.", referring to Ecclesiasticus/Sirach.</note>
> *The religious man will guard and justify the heart; he will give delight and joy:*

From this, the Wise Man speaks of the power of love in this place, arguing from the expansion of the heart:

<margin>Sirach 2:29</margin>
> *Those who fear the Lord will prepare their hearts, and in His sight they will sanctify their souls,*

where by "fear of the Lord" he understands not that servile fear, but love with reverence. Even the Pagans <note>original: "Ethnici"</note> recognized that these pious passions of the soul proceed from the expansion of the heart. Furthermore, concerning the production of faith, the Evangelist says:

<margin>John 14:1</margin>
> *Let not your heart be troubled; you believe in God, etc.*

as if he were saying: Let not your heart be disturbed within you because you believe in God, etc., for faith and hope come forward from the expansion of the heart. For charity is a pious passion which arises from the heart’s expansion. Whence the Apostle says:

<margin>2 Corinthians 9:7</margin>
> *Let each one do as he has purposed in his heart, not with sadness or necessity; for God loves a cheerful giver:*

This means: by expanding the heart and cheerfully looking back toward God Himself or the Supreme Mind, and not by contracting it—namely, by giving with secret grief, sadness, and malice. Furthermore, Ruth spoke of this kind of good action of the heart:

<margin>Ruth 2:13</margin>
> *I have found grace in your eyes, my lord, who have comforted me and spoken to the heart of your handmaid.*

As if she had said: "My lord, by your speaking to the spirits of my life or my soul, hope and joy are produced in me." This, therefore, is that pious disposition of the heart in which a most free communication is made between the human heavenly spirits and those of the world. Through these, there is a spiritual prospect toward the object of the Divine Majesty; and also the seeds of heavenly life in the **macrocosm** <term>Macrocosm: The "great world" or the universe as a whole.</term> and those adhering to the heart of the **microcosm** <term>Microcosm: The "little world," meaning the human being as a reflection of the universe.</term> are united. Regarding this disposition, the Psalmist intends when he says: *Set your hearts upon his strength*, which is to say: spread wide and open your hearts, so that you may exult in the Lord.

From these things we see that, just as the action of the heart in expansion becomes the origin of good and pious passions (because through it, the heavenly spirits enclosed in the chamber of the heart for the benefit of life are refreshed by the expansion of the heart through a copious vision of the mind, both of the macrocosm and the microcosm), those which [arise] from earthly disturbances,

<meta>catchword: -turbances,</meta>

<summary>This page explains Fludd’s theory that spiritual joy, faith, and love are literal "expansions" of the heart that allow the human spirit to perceive the divine, while sin and sorrow are "contractions." He supports this using numerous biblical citations to show that the heart acts as the vital interface between the human (microcosm) and the divine (macrocosm).</summary>

<meta>This page continues Robert Fludd’s discussion of the heart's movement. He is transitioning from how the heart's expansion leads to divine joy to how its "contraction"—a physical shrinking or tightening—results in spiritual suffering and psychological distress. The catchword "Con-" from the previous page completes the word "contentionibus" (struggles/contentions).</meta>

# On the Contest of Wisdom with Folly

[...struggles], cares, and vexations, toward the contemplations of celestial and **supersubstantial** <note>This term refers to things existing beyond the physical realm, often used in mystical theology to describe the "bread" of the soul.</note> things, and by this almost divine rapture they are snatched upwards; so also the soul's evil passions, or vices, are accustomed to arise only from the **contraction of the heart**: namely sadness, despair, fear, hatred, envy, anger, fury, shame, etc. For where the heart inclines more toward contraction than toward **dilation** <note>expansion</note>, there the spirits are imprisoned too deeply in the abyss of the heart, so that they can very rarely reach the vision of the divine mind. Instead, it causes the soul to look downward from that region, whence arises "furious melancholy" and sorrow of the soul, since the way to higher things is closed to it. Much more is it deprived of the divine vision, and thus, stirred by these kinds of passions, it affects the nature of an **evil demon** <note>original: "cacodæmonis"</note>, just as it used to behold the angelic nature in its blessed vision. Now deprived of divine grace, it seems to taste the bitterness of darkness and is subjected to the rage of elementary demons, being shaken and agitated by **hypochondriacal winds** <note>In 17th-century medicine, "vapors" or "winds" rising from the spleen and abdomen (the hypochondrium) were thought to cause depression and "melancholy."</note> and turbulent gusts of air. It is observed to be afflicted by mania, insanity, fear, sadness, despair, fury, and similar infirmities, and deluded by false and deceptive visions. Hence it is said:

<margin>Deuteronomy 28:67</margin>
> I will give you a trembling heart, and failing eyes, and a soul consumed with sorrow; and your life shall hang in doubt before you. You shall fear night and day, and shall have no assurance of your life.

This is to say, it is because of the lack of the vision of truth, which happens because of the heart’s contraction. Vices of this kind, like curses, will vex your soul, whence Job says:

<margin>Job 21:4</margin>
> My spirit is narrowed within me. <note>original: "Spiritus meus in me angustatur." This is often translated in modern Bibles as "why should I not be impatient?" but Fludd uses the literal sense of "narrowed" or "constricted" to support his theory of the heart.</note>

And the royal Psalmist says:

> My spirit is consumed, and I become like those descending into the pit.

This means: my spirit is covered over with darkness. Wherefore the Wise Man says:

<margin>Proverbs 14:19</margin>
> That the way of the impious is dark, and they know not where they may fall,

whence elsewhere:

<margin>Judges 16:16</margin>
> Their soul failed and was provoked even unto death.

And **Hermes Trismegistus** <note>A legendary Hellenistic figure associated with a collection of mystical and philosophical writings called the Hermetica.</note> speaks thus:

<margin>Pymander 10</margin>
> The soul, by the nature of things and the conscience of the good, when deprived of bodily eyes, entangles itself in passions: the soul, corrupted after the manner of an evil demon and ignorant of itself, serves cast-off bodies and deformed monsters; it carries its own body about like a hostile burden, not presiding over the body, but subjugated because of ignorance.

But truly Saint John brings a remedy for this disease in this manner:

<margin>John 14:1</margin>
> Let not your heart be troubled; you believe in God, etc.

where he implies that faith, by dilating the heart, is the cause of the cure for that passion. The Evangelist also teaches that sadness is produced by this kind of action in these words:

<margin>John 16:6</margin>
> But because I have spoken these things to you, sadness has filled your heart.

It is also the cause of perversity and obstinacy, whence it was said:

<margin>Exodus 10:19</margin>
> Pharaoh’s heart is hardened, and he did not listen to them, just as the Lord had commanded.

That is, he had neither faith nor obedience. Now, the reason for this error of the soul is due to the impediment of its vision: for it is blind because of the darkness of the closed-off heart, and it cannot see. This is gathered from many places in the sacred scriptures.

<margin>Isaiah 6:10</margin>
> Blind (says God) the heart of this people, lest they understand with their heart.

And elsewhere,

> All saw him and were troubled, and when the wind ceased they were more amazed among themselves, for their [heart] was blinded;

that is, they could not see the vision of truth because of the contraction of the heart. And elsewhere:

> This people honors me with their lips, but their heart is far from me;

And elsewhere:

<margin>Mark 6</margin>
<margin>Mark 7</margin>
<margin>Romans 1:21</margin>
> They did not glorify Him as God nor give Him thanks, but they became vain in their thoughts, and their foolish heart was darkened.

And elsewhere:

> According to your hardness and impenitent heart, you treasure up for yourself wrath on the day of wrath and the revelation of the righteous judgment of God.

From these things it is explained clearly enough that these passions—namely fear, error due to lack of vision, sadness or sorrow of the soul, despair and distrust, the narrowing of the spirits, the darkness of the soul and its failure, deceptive delusions, perversity or obstinacy, blindness, and its separation from divine contemplations—proceed from this earthly and unlucky action of the heart: namely, its long-lasting **contraction**. That is, when the heart tends more toward contraction than toward dilation, according to that word of the Wise Man:

<margin>Wisdom 9:15-17</margin>
> The body which is corrupted weighs down the soul, and the earthly dwelling oppresses the mind thinking on many things; and we hardly guess at the things that are upon earth, and with labor we find the things that are before us, but who shall seek out the things that are in heaven? And who shall know your thought, except you give wisdom, and send your Holy Spirit from the highest, etc.

<summary>Fludd argues that negative emotions like fear, despair, and madness are the physical result of the heart "contracting" or closing off, which prevents the soul from seeing divine light. He supports this spiritual-physiological theory with a blend of Hermetic philosophy and numerous Biblical passages describing "hardened" or "darkened" hearts.</summary>

<summary>Fludd argues that negative emotions and spiritual blindness result from a physical "contraction" of the heart, which traps the soul in darkness and subjects it to demonic influences. He contrasts this with the "expansion" of the heart through faith, which allows for divine vision and joy.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the heart's movements, specifically focusing on how the physical contraction and expansion (systole and diastole) mirror the spiritual and cosmic relationship between the human soul, the celestial realms, and the divine.</meta>

# On the Contest, Book III.

We conclude, therefore, that because the primary passions arise from the heart’s **dilation** <note>The widening or expansion of the heart's chambers.</note>, the ethereal spirits in the heart cling to super-celestial things. Conversely, in those passions derived from the heart’s **contraction**, the spirits are made neighbors to the sublunary <note>The physical realm "below the moon," subject to change and decay.</note> things. Thus, we see the celestial nature participating at will in either the empyrean <note>The highest, most spiritual heaven.</note> nature or, sometimes, the elementary nature. If its spirit descends downward, it mingles with wicked demons; but when visiting the super-celestial kingdoms, it participates in the angelic nature. This is the same relationship that exists between the spirit of life in man and his intellectual spirit in the brain, as well as the natural spirit in the liver. For just as the spirit of life gives life invisibly, so the natural spirit in the liver causes growth and animation visibly. From this, it is denoted that it belongs to the realm of the flesh and the elementary kingdom. That action of the heart, however, which sits in the middle between these two extremes, is that which participates equally in both ends, so that it may serve only the necessity of life. In this work, there is a relationship of the "middle" to both extremes—namely, of the middle celestial nature (the ether) to the empyrean or angelic heaven, and again its respect toward the elementary nature. From the super-celestial heaven, the ethereal part draws the intellectual spirit; it holds within itself the vital soul and is carried in the sublunary air with its super-celestial soul, and finally is enclosed in the opaque body to complete the whole composition of man.

Hence, by the movement of the heart, which is the seat of the spirit—namely, in **Diastole**, the soul dilates itself—it attracts its own likeness: both the rational spirit from the empyrean heaven (which wanders everywhere in the air) and the vital spirit from its ethereal kingdom. This action is completed by the heart's dilation. By contrast, in its **Systole**, it separates the impure from the pure by contracting, incessantly drawing to itself what is needed. From this, it is clear: the action of life mimics the action of the **Soul of the World** <term>Anima Mundi: A concept in Neoplatonism describing a vital force that animates the entire universe.</term>, or the primary emanation from **Jehovah** <note>original: "Iehoua"</note>. The ancients (skilled in Theology) called this the hidden soul of the world and the **Messiah**; that is, the power of the **Tetragrammaton** <term>The four-letter Hebrew name of God, YHWH, often used in mystical calculations.</term> which the Kabbalists identified as the angel **Metraton** <note>original: "Mitratton," a high-ranking archangel in Jewish mysticism.</note>, or **Michael**, who holds command over the malignant angels who are enemies of human life. By the dilation of its principal organ [the heart], the soul therefore attracts friendship and the gifts of life and reason from heaven: by means of the motion of Diastole, just as a bellows draws air into itself to provide fuel for a fading fire, so that as the air enters, the fire may always burn in its full vigor. In this analogy, the person blowing is like the soul of life, the pumping bellows are likened to the lungs, and the air drawn into the bellows is the same in kind as that drawn into the lungs. Just as the air drawn in by a person using bellows nourishes the flame of an internal and artificial fire, so also the similar air drawn by the heart through the lungs preserves life and successively assists the fire of internal life with supplements. This is the natural flame, by whose growth and preservation man continues and progresses in his state of living. Therefore, the soul of this kind of life is the internal agent or the formal principle within us, just as its internal agent is the Mind, or the **Word** <note>original: "verbum," referring to the Logos or Divine Reason.</note>. The instrumental causes are the heart and lungs; the material cause is the "essentializing" part of the air; and the effect is life. It follows then that the involuntary movement called Diastole, through the expansion of its organ, attracts God’s benevolence (which, according to Holy Scripture, is *life and being*), and Systole, by contracting, expels the useless parts of the introduced air—not unlike how a fire’s flame chooses the pure part of the air blown by the bellows for its nourishment, but expels the impure and superfluous part in the form of useless smoke.

<margin>Proverbs 4:23.</margin>
The heart, therefore, is to be cared for above all, just as the Wise One teaches us: *Keep your heart with all vigilance, for from it life proceeds.* Hence, a great mystery in nature is explained: namely, that in this movement of life, measured by the nature of the pulse, it is necessary that there be an equality. This is no different than how we see the ethereal heaven positioned equally between the empyrean and the elementary realms, since life and the balance of the elements consist of this kind of equality.

<summary>Fludd explains how the heart's expansion (diastole) draws in divine and rational spirits while its contraction (systole) expels impurities, drawing a direct parallel between human physiology and the cosmic "Soul of the World." He argues that the heart acts as a middle ground between the spiritual and material realms, much like the ether sits between the highest heaven and the earth.</summary>

On the previous page, he discussed how the contraction of the heart leads to negative emotions like melancholy and fear; here, he concludes that the heart's movement acts as a bridge between the divine realm and the physical body, comparing the heart to a bellows that feeds the fire of life.</meta>

# On the Contest, Book III.

We conclude, therefore, that while the primary passions arising from the **dilation** <note>The expansion phase of the heartbeat, known as diastole.</note> of the heart cause the ethereal spirits in the heart to cling to super-celestial things, the latter passions—derived from the **contraction** <note>The compression phase of the heartbeat, known as systole.</note> of the heart—cause the spirits to become neighbors to sublunary <note>The earthly realm below the moon, subject to change and decay.</note> things. Thus we see that the celestial nature participates at will sometimes in the **empyrean** <note>The highest, most spiritual heaven, often associated with pure fire or light.</note> nature, and sometimes in the elementary nature. If its spirit descends downward, it mixes with wicked demons; but when visiting the super-celestial kingdoms, it participates in the angelic nature. This same relationship exists between the **spirit of life** in man, his **intellectual spirit** in the brain, and that **natural spirit** in the liver; for just as the spirit of life gives life invisibly, so that natural spirit in the liver causes visible growth. Hence it is denoted that the natural spirit belongs to the part of the flesh and the elementary kingdom. However, that action of the heart, which stands in the middle between these two extremes, is that which participates equally in both extremes, so that it may serve the necessity of life alone. In this work, there is a relationship of the middle to both extremes—namely, the nature of the middle heaven or **ether** <note>The subtle substance filling the upper regions of space.</note> to that of the empyrean or angelic heaven, and again its relationship to the elementary nature. Therefore, from the super-celestial heaven, the ethereal part draws the intellectual spirit, holds the vital soul within itself, and is carried in the sublunary air with its super-celestial soul, until finally it is enclosed in the opaque body for the complete composition of man.

It follows, then, that by the movement of the heart—which is the seat of the spirit—the soul expands itself in **Diastole** so that it may incessantly attract to itself its own likeness: both the **rational** spirit wandering everywhere in the air from the empyrean heaven, and the **vital** spirit from its ethereal kingdom (that is, from the higher realms). This action is completed by the dilation of the heart. Meanwhile, in **Systole**, the soul separates the impure from the pure by contracting. From this it is clear that the action of life imitates the act of the **World Soul** <note>original: "anima mundi"; the spiritual essence that ancient philosophers believed permeated all nature.</note>, or the primary emanation from **Jehovah**, which the ancients (skilled in Theology) called the hidden soul of the world and the **Messiah**. This is the power of the **Tetragrammaton** <note>The four-letter Hebrew name of God, YHWH.</note> which the Cabalists made the angel **Metatron** <note>original: "Mitratton"; a high-ranking angel in mystical Judaism, often viewed as the celestial scribe or mediator.</note>, or Michael, who holds dominion over the malignant angels who are enemies of human life. By the dilation of its principal organ, therefore, the soul attracts friendship and the endowments of life and reason from heaven: namely, by means of the movement of Diastole. Just as a bellows draws air into itself to provide fuel for a fading fire, so that as the air follows, the fire may always burn in its full vigor. In this analogy, the man blowing is like the soul of life, the agitated bellows are likened to the lungs, and the air drawn into the bellows is the same in kind as that drawn into the lungs. Therefore, just as the air drawn by a man through the bellows nourishes the flame of an internal and artificial fire, so also the similar air drawn by the heart through the lungs preserves life and successively aids the fire of internal life with supplements. This is the **natural flame**, by whose growth and preservation man proceeds and progresses in his state of living. Thus, the soul is the internal agent or the **formal principle** <note>The essential nature or "blueprint" of a thing.</note> of this kind of life movement regarding us, just as its internal agent is the mind, or the **Word**. The instrumental causes are the heart and lung; the material cause is the essential part of the air; and the effect is life. From this it follows that the involuntary movement called Diastole, through the dilation of its organ, attracts the benevolence of God (which, according to Holy Scripture, is *life and being*), and Systole, by contracting, expels the useless parts of the introduced air, not otherwise than a flame of fire chooses the pure part of that air blown to it by the bellows for its nourishment, but expels the impure and superfluous part in the form of useless smoke.

<margin>Proverbs 4:23.</margin>
The heart, therefore, is to be cared for above all things, just as the Wise Man <note>Referring to King Solomon, the traditional author of Proverbs.</note> teaches us: *With all watchfulness keep thy heart, because life proceeds from it.* From this is explained a great mystery in nature: namely, that in this kind of life-movement, measured by the nature of the pulse, it is necessary that an equality be made. This is no different than how we see the ethereal heaven situated equally between the empyrean and the elementary realms, since life and the temperament of the elements consist of this kind of equality.

# On the Conflict of Wisdom with Folly

From these things, then, it may be seen that the angel—the presiding governor of life, or the one who establishes its action—operates beyond his duties in the heart's **contraction** and **dilation** alone. He leads toward life, whether concerning the **rational faculty** (over which the mental angel presides) or that **natural faculty** which is subject to the kingdom of darkness.

<margin>Text 2.</margin> But it is still necessary that I respond to one or another of Mersenne’s <note>Marin Mersenne (1588–1648) was a French priest and mathematician who was a fierce critic of Robert Fludd’s Hermetic and alchemical theories.</note> objections before I put an end to this fourth question. The first of these <margin>Text 2.</margin> was made in these words: *“Why does he not admit as many demons in one man as there are in the whole world?”* I respond: because man was made from a small portion of the world, and not from the whole. Therefore, if this so-called "profound philosopher" had been consistent with himself, he would have noticed the answer he provided for himself in his own logic: since (as he himself admits) man has drawn some portion from all parts of the world, etc. Therefore, if he is a portion, he is not the whole, and consequently, a man cannot contain all the demons that exist in the entire world.

<margin>Text 2.</margin> Regarding the consequence of this *text*, I speak in opposition to the speech of that profane Marin: *“Could he have deserved greater praise from God than that, through his officiating Angels, I should report his word in all his creatures (according to the sentiment of the Royal Psalmist <note>King David</note>), in the ethereal, fiery, vital part, as well as the native heat, blood, lungs, and all humors both in man and in other creatures, indeed in the whole world, since all these things were made by the word of God, as St. John testifies?”* But as David testifies in the place cited before, it belongs to the mighty Angels of God to carry out the Word. For as soon as the Word is spoken by God, these angels are like clean mirrors that receive the verbal emanation, which God immediately sends forth to fulfill his will, both in the heavens and on the earth. And these things are true and inexpressible, despite the foolish and insane conspiracies of that **pseudo-monk** <note>Fludd is insulting Mersenne, who was a member of the Order of Minims.</note>, whose imagination is miserably provoked and agitated by ghosts, spirits, Harpies, Fates, Gorgons, Furies, Witches, Centaurs, Dog-milkers, Satyrs, Fauns, Nymphs, and all such things (*as is apparent from his envy, malice, and restlessness*).

Then he proceeds in his insane manner: *“O wonderful blindness of the man who, in Europe, among Christians, has dared not only to treat but impudently to bring to light such foul and horrendous Magic with impunity.”* Thus he spoke, to whom I reply: O wonderful blindness of the religious man who, living his life in Europe and professing to be a Monk among Christians, has dared with a bold pen to affirm—and impudently bring to light—his own ill-formed opinion! He shows foul and horrendous ignorance in both **natural and divine magic**, and a lack of skill in the doctrine of sacred scripture, by denying that a specific Angel is assigned by God to every man. Finally, he dares to claim that the Word of God, through which all things were made, was not put into effect through the mediation of angels.

We now finally come to his fifth and sixth reasons, by which he thinks Robert <note>Fludd refers to himself in the third person here.</note> should be considered a **magus** <note>A magician or practitioner of occult arts; Mersenne uses the term as an accusation of heresy.</note>. You will perceive, upon consideration, that these arguments are of less value than his previous ones and (in my opinion) will be held as worthless by learned men. First, with a single glance at what has been said, you can gather that the Patriarch Jacob had his own angelic power, whence <margin>Zechariah 2</margin> he says: *“May the Angel who rescues me from all evil bless these boys.”* Similarly, that Zechariah was a tabernacle for an angel, or rather, for angels. Furthermore, that St. Peter also possessed his own angel.

Finally, we gather from the testimony of sacred scripture that all men coming into this world have their own particular angels: <margin>Acts 12, 13</margin> *“See”* (says Christ) *“that you do not despise one of these little ones, for I say to you, that their Angels in heaven always see the face of my father, who is in heaven.”* And in <margin>Matthew 18</margin> <margin>Baruch 6:6</margin> we read in the Prophet: *“My Angel is with you, the avenger of your souls.”* From this, it is apparent that every one of the Patriarchs, Prophets, and Apostles had communication with an Angel granted to them by God from birth, but...

### On the Conflict, Book III

...not at all manifest or revealed, except by pure grace through regeneration, and when the cup of salvation has been drunk by them and their bodies thereby freed from darkness. Hence we read of the Angel of Abraham, Lot, the handmaid Hagar, Isaac, Joseph, Moses, Joshua, Gideon, Manoah <note>original: "Manuelis"</note>, Samuel, David, Solomon, the Prophet Gad, Ornan, Jeshua, Daniel, Hezekiah, Ezra, Zechariah, the Prophet Azariah with his companions, Tobit, Mary, Joseph, Zacharias, Philip, Cornelius, Peter, Paul, John, Elijah, Elisha, Isaiah, Jeremiah, and finally, of all holy men to whom it was granted to know themselves rightly—of those, I say, whose lot it was to have "a healthy mind in a healthy body" <note>original: *mentem sanam in corpore sano*</note>. All of these are nonetheless called the Angels of the Lord, for they "see the face of God and stand before him."

<margin>Plutarch, *Moralia*</margin> Have we not also heard from trustworthy writers that Socrates was directed in good ways by his own **good Daemon** <note>In the ancient sense, a *daemon* was a guiding spirit or inner voice, not an evil "demon."</note>, and was fortified against the perversity of his nature and the stigmata of fate? By the mediating power of this spirit, it is read that he was so transfigured that (according to the Poet) he surpassed the light-bringing wheels of the sun with his own light. Indeed, <margin>Pimander, 10</margin> **Mercurius Trismegistus** <note>The legendary Hellenistic figure Hermes Trismegistus, supposed author of the Hermetic Corpus.</note> confessed that he was led into the way of truth by his own good Daemon; and even **Plato** himself seems to acknowledge that he was taught by his own Daemon.

But as for what pertains to the fifth point, in which the greater strength of his argument seems to lie: he boldly asserts that I am a **magician** because I published the names of angels—names, he says, that are "fictitious and meaningless," and which are not found in the scriptures; therefore, "Robert Fludd is a magician." Thus he speaks.

Certainly, I believe the man is blind in both mind and eyes, for if he were endowed with the common light of Nature, he would indeed find many of those names of angels cited in the sacred writings, and he would have also received the specific meanings of those names from me: for in Chapter 2, Part 3, Book 3 of my <term>Genethlialogia</term> <note>Fludd’s work on the science of calculating nativities or astrological charts.</note>, these things were expressed by me.

The **Magi**, **Cabalists**, and ancient **Sages** attributed to each of the Patriarchs and Prophets their own proper tutelary spirit. They wrote that **Adam** had a familiar society with the **Angel Raziel**, whose name in the Hebrew language is seen to denote or signify "the secret" or "mystery," and likewise "the will of God." This truly had the best relationship to Adam, either insofar as he was the first pure vessel and a kind of sanctuary or perfume-box <note>original: *myrothecium*</note> in which God had placed the mystery of humanity (which is His Image), or insofar as through this angel He first made the revelation of His Divine Majesty. **Seth**, the son of Noah, had **Jophiel** for his Angel, whose name signifies "the beauty of God." To **Abraham** they attribute **Zadkiel** or **Zabdiel**, that is, "the gift" or "the given of God," who is the Archangel having dominion over the star of **Jupiter** <note>original: *stellam Iouialem*</note>; others call him **Zachariel**, that is, "the memory of God." And certainly, Zabdiel not without reason implies "the gift of God," and is said to preside over Jupiter itself (that is, the supreme God of the Heathens), since where the gift of God is, there also is virtue and prosperity. Hence Jehovah Himself, and this His Angel, appeared so often to Abraham, rendered him so wealthy, blessed him with such prosperity, and granted him such a complete age—namely, one hundred and seventy-five years—so that it is found written of him that he died "failing in a good old age and full of days." This means he completed the root of the "squared seven" <note>7 x 25 = 175; Fludd often uses numerology to explain biblical lifespans.</note> in the number of his age, of which we spoke above regarding divine numbers. From this it also follows—namely, by the benefit and dominion of this angel—that Jupiter is such a favorable star for human life, which is why the Astrologers called it the "Greater Fortune." Furthermore, they assigned the angel **Peliel** to **Jacob**, as if one were to say *Pelielum*, that is, "the hidden one of the Lord" and "the miracle of the Lord." For by the power of this Angel, Jacob perceived in a vision the angels ascending and descending by the ladder, and he possessed the Divine mystery, as appears in Genesis 28.

<margin>Genesis 28:16-18</margin>
> And when he had awaked out of sleep, he said: "Truly the Lord is in this place, and I knew it not." And trembling he said: "How terrible is this place! This is no other but the house of God, and the gate of heaven." And Jacob, arising in the morning, took the stone which he had laid under his head, and set it up for a title, pouring oil upon the top of it, etc.

And so they dedicated **Gabriel**, the Lord of the Moon, to **Joseph**, **Joshua**, and **Daniel**; the secret of this name is "the strength of God," "the man of God," and "my strong God." **Moses** claimed for himself the **Angel Metatron**, as if one were to say "the gift of God," by whose power so many wonders in...

# Of the Conflict between Wisdom and Folly <note>original: "De Sophiæ cum Moria". This is the short title of Fludd’s work *Sophia cum Moria Certamen*, which translates to "The Contest of Wisdom with Folly," directed against Marin Mersenne.</note>

...Egypt, and elsewhere. **Elijah** had **Melticlem** <note>original: "Melticlem", a name derived from Hebrew roots meaning "liberation of God"</note> serving him; over **Tobit** presided **Raphael**, which means "the medicine of God." Raphael is the primary leader of the star of Mercury or, as others wish, the Sun. To **David** they give **Ceruviel**, to **Ezekiel** **Hazmael**, to **Ezra** **Uriel**, which signifies "the light" or "the fire of God." Likewise, they assign **Michael** to **Solomon**, denoting by the explanation of his name, "Who is like God?" and also "the striking of God." This Angel is indeed the presiding officer and governor of the solar body. Hence came Solomon’s glory, hence his dignity, wisdom, and dominion; so much so that it was common on earth to exclaim: "Who is like Solomon?" Indeed, he was like the "striking of God," that is, a source of fear and dread among all nations. This is no wonder, since the Archangel Michael is the Leader of Jehovah, by whose guidance and power Satan was conquered with his angels and cast into the abyss—and so on for the others. I have written these things in the place mentioned before.

Besides these things, it must be known that the names of angels are by no means given to them according to their essence—for such a mystery is hidden from men and reserved for God alone who imparted it to them—but nevertheless, according to human capacity, names are assigned to them in many ways, speaking in the human manner and language: either according to the property of the virtue they exercise toward men, or from the human passions around which they are most often read to be occupied in the Holy Scriptures, or from the man whom they were accustomed to serve—and this without any sin or crime. From virtue, I say: thus **Michael**, "Who is like God," arguing for his invincible triumph in overcoming the Devil. Similarly, he signifies "striking," as one who strikes with terror everyone against whom he directs his battle line. **Uriel** from his fiery and bright disposition, for that name denotes "the light or fire of God." **Raphael**, "the medicine of God." **Gabriel**, "the strength of God." And so it is with the others named above, as we gather from the etymology of the Hebrew vocabulary. Similarly, names come from a passion—not the Angel's (for he suffers not at all) but the man's whom he assists. Hence Raphael was called "the medicine of God" from his office, because of the disease he cured. Michael from his victory and invincible strength against Lucifer and his angels, etc.

<margin>
Augustine, Book 3, 
Chapter 5, *The City 
of God*.
</margin>
Do we not also have this confirmed by **Augustine**, who seems to speak in this sense? Even if angels are free from passions, men are nonetheless accustomed to impose names on them from the passions of the men among whom they move, according to the custom of human speech—namely, by reason of some similarity or the subject they are working upon. There are also others who, as the **Hebrew Doctors** testify (however much they are condemned by **Marin** <note>referring to Marin Mersenne</note>), take their names from the men they serve. Thus we read in **Ezra** the name of the Archangel **Uriel** from the Prophet **Uriah** whom Jehoiakim killed, and **Jeremiel** from the Prophet **Jeremiah**, **Zachariel** from the Prophet **Zechariah**, etc.

But truly, by Mersenne’s leave, I cannot in this place omit his false accusations against **Georgius Venetus** <note>Francesco Giorgio (1460–1541), a Venetian friar and influential Christian Kabbalist</note> and **Cornelius Agrippa** <note>Heinrich Cornelius Agrippa (1486–1535), a famous scholar of occult philosophy</note>.

### Text of Marin [Mersenne], 39.

*It is a wonder how easily Venetus allows himself to slide into the opinions of the Platonists, Rabbis, and Mages; we have hardly discussed any problem that does not reek of their errors.*

### Robert’s Examination.

It is a wonder how Marin allows himself to slide into such absurdity, as to tear away the "poison" from the opinions of the Platonists, Rabbis, and Mages even if they are concerned with the truth—much less to "correct" Venetus’s problems, which are true and blameless. Therefore, he is rather to be condemned who strives to stain the truth with falsehood. But the outcome will prove the deeds.

### Text of Mersenne, Ibid.

*And who (I ask) taught him that **Raziel** was set over Adam? Did he receive that from the **Kabbalists** <note>original: "Mecubalibus", referring to the receivers of the Kabbalah</note> or the Mages? Indeed, the masters of the Hebrews seem to judge that Adam was more learned than the Angels, since they assert that names were imposed on the Angels by him—as Agrippa witnesses in Book 3, Chapter 24. Agrippa, having said correctly at the beginning that we know no names of Angels except by divine revelation, then forgot himself; being an "Arch-mage" most addicted to magic, he [wanders into]...*

<meta>This page continues Robert Fludd’s direct rebuttal to Marin Mersenne. Fludd is defending the use of specific angelic names—specifically Raziel—by appealing to the authority of Divine Wisdom and ancient tradition rather than just the technical methods of later Kabbalists.</meta>

# On the Contest, Book III.

...imagines the names of angels, among which he places **Raziel**, who rouses horrible thunders.

### Robert’s Examination.

Is this your formula for refuting and correcting? Do you hope to teach so great a man as the Venetian <note>original: "Venetus", referring to the Franciscan friar and Christian Kabbalist Francesco Giorgio (1466–1540), whom Mersenne had attacked</note> and dismiss his meaning with such ease using only futile questions? So be it. Ask, and I will answer with a word: You ask, "Who taught him that Raziel was set over Adam?" I respond that the tradition of the Hebrew teachers and the Cabalistic <term>Cabalistica: referring to Kabbalah, the Jewish mystical tradition concerning the nature of God and the universe</term> documents left this to posterity through the succession of ages. What then do you have to say to the contrary? Then you proceed by asking: *Whether from the Mecubales, or from the Magi.* <note>Fludd uses "Mecubales" as a Latinized form of the Hebrew "Mekubbalim," meaning practitioners of Kabbalah</note> I answer that the rules of the Mecubales for extracting the sacred names of Angels from the holy letters are not sufficiently certain; indeed, they are inconsistent. For which reason, we will scarcely—or rather not at all—place any faith in them. Therefore, for the resolution of such a question, we shall take refuge in the doctrine of the Magi and the Sages, such as that of Solomon, Moses, and others of this kind. For you ought to know further (Mersenne, you who boast that you know everything) that Adam had a stronger and more powerful guide than the Mecubales to reveal all these things to him, according to that of the Prophet:

<margin>Daniel 2:27</margin>
> *The mystery about which Marinus <note>Mersenne’s name in Latin</note> asks—the Sages, the Magi, the Soothsayers, the Diviners, and even the Mecubaei cannot judge. But there is a God in heaven revealing angelic mysteries:*

Divine Wisdom was therefore the Teacher to Adam, by whose mediation he gave sacred names not only to earthly creatures, but also to those celestial, and indeed even those of the empyrean <note>the highest part of heaven, thought to be composed of pure light or fire</note>. This we also gather from these words of Solomon:

<margin>Wisdom 10:1</margin>
> *Wisdom (he says) guarded him who was first formed by God as the father of the world <note>Adam</note>, when he was created alone, and brought him out of his sin, and led him out of the mud of the earth, and gave him the power of containing all things, etc.*

From which, if he contained *all* things, would he not (O Marinus) have been able to give names to the Angels according to their properties? Indeed, do you believe it is impossible that, through this same wisdom, other descendants of Adam could elicit the names of angels, since you have received these words from Solomon himself:

<margin>Wisdom 7</margin>
> *Wisdom taught me all things.*

And elsewhere:

> *by her mediation, I had knowledge of how the world was made, and the powers of the elements, and the positions of the stars, and the natures of living creatures, and the rage of wild beasts, the force of winds, and the differences of plants, and the virtues of roots, etc.*

Could he not then, just as well as Adam, provide names according to properties for animals, wild beasts, and plants? For they all had no one but the one and same teacher, Wisdom—that is, the Holy Spirit. But Marinus will answer that this is true in lower things, but not in things either celestial or super-celestial. Have you not understood the Royal Psalmist speaking thus:

<margin>Psalm 146:4</margin>
> *He who counts the multitude of the stars and calls them by their names, etc.*

Which he doubtlessly would not have said unless, taught by wisdom, he had understood both the number and the names of the stars. For the Holy Bible testifies that David possessed wisdom and the divine spirit. But you will still replicate that I have said nothing, or can say nothing, regarding the naming of angels: to which I say, pay attention to the logic. Consider the words of the Sage concluding the preceding discourse:

> *Wisdom (he says) gave me knowledge of the thoughts of men, and by her mediation I understood whatever things there are, whether hidden or manifest, etc.*

If therefore *whatever* things, then it must be taken as admitted that the mysteries of the angels themselves, and consequently their names, are to be understood. But perhaps, still excusing yourself further, you will say: "In this place it is understood to mean all those things which are hidden on earth, and it is impossible that we should understand those things which are in the heavens." I would wish you, meanwhile, to recall these words of the Sage for the refutation of such a concept:

<margin>Wisdom 9:16</margin>
> *Who will investigate the things that are in the heavens? And who shall know your counsel, unless you have given wisdom and sent the Holy Spirit from the highest heavens, etc.*

By which he also seems to prove that the possessor of Divine Wisdom himself, by the instructions of that same spirit, can comprehend the Mystery of God as much in the heavens as on the earth.

He uses several scriptural passages to prove that Wisdom reveals both earthly and celestial secrets to the faithful.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Robert Fludd’s defense of the Christian Kabbalist Francesco Giorgio (the Venetian) against the attacks of Marin Mersenne. Fludd is arguing that the knowledge of angelic names—such as Raziel—is a gift of Divine Wisdom rather than illicit magic.</meta>

# Of Wisdom with Folly

<margin>Text.</margin>
From this, therefore, it is very easily answered to that hollow doubt you proposed: that the Hebrew teachers seem to suggest Adam was more knowledgeable than the angels because he was the one who gave names to the creatures, etc. No, Marin, they do not conclude from this that Adam was more learned than the angels themselves. Rather, they suggest that in doctrine and science he was by no means inferior to them, seeing as both were instructed by the same Teacher. Thus, Adam understood the nature and name of the Angel no less than the Angel understood those of Adam.

<margin>Psalm 8:5.
Revelation 22.</margin>
Indeed, as David testifies, *God made man a little lower than the Angels, inasmuch as he crowned him with glory and honor.* <note>original Latin: "fecit Deus hominem Angelis paulo minorem, vtpote quem gloria & decore coronauit"</note> Hence the Angel whom John sought to adore speaks to him thus: *See thou do it not: for I am thy fellow-servant, and of thy brethren the prophets.* <note>original Latin: "Ne ita feceris, conseruus enim tuus sum, & fratrum tuorum prophetarum"</note> Truly, in this regard, man is even to be considered above the condition of Angels: that he himself is transmuted into the nature of God, according to that saying, *he who adheres to God, will become one spirit with him.* <note>A reference to 1 Corinthians 6:17</note> And elsewhere: *at last we shall be transformed into that same image.* <note>A reference to 2 Corinthians 3:18</note> We read that this was by no means said of the angelic nature in the sacred scriptures. From this we conclude that it would not be a great error if we said that the gifts of the Spirit of Wisdom were more eminent in Adam than in the lower orders of angels, who are called "ministering" by the Apostle; for to Adam in his state of innocence it was given to have dominion over all creatures, as the Sage testifies:

<margin>Wisdom 7.</margin>
*God,* he says, *made man by his wisdom, that he should have dominion over the creatures which were made by him.* <note>original Latin: "Hominem; inquit, fecit Deus sapientia sua, vt dominetur creaturis, quæ ab eo factæ sunt." From the Book of Wisdom 9:1-2.</note> From these things it is clear that not only to Adam, but indeed to Solomon—and even to any other man to whom God has given wisdom—it is granted by God to know and understand the secret and hidden names of the Angels. This is proven by experience: God has granted this wisdom, or the one and only spirit of holy discipline, to many good men of this age.

<margin>Psalm 119:66.
Exodus 28.
Exodus 31.
Wisdom 8:4.</margin>
*Teach me goodness, discipline, and knowledge,* (says David) *for I have believed your commandments.* <note>original Latin: "Bonitatem disciplinam & scientiam doce me... quia mandatis tuis credidi."</note> And Jehovah, speaking to Moses, says: *You shall speak to all the wise of heart, whom I have filled with the spirit of wisdom, that they may do this.* <note>original Latin: "Loqueris cunctis sapientibus corde quos repleui spiritu sapientiæ, vt faciant hoc."</note> And elsewhere: *Behold, I have filled Bezalel with the spirit of God, in wisdom, understanding, and knowledge in every work.* <note>Bezalel was the artisan of the Tabernacle</note> And elsewhere: *Wisdom is the teacher of the discipline of God, and the chooser of his works.* From these passages, one may see that those who assign proper names to angels according to the instinct of divine Wisdom and the doctrine revealed to our fathers are not to be held as impious Magi or Arch-Magi. I speak of a doctrine arising not from the help of <term>Cacōmagiæ</term> <note>Evil magic, from the Greek 'kakos' (bad)</note> or the <term>Mecubeorum</term> <note>Practitioners of Kabbalah (Mekubbalim)</note>, but by the revelation of divine Wisdom. This Wisdom is as frequent and familiar with the human spirit in our own age as it was in past centuries, although it cannot be discerned because of the obstacles of the dark body and flesh; and because in past times there was one age of Gold, another of Silver, and another of Iron, of which the Poets spoke in a mystical manner.

<margin>Proverbs 8:23.</margin>
Hence Wisdom herself says: *My delights* or *my pleasures are with the sons of men.* <note>original Latin: "Deliciæ meæ vel delectationes meæ sunt cum filiis hominum."</note> But now, I seem to hear Marin snarling and replying in this way: "What has this to do with the Angel Raziel? We all acknowledge that revelations come from God. But what have we to do with the angel Raziel whom the Venetian proposes?" To which I answer most fully in this manner: Although there is but one unique GOD in essence, and for this reason only one essential name is revealed, namely, **Jehovah**; nevertheless, with respect to the variety of His properties, that unique essence is distinguished into three persons and is called by many names. Ten of these are numbered by the <term>Cabalists</term>, as they observe just as many in the sacred scriptures. They observe that these various names were uttered in the holy pages according to various properties distinct from one another: namely, **Ehieh** <note>Hebrew: "I am"</note> signifying paternal goodness; **Jah** signifying wisdom; **Elohim** signifying severity and punishment; **Eloah** signifying grace; **Shaddai** signifying redemption; **Adonai** signifying kingdom and dominion, etc.

By the same reasoning, although the second person or holy <term>emanation</term> <note>The process by which all things flow from the Divine source</note> is of one name in the divine essence (whence he is called **Jehovah**), yet he assumes as many names from his property as he has various actions in this worldly fabric, by means of which he operates all variable things in this world. Hence it is said by Solomon: *This emanation of eternal light is a spirit simple, unique, and yet mul-*

<margin>Wisdom 7.</margin>

<summary>Fludd argues that humans can know the secret names of angels through Divine Wisdom, which bridges the gap between the human and the divine. He explains that while God is one, His many names (like Elohim and Shaddai) represent different divine attributes used to govern the world.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Robert Fludd’s discourse on the nature of divine knowledge and the hierarchy of spiritual beings, specifically transitioning from the gifts given to Adam to the nature of the "Universal Angel" who governs the world.</meta>

# On the Contest, Book III.

manifold. He is simple and unique in his divine identity, yet manifold in respect to the variety of his properties and actions within this universe. Therefore, insofar as he is considered in his universal unity and simplicity, he is rightly called **GOD**, **Jehovah**, and the uncreated spirit; but in respect to the office and property for which it was made—that is, such a divine emanation and emission of splendor—he is said to be "Manifold" in his universal multiplicity. This is because he mixes the rays of his instruction with the spirit of the entire world.
<margin>Ecclesiastes 1</margin>
And from this universal property of his, he is called a "creature" by the Wise Man in many places, and in Sacred Scripture he is sometimes called an "angel," and occasionally referred to by the name of **Jehovah**. In this universal nature of his multiplicity, he is called *Mitattron* <note>Metatron, a high-ranking angel in Jewish celestial hierarchy, often viewed as the mediator between God and the world.</note> by the Hebrew Doctors, or the Angel of the universal world; and Rabbi Moses <note>Moses Maimonides, the preeminent medieval Jewish philosopher.</note>, along with Reuchlin <note>Johannes Reuchlin, a German humanist and pioneer of Christian Kabbalah.</note>, rightly calls him the prefect of the universe, by whom all virtues are governed. He is also called the **agent intellect**, from whom all forms flow, and they say that from this very universal source, all angels gush forth. This indeed seems to agree perfectly with the opinions of Orpheus and Heraclitus, who, when they said "all things are full of gods"—as if they had said with the Kabbalists that all things are full of forms or angels—nevertheless referred all those gods back to the one Jove.

In this same way, all angels in the world are referred back to this one universal Being (whom we call **Wisdom** along with the Holy Page), for all angels find their end in Christ, their origin, who contains and embraces the nature of both angels and God, in whom all things terminate, according to that saying of the Apostle: *from him, in him, and through him are all things.* <note>original Latin: "ab eo, in eo, & per eum sunt omnia," referencing Romans 11:36.</note> Did the Apostle not also fall upon these words: *If there be those who are called gods in heaven and on earth, yet to us there is but one GOD the Father, from whom are all things, and we in him; and one Lord Jesus Christ, through whom are all things, and we through him: This is the Word, from which, and through which, all things exist*? <note>original Latin: "Si sunt qui dicuntur in cælo & in terra, nobis tamen vnus DEVS pater, ex quo omnia, & nos in illum, & vnus Dominus Ieſus Chriſtus, per quem omnia, & nos per ipſum : Hoc est verbum, à quo, & per quod omnia," referencing 1 Corinthians 8:5-6.</note> But this Word is carried out by the angels of God, most mighty in strength, whose task it is to do God’s pleasure, as the royal Psalmist testifies. 
<margin>Psalm 104</margin>
For angels are nothing other than the purest spirits receiving into themselves the rays of divine emanation. Wherefore Dionysius <note>Pseudo-Dionysius the Areopagite, an influential early Christian theologian and Neoplatonist.</note> defines an 
<margin>Book on Divine Names, ch. 6.</margin>
Angel (as has been said) as *the Image of God, and the manifestation of his hidden light, a pure mirror, most bright and without stain.* <note>original Latin: "Imago Dei & lucis eiuſdem abſconditæ manifestatio & ſpeculum purum & lucidiſſimum ſine macula"</note> For the first emanation sent forth from the divine majesty reaches the angelic substance immediately; hence they are called *Agalmata* <note>A Greek term for "images" or "statues," used by Dionysius to describe angels as divine reflections.</note> by Dionysius, as if they were the brightest mirrors, since they receive the eternal light into themselves.

<margin>Wisdom 7</margin>
Do we not also read: *Wisdom is a certain pure emanation of the glory of GOD, the brightness or splendor of eternal light, a spotless mirror of the majesty of God, and the image of his goodness*? <note>original Latin: "Sapientiam eſſe emanationem quandam claritatis DEI ſynceram, & candorem ſeu ſplendorem lucis æternæ, & ſpeculum ſine macula maieſtatis Dei, & imagine bonitatis illius?" referencing Wisdom of Solomon 7:25-26.</note> From these things it is clear that there is (in effect) no difference between the angelic nature and that emanation in the world; for that emanation, or angelic emission, is the form informing the material nature of the angel, which is the purest and most select spirit of the world. And hence it is called by the Platonists the *Soul of the World*, and by the Kabbalists the *Great and Universal Angel of the World, Mitattron*, etc., as has been more clearly demonstrated in our discourse upon the soul of the world. And *Mitattron* among the Hebrews denotes the "gift of God," as it is the being upon which all other formal gifts in the world depend, and from which all other particular Angels have their naming and their being. For *God spoke and they were made; he sent his spirit and they were created.* <note>original Latin: "dixit Deus & facta sunt, misit spiritum & creata sunt," referencing Psalm 33:9 and 104:30.</note> And by this "spirit," the wise Hebrews intend the universal Angel Metatron, or Wisdom. For just as God brought forth the first Word as a spirit for creation, more swiftly than the imagination, so this luminous and angelic being was sent forth to carry out the Word, and to do the pleasure of him 
<margin>Psalm 104</margin>
who sent him, according to the aforementioned intention of the royal Psalmist. Wherefore it is affirmed elsewhere that Wisdom is the source of the Word of God in the highest: for when the Word is made, Wisdom is sent forth.

<summary>This page defines the nature of God's emanation into the world through the figure of Metatron, whom Fludd identifies with the "Soul of the World," the "Agent Intellect," and Divine Wisdom. He argues that angels are pure mirrors of this divine light and that all creation flows from this single, manifold source.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the nature of the "Universal Angel" or Divine Wisdom, moving from a general description of this spirit to how it manifests as specific angelic beings (Michael, Raphael, Uriel, and Raziel) and how it relates to the knowledge given to Adam.</meta>

# On Wisdom and Folly

<margin>Ecclesiasticus 1</margin>
With these things understood, we see that Wisdom herself is the first messenger, and as it were the fountain and origin of the angels; although in herself she is a simple and singular spirit, yet this spirit renders itself manifold to fulfill the will of God. To accomplish the various mandates of God, she assumes different names according to the variety of the office for which she is sent. Therefore, just as in her general nature she is said to adorn, inform, and enliven the entire world with her presence, so she extends herself into branches and particular portions to perfect any particular thing according to its angelic nature. Do we not read this written concerning Wisdom: *I came out of the mouth of the most High, and as a cloud I covered the earth; my throne is in a pillar of a cloud,* etc. <note>original: "Ex ore altissimi prodij & tanquam nebula cooperui terram, thronus meus in columna nubis." This is from Ecclesiasticus (Sirach) 24:3-4.</note> And elsewhere: *Wisdom was a covering for them by day and the light of stars by night,* <note>original: "Fuit sapientia iis tegumentum interdiu & lux stellaris nocte." From Wisdom of Solomon 10:17.</note> and yet you will perceive with a slightly more accurate gaze that all these things were effected under the name of the <term>Angel of Jehovah</term> <note>Hebrew: Malakh Yehovah; often interpreted in mystical traditions as the visible presence of God's wisdom.</note>, as is right to see everywhere in Exodus and the following books of Moses.

<margin>Wisdom 10, 11</margin>
Indeed, the Sage <note>Referring to the author of the Book of Wisdom, traditionally Solomon.</note> narrates what wonders this general Angel performed in his manifold property from the beginning of the world until the leading of the Israelites out of Egypt. And yet we read that various angels appeared while these things were done. But you see that this Wisdom was held to be that angel who was the leader of the Israelites through the desert. Therefore, as was said before, God is said to work all things in all, and yet to bring forth various and distinct things according to his manifold property. Hence, according to the variety of properties, he has obtained various names (as has been said). So indeed this highest angel—in imitation of Him whose image he is—insofar as he is unique and universal in and through himself, is called <term>Mitattron</term> <note>Metatron, a supreme angel in Kabbalistic tradition, sometimes called the "Prince of the Countenance" or the "lesser YHWH."</note>. Thus, in respect to his manifold nature, he puts on various names according to the condition of the divine property. Just as God is sometimes called <term>Elieh</term> <note>Ehyeh, meaning "I Am."</note>, sometimes <term>Iah</term> <note>Yah, a shortened form of the Tetragrammaton.</note>, and occasionally <term>Elohim</term>, etc., so also this general angel, when sent out, sometimes claims for himself the office and name of <term>Michael</term> <note>Literally "Who is like God?" In this context, associated with God's victory and strong hand.</note>, as if one were to say "God obtaining victory with a strong hand." Sometimes he has the name of <term>Raphael</term> <note>"God heals."</note>, namely when he wishes to exercise holy medicine; sometimes he is called <term>Vriel</term> <note>Uriel, "Light of God" or "Fire of God."</note> when he desires to manifest his fiery virtue; and sometimes <term>Raziel</term> <note>"Secret of God."</note>, of whom we treat here, when he intends to explain the mystery and secret of God to man. In this sense it is said of this angelic nature that it gave to Adam the power of containing all things, or as it is held *elsewhere*:

<margin>
Wisdom 10:3
Genesis 2
Wisdom 7
</margin>
Delivering him from his sin and giving him the power of ruling over all things, and of knowing their properties, and of imposing names upon both plants and animals. From these things it is evident that the multitude of angelic names comes from the variety of the property of that general angel who is unique. But truly their simplicity consists in his unique nature and essence. Therefore, he who is a possessor of divine Wisdom can by no means be ignorant of the names of the angels. Nor should he think it impossible for these to be revealed by Wisdom herself, putting on the property signified by the name *Raziel*. For from her, in her, and through her are all things; she is the father of all angels, who is above all angels, and through all, and in all, and consequently nothing in the angelic mysteries can be hidden from her.

Finally (says Mersenne) <note>Marin Mersenne (1588–1648), a French polymath and critic of Fludd’s mystical philosophies.</note>, if the angel Raziel is Wisdom changed into 그at angelic property, why is it that Raziel here is the stirrer of such horrible thunders? I answer that just as the name Raziel signifies the secret and mystery of God, so indeed do the thunders of God—which are called "exerted voices"—denote and signify the very great mysteries of God. But he will say: "Why then is he changed from Wisdom into the nature of Raziel?" To whom I answer with

<margin>
Job 28:25
Psalm 103:24
</margin>
Job: that *God by his wisdom, sets a weight for the winds, and weighs the waters by measure, and makes a decree for the rain, and a way for the lightning of the thunders.* <note>Job 28:25-26.</note> And elsewhere he says: *that the works of God are wonderful, and in wisdom you have made them all.* <note>Psalm 104:24 in modern bibles; 103:24 in the Vulgate.</note> Therefore, the name Raziel is not essential, but comes from property alone, as its etymology implies.

<summary>This page argues that all specific angels, such as Michael, Raphael, and Raziel, are actually different functional manifestations (properties) of a single "Universal Angel" or Divine Wisdom. Fludd defends the mystical naming of these beings against the criticisms of Marin Mersenne, asserting that Wisdom encompasses all these powers.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the defense of mystical philosophy, specifically responding to the attacks made by Marin Mersenne against Robert Fludd and other Hermetic thinkers.
